
\medterm{MacConkey Agar} A selective and differential culture medium used mainly for gram-negative bacteria. Bile salts and
crystal violet inhibit many gram-positive organisms, while lactose fermentation produces
different colony colors and helps distinguish bacterial groups.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macrophage} Macrophage concerns macrophage, a body structure, substance, or clinical concept whose meaning depends on location, function, and patient context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magenblase Syndrome} Stomach discomfort and bloating caused by swallowing excessive air while eating.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Aerophagia; stomach bubble syndrome

\medterm{Major Depression} A depressive disorder causing persistent low mood or loss of interest with significant functional impairment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Major depressive disorder

\medterm{Major Histocompatibility Complex Molecule} A cell-surface molecule that presents protein fragments to immune cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
MHC molecule

\medterm{Maladaptive Stress Response} A persistent or disproportionate stress reaction that harms health or functioning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant} Cancerous and capable of invading nearby tissue or spreading to distant sites.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mastopexy} Surgery that lifts and reshapes sagging breasts.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Breast lift

\medterm{Meglitinides} Short-acting medicines that stimulate pancreatic insulin release to lower blood glucose.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melanin} Melanin concerns melanin, a body structure, substance, or clinical concept whose meaning depends on location, function, and patient context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melatonin} A hormone produced mainly by the pineal gland that helps regulate sleep-wake timing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Membrane} A thin tissue layer that surrounds a cell, organ, or body cavity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Memory T Cells and B Cells} Long-lived immune cells that respond rapidly when they encounter a previously recognized antigen.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Memory lymphocytes

\medterm{Ménière’s Disease} An inner-ear disorder associated with episodic vertigo, fluctuating hearing loss, tinnitus, and ear pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Meniere disease

\medterm{Metabolic Equivalents} Units estimating the energy cost of physical activity relative to resting metabolism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
METs

\medterm{Methanobrevibacter Smithii} A methane-producing microorganism commonly found in the human intestine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{METs} Metabolic equivalents, units used to express the intensity or energy cost of physical activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Metabolic equivalents

\medterm{MHC Molecule} A major histocompatibility complex molecule that displays antigens to T cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Major histocompatibility complex molecule

\medterm{Microbe} A microorganism, such as a bacterium, fungus, protozoan, or virus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Microorganism

\medterm{Microdermabrasion} A cosmetic procedure that mechanically removes a superficial layer of skin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micronized} Processed into very small particles to improve dispersion or absorption.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microvasculature} The network of the body’s smallest blood vessels, including arterioles, capillaries, and venules.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Migraine Headache} A recurrent headache disorder often causing moderate-to-severe pain, nausea, and sensitivity to light or sound.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Migraine

\medterm{Miotic} A substance or medicine that constricts the pupil.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Miotic agent

\medterm{Monocytes} Large circulating white blood cells that can enter tissues and become macrophages or dendritic cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Morton’s Neuroma} Thickening and irritation of a nerve, usually between the toes, causing burning forefoot pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Morton neuroma; interdigital neuroma

\medterm{Motilin} A hormone that helps regulate contractions and movement in the upper gastrointestinal tract.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motility} The ability of an organ, especially the intestine, to move its contents.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motor Neuron} A nerve cell that controls skeletal or smooth muscle activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Motor nerve cell

\medterm{Mucosa} Mucosa is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Mucous membrane

\medterm{Mucous Cysts} Small fluid-filled cysts near the end joint of a finger, often associated with osteoarthritis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Digital mucous cyst

\medterm{Mucous Membrane} A moist tissue lining body cavities that open to the outside, including the nose, mouth, and lungs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Mucosa

\medterm{Multi-Infarct Dementia} Cognitive decline caused by the cumulative effects of multiple small strokes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular dementia

\medterm{Multinodular Goiter} An enlarged thyroid containing multiple nodules.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscle Fatigue} Reduced ability of a muscle to generate force after sustained or repeated activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscle Fibers} Individual contractile cells that combine to form skeletal muscle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Muscle cells

\medterm{Muscle-Contraction Headache} A pressure-like headache related to sustained contraction of scalp, neck, or jaw muscles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Tension-type headache

\medterm{Muscular Endurance} The ability of a muscle or muscle group to sustain repeated or prolonged effort.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscularis} A smooth-muscle layer in the wall of a hollow organ, especially the intestine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Muscular layer

\medterm{Musculoskeletal} Relating to muscles, bones, joints, tendons, ligaments, and related tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MUSE} A medicated urethral system that delivers a pellet into the urethra to treat erectile dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Medicated urethral system for erection

\medterm{Myalgia} Muscle pain or tenderness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Myodynia

\medterm{Mydriatic} A substance or medicine that dilates the pupil.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Mydriatic agent

\medterm{Myocyte} A muscle cell.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Muscle cell

\medterm{Myofibrils} Contractile structures inside muscle fibers made of repeating sarcomeres.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myofilaments} Actin and myosin protein filaments that slide past each other to produce muscle contraction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Contractile filaments

\medterm{Myosin} A motor protein that interacts with actin to generate muscle force.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macrobiotics} Diet emphasizing whole grains, vegetables, beans. May be nutritionally inadequate if
restrictive. Long-term followers generally healthy but may have deficiencies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macrodontia} Abnormally large teeth. Microdontia: abnormally small teeth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macroglossia} Macroglossia is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macrolides} A class of bacteriostatic antibiotics that bind to the 50S ribosomal subunit and inhibit
translocation of peptidyl-tRNA during protein synthesis. The major macrolides include
erythromycin, clarithromycin, and azithromycin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macronutrients} Nutrients needed in large amounts: carbohydrates, protein, fat. Provide energy and
structural components.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macrophage Activation Syndrome} A life-threatening complication of rheumatic diseases (especially systemic JIA and SLE)
characterized by uncontrolled cytokine release and hemophagocytosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macrosomia} Macrosomia is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macula} The macula is a specialized region of the central retina approximately five and a half
millimeters in diameter that is responsible for high-acuity central vision, color
perception, and fine detail discrimination, and it contains the highest density of cone
photoreceptors in the eye.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macula Lutea} Small oval yellowish central retinal area approximately 5.5 mm in diameter for
high-acuity vision. Yellow color from xanthophyll pigments (lutein, zeaxanthin)
filtering blue light and protecting photoreceptors. The fovea centralis is the central
depression (1.5 mm) with highest cone density and no rods.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macular} Relating to a macule, a flat, circumscribed change in skin color without a change in texture or elevation.
Macules may be caused by pigment, inflammation, vascular change, or other processes and are
distinguished from raised lesions such as papules.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macular degeneration} A progressive degenerative disease of the central retina (macula) and the leading cause
of irreversible vision loss in individuals over age 50 in developed countries, affecting
approximately 10 percent of those over 65 and 30 percent over 80.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macular Hole} A full-thickness defect of the fovea usually caused by vitreomacular traction,
presenting with central scotoma and distorted or reduced central vision. Staged by OCT;
surgical vitrectomy with internal limiting membrane peel restores closure in most cases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macular Pucker} An epiretinal membrane of fibrocellular tissue on the inner retinal surface that
contracts to distort the macula, causing metamorphopsia and reduced vision. Diagnosed by
OCT; vitrectomy with membrane peeling is reserved for visually significant symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macule} Macule is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maffucci Syndrome} A rare congenital disorder characterized by multiple enchondromas together with
soft-tissue hemangiomas or lymphangiomas. Skeletal deformity, limb shortening,
pathological fractures, and increased risk of cartilage or other malignancy may occur.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MAG3 Renal Scan} Nuclear medicine scan evaluating renal function and obstruction. Differential function
between kidneys. Non-obstructive dilation does not show delayed excretion. Useful for
assessing split renal function and planning surgical interventions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnesium} A predominantly intracellular cation that stabilizes ATP, supports hundreds of enzymes,
and contributes to neuromuscular transmission, nucleic-acid synthesis, and bone
metabolism. Deficiency may cause weakness, tremor, arrhythmias, hypokalemia, or
hypocalcemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnesium Deficiency} An abnormally low body magnesium level, often caused by poor intake, gastrointestinal
loss, renal wasting, or medications. It may cause weakness, tremor, muscle cramps,
seizures, cardiac arrhythmias, hypokalemia, and hypocalcemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnesium Sulfate for Neuroprotection} Intravenous magnesium sulfate given in selected cases of imminent very preterm birth
to reduce the risk of neurologic injury in the infant. The gestational-age criteria, dose,
duration, and monitoring follow local obstetric and neonatal guidance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnetic Resonance Angiography} MRI techniques used to depict arteries or veins, with or without contrast. It may
evaluate stenosis, aneurysm, dissection, thrombosis, and vascular malformation without
ionizing radiation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnetic Resonance Cholangiopancreatography} MRI technique visualizing biliary and pancreatic ducts without contrast. Non-invasive
alternative to ERCP. Used for choledocholithiasis, biliary strictures, pancreatic
pathology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnetic Resonance Imaging} Uses strong magnetic field and radiofrequency pulses to generate images from hydrogen
protons. Excellent soft tissue contrast. No ionizing radiation. Sequences: T1-weighted
(fat bright, fluid dark), T2-weighted (fluid bright), FLAIR (suppresses CSF), DWI
(diffusion restriction). Contrast: gadolinium-based.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnetic Resonance Safety} The practices used to prevent injury from the static magnetic field, gradient fields,
radiofrequency energy, and implanted or ferromagnetic objects in the MRI environment.
Screening must address implants, foreign bodies, devices, pregnancy, burns, hearing
protection, and projectile risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnetic Resonance Spectroscopy} MRI technique measuring metabolic compounds. Used for tumor characterization, metabolic
disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnetic Seed} Sentinel lymph node localization using magnetic seed. Alternative to radioactive tracer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Magnetic Therapy} PEMF for bone healing, pain. Evidence mixed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mahvash Disease} A rare inherited disorder of glucagon signaling caused by inactivating variants in the
glucagon receptor. It may cause pancreatic alpha-cell hyperplasia, enlarged pancreas,
abnormal glucose regulation, and elevated circulating glucagon.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maintenance Dose} The dose of a drug administered at regular intervals to maintain a desired steady-state
concentration, balancing the rate of drug administration with the rate of elimination.
The maintenance dose is calculated as: Maintenance dose rate = (Clearance x desired
concentration) / Bioavailability.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maintenance Therapy} Long-term treatment to prevent recurrence. Examples: hormonal therapy, targeted therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Major Depressive Disorder} A mood disorder characterized by at least one major depressive episode consisting of
five or more symptoms during the same two-week period. At least one symptom must be
either depressed mood or loss of interest or pleasure (anhedonia).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Major Histocompatibility Complex} A locus of highly polymorphic genes encoding cell-surface glycoproteins that present
antigen peptides to T cells. Human Leukocyte Antigen (HLA) in humans. MHC Class I
(HLA-A, B, C): expressed on all nucleated cells, presents endogenous antigens
(viral/tumor) to $\mathrm{CD8^+}$ cytotoxic T cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malabsorption} Malabsorption is impaired digestion or absorption of nutrients in the gastrointestinal tract, causing diarrhea, weight loss, anemia, vitamin deficiency, or bone disease.
pancreatic insufficiency, bacterial overgrowth, Crohn disease, short bowel syndrome, and
infections. Presents with diarrhea, steatorrhea, weight loss, and deficiency of specific
nutrients (iron, B12, vitamin D, calcium).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malacoplakia} A chronic granulomatous inflammatory reaction characterized by macrophages containing
basophilic Michaelis-Gutmann bodies. It most often affects the urinary or
gastrointestinal tract and may form mass-like lesions that mimic malignancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malaise} Malaise is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malakoplakia} Rare chronic inflammatory condition characterized by accumulation of macrophages
containing calcified inclusion bodies called Michaelis-Gutmann bodies, most commonly
affecting the urinary bladder but also occurring in the gastrointestinal tract.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malar Rash} A red or violaceous rash across the cheeks and bridge of the nose, often sparing the folds beside the nose.
It is classically associated with systemic lupus erythematosus but can occur with other conditions;
appearance alone does not establish a diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malaria} Malaria is a mosquito-borne infection caused by Plasmodium parasites, producing fever and hemolysis and potentially progressing to cerebral disease, severe anemia, organ failure, or death.
and anemia; severe disease may affect the brain, lungs, kidneys, blood glucose, or other
organs. Diagnosis and treatment depend on the species, illness severity, and travel or
exposure history.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malate Dehydrogenase} Converts malate to oxaloacetate in TCA cycle. Produces NADH.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MALDI-TOF Mass Spectrometry} Matrix-assisted laser desorption/ionization time-of-flight mass spectrometry, a method
that identifies microorganisms by comparing their protein spectral fingerprints with a
reference database. It provides rapid identification from an isolated colony and does
not by itself establish antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Male Hypoactive Sexual Desire Disorder} A persistent or recurrent lack of sexual or erotic thoughts and desire
in a man that causes personal distress or relationship difficulty. It is distinct from erectile dysfunction, although the
conditions may coexist.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Male Hypogonadism} Reduced production of testosterone, impaired sperm production, or both due to disease of the testes or
the hypothalamic-pituitary system. Possible features include low libido, erectile difficulty,
infertility, reduced muscle mass, fatigue, and loss of bone density.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Male Infertility} Reduced ability to achieve a pregnancy because of factors affecting sperm production, sperm
transport, ejaculation, sexual function, or other reproductive processes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Male Menopause} A nontechnical term sometimes used for age-related decline in testosterone, although it is not equivalent to female
menopause. Low testosterone may cause reduced libido, erectile difficulty, fatigue, depressed mood, or loss of muscle
mass, but symptoms have many causes and require appropriate hormone testing before treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignancy} Malignancy is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant acanthosis nigricans} A paraneoplastic syndrome in which velvety, hyperpigmented, verrucous plaques develop
rapidly in flexural and mucosal areas as a marker of an underlying internal malignancy,
most commonly gastric adenocarcinoma. Unlike benign acanthosis nigricans, the malignant
form is extensive, abrupt in onset, and may involve the oral mucosa.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant Ascites} Intraperitoneal fluid accumulation caused by peritoneal tumor spread, most often ovarian
or gastrointestinal cancer. Management includes therapeutic paracentesis and, in
selected cases, intraperitoneal chemotherapy or shunt placement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant Hypertension} Severely elevated BP greater than 180/120 with end-organ damage. Renal findings include
hyperplastic arteriolosclerosis with onion-skinning and fibrinoid necrosis. Treat with
IV antihypertensives. Poor prognosis if untreated. May cause AKI, encephalopathy, and
retinopathy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant Hyperthermia} A rare, life-threatening pharmacogenetic disorder of skeletal muscle triggered by
volatile inhalation anesthetics and succinylcholine, with incidence of approximately 1
in 15,000 to 50,000 anesthetics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant melanoma} A highly aggressive malignancy arising from melanocytes, characterised by uncontrolled
proliferation and potential for early metastasis via lymphatic and haematogenous spread.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant Mesothelioma} A cancer of the mesothelial lining of the pleura (most common), peritoneum, or
pericardium. Strongly associated with asbestos exposure, presenting with effusion, chest
pain, and dyspnea; prognosis is generally poor.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant Neoplasm} Malignant neoplasms, commonly called cancers, are uncontrolled proliferations of cells
that invade surrounding tissues and have the capacity to metastasize to distant sites
through lymphatic, hematogenous, or transcoelomic routes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
cancers, are uncontrolled proliferations of cells.

\medterm{Malignant Pleural Effusion} Pleural fluid accumulation from pleural or metastatic disease, causing dyspnea. Managed
with thoracentesis, pleurodesis, or an indwelling pleural catheter.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant syphilis} A severe, destructive form of secondary syphilis presenting with necrotic, gummatous, or
ulcerative skin lesions, often in immunocompromised patients, particularly those with
HIV co-infection. It represents an aberrant immune response to heavy Treponema pallidum
infection and requires aggressive intravenous penicillin therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant Tumor} A cancerous growth capable of invading nearby tissue or spreading to distant sites. Its behavior and treatment depend on its type, grade, stage, and molecular features.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malingering} The intentional fabrication or exaggeration of physical or psychological symptoms for
external incentives (avoiding work, obtaining financial compensation, evading criminal
prosecution, obtaining drugs). Not a psychiatric illness (coded as a V-code/Z-code).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mallampati Classification} A simple scoring system used to predict the ease of endotracheal intubation based on the
anatomical structures visible in the oral cavity when the patient opens their mouth wide
and protrudes their tongue without phonating. Class I: soft palate, fauces, uvula, and
tonsillar pillars visible.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malleus} The largest of the three ossicles in the middle ear. See Hammer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mallory-Weiss Tear} Longitudinal mucosal laceration at the gastroesophageal junction caused by forceful
vomiting or retching. It typically presents with hematemesis. Most bleeds spontaneously
within twenty-four to forty-eight hours. Diagnosis is made by upper endoscopy showing a
linear mucosal tear.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malnutrition} Malnutrition includes inadequate, excessive, or imbalanced nutrient intake and can impair growth, immunity, wound healing, muscle function, and organ reserve.
form, and function. Undernutrition: protein-energy malnutrition, micronutrient
deficiencies. Overnutrition: overweight, obesity, related comorbidities. Screen with MNA
(Mini Nutritional Assessment), MUST (Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malocclusion} Misalignment of teeth or incorrect relation between dental arches. Types: Class I
(normal molar relationship, crowding), Class II (retrognathic mandible), Class III
(prognathic mandible).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malpresentation} A fetal presentation other than the usual vertex presentation, including breech, face, brow,
shoulder, or compound presentation. It may complicate labor and influence delivery planning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malrotation} Abnormal rotation or fixation of an organ during development, most often referring to abnormal rotation of the
intestine and its risk of volvulus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maltol} A flavoring compound used in some foods and medicines to provide a sweet, caramel-like odor or taste.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mammary Gland} Modified sweat (apocrine) gland in the breast, overlying pectoralis major, with 15-20
lactiferous ducts converging on the nipple. Composed of glandular lobules (alveoli),
fibrous stroma (Cooper's ligaments), and fat. Development regulated by estrogen,
progesterone, prolactin, and oxytocin (milk let-down).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mastalgia} Breast pain or tenderness, which may be cyclical with menstruation or noncyclical. Causes include hormonal changes, cysts, infection, trauma, and musculoskeletal pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mammary Implant} Breast augmentation/reconstruction device. Types: saline (silicone shell, saline fill),
silicone gel (cohesive gel). Incisions: inframammary, periareolar, axillary. Placement:
subglandular or submuscular. Complications: capsular contracture, rupture, infection,
BIA-ALCL (anaplastic large cell lymphoma).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mammogram} A mammogram is a low-dose x-ray examination of breast tissue used for screening and evaluation of masses, asymmetry, calcifications, or other abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mammography} X-ray imaging of the breast used to screen for or evaluate breast abnormalities. Screening intervals
depend on age, risk, symptoms, and the guideline being followed. Standard views include
the craniocaudal and mediolateral-oblique projections.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mandible} Largest facial bone forming the lower jaw, with horseshoe-shaped body and two rami
ending in condylar (TMJ) and coronoid processes. Mental foramen transmits the mental
nerve. Mandibular foramen is the entry point for inferior alveolar nerve blocks. Most
commonly fractured facial bone, typically at the condyle, angle, or symphysis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mandibular fracture} A break in the lower jaw bone typically caused by trauma, presenting with pain,
swelling, malocclusion, and difficulty opening the mouth; may require surgical fixation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Manganese} An essential trace element required in small amounts. Excessive exposure, especially by inhalation, can injure the nervous system and cause manganism, a movement disorder resembling parkinsonism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mania} A distinct period of abnormally elevated, expansive, or irritable mood with increased
goal-directed activity or energy, lasting \(\geq 1\) week (or any duration if hospitalization
required).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Manic Episode} A distinct period lasting at least seven days (or any duration if hospitalization is
required) of abnormally elevated, expansive, or irritable mood with increased energy or
activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Manifestation} A sign, symptom, or other observable expression of a disease or condition.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mannitol} An osmotic diuretic that is freely filtered at the glomerulus but not reabsorbed,
increasing tubular osmotic pressure and water excretion. Used to reduce intracranial
pressure (cerebral edema) and intraocular pressure (acute glaucoma), and to promote
diuresis in rhabdomyolysis/contrast nephropathy prophylaxis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mannose-Binding Lectin Deficiency} An inherited or acquired deficiency of mannose-binding lectin, a protein
that activates the lectin pathway of complement. It may increase susceptibility to infection in selected people,
especially when other immune defenses are impaired.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mantle Cell Lymphoma} An aggressive B-cell lymphoma characterized by t(11;14) translocation leading to cyclin
D1 overexpression and CCND1-IGH fusion. Clinical: older males, widespread
lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, GI involvement (lymphomatous polyposis), bone marrow,
peripheral blood (leukemic phase).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Manual Removal of Placenta} A procedure in which a clinician removes a placenta that has not separated or cannot be delivered after birth. The timing and need depend on bleeding, maternal condition, and local clinical protocols.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MAOIs} Monoamine oxidase inhibitors are a class of antidepressant drugs that inhibit the enzyme
monoamine oxidase, which breaks down serotonin, norepinephrine, and dopamine in the
brain. By inhibiting this enzyme, MAOIs increase the levels of these neurotransmitters
in the synaptic cleft.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maple Syrup Urine Disease} An autosomal recessive inborn error of branched-chain amino acid metabolism caused by
deficiency of the branched-chain alpha-ketoacid dehydrogenase complex (BCKDC) —
mutations in BCKDHA, BCKDHB, DBT, or DLD.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Marasmus} A severe form of protein-energy malnutrition (PEM) resulting from chronic total calorie
and protein deprivation (starvation). Clinical features include severe muscle wasting,
loss of subcutaneous fat ("skin and bones" appearance), stunted growth, a "monkey-like"
facial appearance, and alertness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maraviroc} A CCR5 antagonist (entry inhibitor) used in combination antiretroviral therapy for HIV-1
infection in patients with CCR5-tropic virus. Maraviroc binds to the human CCR5
coreceptor on CD4+ T cells, preventing HIV-1 gp120 binding and viral entry.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Marfan Syndrome} An autosomal dominant connective tissue disorder caused by mutations in the FBN1 gene
(fibrillin-1). Cardiovascular manifestations include aortic root dilation and
dissection, mitral valve prolapse, and aortic regurgitation. Skeletal features include
tall stature, arachnodactyly, pectus deformity, and joint hypermobility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Marfanoid Habitus} A body habitus characterized by tall stature, long limbs, long fingers (arachnodactyly),
and joint laxity. Seen in Marfan syndrome, but also in other connective tissue disorders
and homocystinuria. Differentiated from Marfan syndrome by absence of aortic root
dilation and lens subluxation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Marfan’s syndrome} An autosomal dominant connective tissue disorder caused by mutations in the FBN1 gene
encoding fibrillin-1. Neurological manifestations include dural ectasia (widening of the
dural sac, causing back pain and headaches), spontaneous intracranial hypotension from
CSF leaks, and increased risk of stroke from arterial dissection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Marrow hyperplasia} Increased cellularity of the bone marrow due to accelerated production of blood cells,
typically seen as a compensatory response to chronic hemolysis, hemorrhage, or hypoxia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mass} A discrete area of abnormal tissue that is larger or more substantial than a nodule. A mass may be benign or malignant and can occur in many organs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mass Casualty Incident} Event overwhelming available resources, requiring triage and resource allocation. Triage
systems: START (Simple Triage and Rapid Treatment), SALT. Categories: immediate (red),
delayed (yellow), minor (green), expectant (black). Incident command system (ICS)
coordinates response.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Masseter Muscle} Thick rectangular muscle of mastication on the mandibular ramus, from the zygomatic arch
to mandibular angle/ramus. Innervated by the masseteric nerve (V3). Most powerful
masticatory muscle, elevating the mandible with up to 70 kg force. Involved in bruxism,
TM disorders, and myofascial pain. Hypertrophy can widen facial appearance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Massive Hemoptysis} Life-threatening coughing of blood that threatens the airway, breathing, or circulation; a fixed
volume threshold is not required. It requires urgent airway assessment, stabilization, and
definitive treatment of the bleeding source.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Massive Transfusion Protocol} A coordinated protocol for life-threatening hemorrhage that provides rapid, balanced
delivery of red cells, plasma, and platelets with monitoring and correction of
fibrinogen, calcium, temperature, and acid-base abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mast Cell} A tissue-resident immune cell with large granules containing histamine, heparin,
tryptase, and cytokines; bears high-affinity IgE receptors (Fc-epsilon-RI).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mast Cell Activation Syndrome} A condition involving recurrent symptoms from inappropriate release of mast-cell mediators, such as flushing,
itching, hives, abdominal symptoms, wheezing, or low blood pressure. Diagnosis requires compatible episodes,
objective evidence of mediator release, and response to appropriate treatment; many similar conditions must be excluded.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mastectomy} Mastectomy examines mastectomy; interpretation depends on the specimen or body region, test method, reference range, and clinical question.
areola, and nipple. Modified radical mastectomy (MRM): total mastectomy + axillary lymph
node dissection (levels I-III), for invasive breast cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mastitis} Inflammation of breast tissue, often occurring during lactation but also possible outside lactation. It may
cause localized pain, warmth, swelling, and fever; infection and inflammatory causes
must be distinguished clinically.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mastocytosis} A clonal disorder in which abnormal mast cells accumulate in the skin or internal
organs. Cutaneous disease commonly causes urticaria pigmentosa: tan-brown macules or
papules that swell and itch after rubbing (Darier sign).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mastoidectomy} Mastoidectomy examines mastoidectomy; interpretation depends on the specimen or body region, test method, reference range, and clinical question.
from the mastoid portion of the temporal bone, performed for chronic otitis media with
cholesteatoma, mastoiditis, or as a component of surgery for acoustic neuroma and other
skull base tumors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mastoiditis} Mastoiditis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
media. Presents with postauricular pain, swelling, erythema, and tenderness, fever, and
otorrhea. Diagnosis: CT temporal bone shows mastoid air cell opacification with bony
destruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Masturbation} Self-stimulation of the genitals for sexual pleasure. It is a common human behavior; it is generally
not harmful unless it causes injury, distress, or interferes with daily life.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maternal Physiologic Changes in Pregnancy} Extensive adaptive changes across all organ systems to support the growing fetus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maternal Pushing (Second Stage)} Maternal expulsive efforts during the second stage of labor to facilitate fetal descent.
Directed (closed-glottis, Valsalva) pushing: sustained breath-hold for 10 seconds with
three pushes per contraction. Spontaneous (open-glottis) pushing: shorter pushes with
the mother pushing when she feels an urge.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Second Stage

\medterm{Maternal Sepsis} Organ dysfunction caused by a dysregulated maternal response to infection during
pregnancy, childbirth, or the postpartum period. It may arise from uterine, urinary,
respiratory, wound, or other infection and requires prompt cultures, antimicrobial
therapy, source control, and hemodynamic support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maturity Onset Diabetes of the Young} A monogenic form of diabetes with autosomal dominant inheritance, typically presenting
before age 25 with non-insulin-dependent hyperglycemia. Caused by mutations in
transcription factors or enzymes of beta-cell function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maxilla} Paired bone forming the upper jaw, central face, and parts of orbit, nasal cavity, and
hard palate. Body contains the maxillary sinus (largest paranasal sinus). Four
processes: frontal, zygomatic, alveolar (upper teeth), and palatine (anterior hard
palate). Fractures classified using the Le Fort system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maxillofacial Prosthesis} Artificial replacement of maxillofacial structures. Obturator for cleft palate or
surgical defect. Ocular, nasal, auricular prostheses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maximum Plasma Concentration (Cmax)} The highest measured plasma concentration after a dose. Cmax and the time to reach it
are used to describe absorption rate, peak-related toxicity, and comparative
pharmacokinetics.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Cmax

\medterm{Maximum Tolerated Dose} The highest dose of a drug that produces acceptable, manageable toxicity without causing
unacceptable adverse effects, determined through dose-escalation studies, most commonly
in Phase I clinical trials. The MTD identifies the dose range for subsequent efficacy
studies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{May-Thurner Syndrome} Compression of left common iliac vein by right common iliac artery. Causes left lower
extremity DVT. Diagnosis: CT/MR venography, phlebography. Treatment: angioplasty and
stenting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MCA Peak Systolic Velocity} Doppler measurement of the peak systolic velocity in the fetal middle cerebral artery.
An indicator of fetal anemia: elevated MCA PSV (>1.5 MoM) correlates with
moderate-to-severe anemia. The most important application is surveillance of
Rh-sensitized pregnancies (detect anemia before hydrops develops).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{McCune-Albright Syndrome} Sporadic condition caused by activating mutations in the GNAS1 gene encoding the Gs
alpha subunit leading to constitutive activation of adenylyl cyclase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{McDonald Cerclage} A transvaginal cervical cerclage using a purse-string suture (Mersilene tape or Prolene)
placed at the cervicovaginal junction, without bladder dissection. The most common
cerclage technique. Indications: cervical insufficiency (history-indicated or
ultrasound-indicated short cervix <25 mm before 24 weeks). Placed at 12-14 weeks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{McMurray Test} A knee examination in which the leg is flexed, rotated, and extended to place stress on the menisci. Pain or a palpable click may suggest a meniscal lesion but is not diagnostic alone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{McRoberts Maneuver} A positioning maneuver for managing shoulder dystocia where the mother’s legs are
hyperflexed onto her abdomen, with thighs against the abdomen. This flattens the sacrum,
increases the anteroposterior diameter of the pelvis, and rotates the symphysis pubis
superiorly. Often combined with suprapubic pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mean Arterial Pressure} The average pressure driving systemic blood flow during one cardiac cycle. It is
influenced by cardiac output and systemic vascular resistance and is approximated
clinically as diastolic pressure plus one-third of pulse pressure at normal heart rates.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mean Corpuscular Hemoglobin Concentration} The mean concentration of hemoglobin within packed erythrocytes, calculated from
hemoglobin and hematocrit. A reduced MCHC indicates hypochromia; an increased value may
occur with spherocytosis or analytical interference.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mean Corpuscular Volume} The average volume of an erythrocyte, reported in femtoliters as part of the CBC. A low
MCV indicates microcytosis, whereas a high MCV indicates macrocytosis; interpretation
requires correlation with hemoglobin, reticulocytes, and other indices.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Measles} Measles is a highly contagious measles-virus infection causing fever, cough, conjunctivitis, and a spreading maculopapular rash, with possible pneumonia, encephalitis, or other complications.
droplets. Prodrome of fever, coryza, cough, and conjunctivitis, followed by Koplik spots
(white buccal mucosal lesions) and maculopapular rash spreading cephalocaudally.
Complications: pneumonia, encephalitis, and subacute sclerosing panencephalitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Measles virus} A negative-sense RNA virus that causes measles, a highly contagious respiratory illness.
The virus is transmitted by respiratory droplets and is one of the most infectious
pathogens known. Symptoms include fever, cough, coryza, conjunctivitis, and rash.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Measles-Rubella Vaccine} A combined live attenuated vaccine containing measles and rubella antigens without the
mumps component, widely used in countries where mumps is not a significant public health
burden, including India's Universal Immunization Schedule.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Measurable Residual Disease} The burden of cancer remaining after therapy that can be quantified using a validated
assay. The term is increasingly preferred to minimal residual disease because residual
disease may be clinically important even when not minimal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meatuses} The nasal meatuses are the air passages located beneath each corresponding turbinate on
the lateral wall of the nasal cavity, and they serve as the drainage pathways for the
paranasal sinuses and the nasolacrimal duct.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mebrofenin Scan} Hepatobiliary imaging using Tc-99m mebrofenin. Evaluates liver function, biliary
obstruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mechanical Circulatory Support} Devices that provide temporary or permanent cardiac support. Options include
intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP), Impella (percutaneous ventricular assist device),
TandemHeart, extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO), and durable left ventricular
assist devices (LVADs).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mechanical Insufflation-Exsufflation} A device that delivers positive pressure (insufflation) followed by rapid negative
pressure (exsufflation) to simulate a cough. It is used in neuromuscular disease
(Duchenne muscular dystrophy, spinal cord injury, ALS) to augment cough effectiveness
and clear secretions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mechanical Ventilation} Mechanical ventilation provides respiratory support by delivering controlled or
spontaneous breaths through an endotracheal tube or tracheostomy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mechanism of Death} The physiologic or biochemical derangement that causes death (the final failure) as
opposed to the underlying disease or injury (cause of death) and the circumstances
(manner). Examples: ventricular fibrillation, cerebral hemorrhage, sepsis, respiratory
failure, exsanguination, cardiac tamponade.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mechanism of Toxicity} How substance causes harm. Examples: enzyme inhibition, membrane disruption, receptor
binding, oxidative stress, DNA damage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meckel Diverticulum} Congenital remnant of the omphalomesenteric duct located on the antimesenteric border of
the distal ileum approximately sixty centimeters from the ileocecal valve.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meckel's diverticulum} A congenital pouch of the small intestine caused by persistence of the embryonic vitelline duct. It
may bleed, inflame, or cause obstruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meconium Aspiration} Meconium aspiration syndrome (MAS): aspiration of meconium-stained amniotic fluid into
the fetal/neonatal airway causing airway obstruction, chemical pneumonitis, and
surfactant dysfunction—respiratory distress in term/post-term infants. Risk: fetal
distress/hypoxia (passage of meconium in utero).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meconium Aspiration Syndrome} Respiratory distress in newborns born through meconium-stained amniotic fluid, caused by
aspiration of meconium into the airways. Presents with respiratory distress,
barrel-shaped chest, and coarse crackles. Meconium is thick, greenish-black fetal stool
passed in utero. Risk factors include post-term pregnancy and fetal distress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meconium Ileus} Intestinal obstruction in neonates caused by inspissated meconium most commonly
associated with cystic fibrosis. Presents with abdominal distension, bilious vomiting,
and failure to pass meconium within forty-eight hours.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meconium Peritonitis} Sterile chemical peritonitis resulting from in utero intestinal perforation allowing
meconium to spill into the peritoneal cavity. Most commonly associated with cystic
fibrosis causing inspissated meconium obstruction, intestinal atresia, and volvulus. May
be detected prenatally as fetal ascites with echogenic peritoneal calcifications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meconium-Stained Amniotic Fluid} Greenish or yellowish discoloration of amniotic fluid due to fetal meconium passage in
utero. Associated with fetal distress, post-term pregnancy, and fetal growth
restriction. Thick meconium increases risk of meconium aspiration syndrome. Management
of non-vigorous newborns: tracheal suctioning before positive pressure ventilation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medial} Medial concerns medial, a body structure, substance, or clinical concept whose meaning depends on location, function, and patient context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medial Collateral Ligament Injury} Tear of the MCL, the most common knee ligament injury. Mechanism: valgus stress to the
knee (direct blow to lateral knee). Presents with medial knee pain, swelling, and valgus
laxity. Grading: I (pain, no laxity), II (partial tear, some laxity), III (complete
tear, significant laxity).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medial Cuneiform} Largest cuneiform, on the medial foot between navicular and first metatarsal. Insertion
for tibialis anterior and posterior tendons supporting the medial longitudinal arch. The
first tarsometatarsal joint (Lisfranc joint) is an important landmark; Lisfranc injuries
involve fracture-dislocation at this level.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medial Rotation} Rotation of a limb toward the median plane of the body.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Median Nerve} From lateral and medial cords (C5-T1), passing through the arm without branching,
through the cubital fossa, into the forearm innervating most flexor muscles. In the hand
innervates thenar muscles (opponens, abductor brevis, flexor brevis pollicis) and first
two lumbricals. Sensation to lateral 3.5 digits.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Median Plane} A sagittal plane that passes directly through the midline of the body, dividing it into
equal right and left halves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Median rhomboid glossitis} A smooth, red, diamond-shaped depapillated area on the dorsum of the tongue caused by
chronic Candida infection, usually asymptomatic and managed with antifungal therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Median Survival} The time by which half of the people in a study have experienced the specified event, such as death. It is a summary measure of survival in a defined population.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mediastinitis} Mediastinitis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
may follow surgery, esophageal injury, or infection and can cause severe systemic illness; chronic cases may result from
granulomatous or other inflammatory disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mediastinoscopy} A surgical procedure that accesses the mediastinum through a small incision near the sternum to
inspect or biopsy mediastinal lymph nodes or masses. It is one option for staging lung cancer
when less-invasive sampling is nondiagnostic or unsuitable.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mediastinum} The central compartment of the thoracic cavity between the lungs, containing the heart,
major blood vessels, esophagus, and trachea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mediators of Inflammation} Inflammatory mediators are soluble molecules that initiate, amplify, or regulate the
inflammatory response.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Cannabis and Cannabidiol} Cannabis (marijuana) and cannabidiol (CBD) are cannabinoid compounds derived from
Cannabis sativa that interact with the endocannabinoid system (CB1 and CB2 receptors) to
modulate pain, appetite, mood, and inflammation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Condition} A state affecting health or bodily function, whether acute, chronic, symptomatic, or
asymptomatic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Ethics} Moral principles guiding healthcare practice, including autonomy, beneficence,
non-maleficence, and justice.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Examiner} An appointed physician, usually a board-certified forensic pathologist, charged with
investigating sudden, unexpected, violent, or suspicious deaths, ordering autopsies,
determining cause and manner of death, and certifying the death certificate. Unlike the
elected coroner, a medical examiner must meet professional qualifications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Illness} The subjective experience of being unwell, which may or may not correspond to an
identifiable disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Jargon} Specialized terminology used by healthcare professionals. Unexplained medical jargon can
hinder communication with patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Jurisprudence} The branch of law (and its study) that deals with the rights and duties of physicians
and the legal aspects of medical practice, distinct from but overlapping with forensic
medicine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Negligence (Malpractice)} Legal tort occurring when a healthcare provider fails to exercise the standard of
reasonable care, skill, or diligence expected of a prudent practitioner of similar
standing, resulting in injury, harm, or death to a patient.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Malpractice

\medterm{Medical Nutrition Therapy} Individualized nutrition intervention by registered dietitian. Conditions: diabetes,
renal disease, GI disorders, cancer, obesity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Physicist} Expert in radiation physics and safety.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Physics} The application of physics to medicine, including diagnostic imaging, radiation therapy, radiation safety, and physiological
measurement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Privacy} Protection of identifiable patient information and control of its disclosure in
healthcare.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Records} A documented account of a patient's clinical history, examination, investigations,
treatment, and outcome maintained by providers. In medicolegal practice, records are
contemporaneous evidence in malpractice, injury, criminal, and death cases, so accuracy,
timeliness, and unaltered amendments are essential.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Tattooing} Medical tattooing (permanent makeup, micropigmentation) covers depigmented skin
(vitiligo, vitiligo camouflage), restores areolas after mastectomy, and masks scars.
Trichopigmentation simulates hair on bald scalp. Medical tattooing uses specialized
pigments and techniques.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medical Tests} Diagnostic procedures used to screen for, confirm, or monitor disease through analysis
of biologic specimens, imaging, physiologic measurements, or genetic material.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Adherence} The extent to which a patient’s medication-taking behavior corresponds with the agreed
treatment plan. Adherence is influenced by understanding, adverse effects, cost, access,
regimen complexity, beliefs, and cognitive or social factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Adverse-Effect Profile} The clinically relevant pattern of predictable pharmacologic effects and harmful
reactions associated with a medication. It includes common dose-related effects, serious
rare events, organ-specific toxicity, interactions, and monitoring requirements.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Cascade} A prescribing sequence in which an adverse drug effect is misinterpreted as a new
medical condition and another drug is prescribed to treat it. Recognizing cascades is an
important goal of medication reconciliation and deprescribing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Deprescribing} A planned, supervised process of reducing the dose or stopping a medicine when its
current or future harms outweigh its benefits or when treatment goals change. Tapering
may be required to prevent withdrawal or rebound disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Error} A preventable event that may cause or lead to inappropriate medication use or patient
harm while the medication is in the control of a healthcare professional, patient, or
consumer. Errors can occur during prescribing, transcribing, dispensing, administration,
or monitoring.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Management} The systematic process of optimizing pharmacotherapy to achieve therapeutic goals while
minimizing adverse effects, encompassing prescribing, dispensing, administration,
monitoring, and deprescribing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Reconciliation} A structured process of obtaining and comparing the most accurate medication list with
current prescriptions at transitions of care. It identifies omissions, duplications,
incorrect doses, interactions, and unintended changes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Review} Systematic examination of all medications for appropriateness. Components: indication,
efficacy, safety, adherence, interactions. Tools: Beers Criteria, STOPP/START. Goals:
deprescribing, optimizing therapy, reducing adverse effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Safety} Practices that reduce medication errors and prevent avoidable harm during prescribing, dispensing, administration, and use.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Side Effects} Unintended harmful responses to medications occurring at standard doses, ranging from
mild (nausea, rash, dry mouth) to severe (anaphylaxis, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, QT
prolongation, hepatotoxicity).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medications and Treatments} all pharmacologic and therapeutic interventions used to prevent, manage, or cure
disease, classified by mechanism of action, therapeutic class, and route of
administration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medicolegal} Pertaining to the application of medical knowledge to legal questions and the
intersection of medicine and the law. Covers forensic pathology, toxicology, psychiatry,
and clinical issues: informed consent, malpractice, medical records, disability,
personal injury, occupational disease, and public health law.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mediterranean Diet} Traditional eating pattern of Greece, Southern Italy. Emphasizes: fruits, vegetables,
whole grains, legumes, nuts, olive oil, moderate fish/poultry, limited red meat.
Associated with reduced cardiovascular disease, diabetes, cognitive decline.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medulla Oblongata} Most inferior brainstem segment, continuous with the spinal cord at the foramen magnum.
Contains vital autonomic centers: cardiovascular center (heart rate, blood pressure),
respiratory center (dorsal/ventral respiratory groups), and area postrema (vomiting
center).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medullary Sponge Kidney} Congenital malformation characterized by cystic dilatation of the collecting ducts in
the renal papillae predisposing to nephrolithiasis and nephrocalcinosis. Presents with
recurrent kidney stones, hematuria, and urinary tract infections.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma} Neuroendocrine tumor arising from parafollicular C cells that produce calcitonin.
Accounts for approximately five percent of thyroid carcinomas. May be sporadic or
hereditary as part of multiple endocrine neoplasia type two A or two B. Serum calcitonin
serves as a tumor marker. Diagnosis requires FNA with calcitonin immunohistochemistry.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medulloblastoma} A fast-growing embryonal tumor of the cerebellum and the most common malignant brain
tumor of childhood. Treated with surgical resection, craniospinal radiation, and
chemotherapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meige Syndrome} A segmental cranial dystonia characterized by involuntary bilateral blepharospasm with
dystonic contractions of the lower face, jaw, or tongue. Symptoms fluctuate and may be
worsened by stress, speech, chewing, or sensory stimuli.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meigs Syndrome} The triad of benign ovarian fibroma, ascites, and pleural effusion. The effusions
typically resolve after removal of the ovarian tumour and should be distinguished from
malignant ovarian disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meiosis} A specialized cell division that produces haploid eggs or sperm from a diploid precursor cell and creates
genetic variation through recombination and chromosome assortment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melancholic Features} A specifier for major depressive episodes characterized by a loss of interest or
pleasure in all or almost all activities, a depressed mood that is qualitatively
distinct from grief, prominent psychomotor retardation or agitation, significant
anorexia or weight loss, excessive guilt, and worsening of mood in the morning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melanocytes} Dendritic pigment-producing neural crest-derived cells in the epidermal basal layer and
hair follicles, comprising approximately 5-10 percent of basal keratinocytes. Produce
melanin (eumelanin, brown-black; pheomelanin, yellow-red) in melanosomes, transferred to
keratinocytes via dendritic processes for UV protection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melanoma} Melanoma is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melanoma Surveillance} regular skin examination, patient education on self-examination, and dermoscopic
monitoring of atypical nevi. High-risk patients (personal or family history, numerous
atypical nevi, immunosuppression) require 6-month follow-up. Standard patients require
annual examination. Total body photography establishes baseline for change detection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melanoma Treatment} Stage-dependent. Stage 0 to IA: wide local excision. Stage IB to IIA: wide excision with
sentinel lymph node biopsy. Stage IIB to IIC: wide excision, SLNB, and adjuvant therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melasma} Melasma is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
chloasma or.

\medterm{Melasma (Chloasma)} An acquired, chronic pigmentary disorder characterized by symmetric, hyperpigmented,
light-to-dark brown macules and patches on sun-exposed areas of the face (malar,
forehead, upper lip, chin). It primarily affects women of childbearing age and
individuals with darker skin types (Fitzpatrick types III-V).



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Chloasma

\medterm{Melatonin Receptor Agonists} Medications that act on MT1 and MT2 receptors in the suprachiasmatic nucleus to regulate
circadian rhythms. Ramelteon is approved for insomnia with sleep onset difficulty. It
has no abuse potential and does not cause dependence. It is a useful alternative to
benzodiazepines for sleep initiation problems.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MELD Score} Model for End-Stage Liver Disease predicting three-month mortality in advanced liver
disease used for liver transplant allocation. The score is calculated using serum
bilirubin, creatinine, and international normalized ratio generating a continuous score
from approximately six to forty points.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melena} Passage of black, tarry, foul-smelling stools due to the presence of digested blood,
indicating upper gastrointestinal bleeding (usually proximal to the ligament of Treitz).
The black color results from oxidation of haemoglobin to haematin by intestinal enzymes
and bacteria.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melioidosis} An infectious disease caused by Burkholderia pseudomallei, usually acquired from
contaminated soil or water through inoculation or inhalation. It may cause pneumonia,
skin infection, abscesses, sepsis, or chronic disease and is more severe in people with
diabetes or other risk factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Melorheostosis} A rare sclerosing bone dysplasia producing irregular, flowing cortical hyperostosis on
imaging. It may cause pain, stiffness, limb deformity, restricted movement,
contractures, or soft-tissue fibrosis and can affect one or several contiguous bones.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Memantine} An NMDA receptor antagonist (uncompetitive, low-to-moderate affinity) used for moderate
to severe Alzheimer disease, often added to an acetylcholinesterase inhibitor. Blocks
excessive glutamate-mediated excitotoxicity while preserving normal synaptic
transmission. Once or twice daily oral.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Membrane Potential} The electrical potential difference across a cell membrane, approximately -70 mV in most
neurons (resting membrane potential). Established by the unequal distribution of ions
(primarily K+, Na+, Cl-) across the membrane and selective permeability.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Membranoproliferative Glomerulonephritis} Pattern of glomerular injury with mesangial proliferation and capillary wall thickening
creating tram-track appearance on silver stain. Hepatitis C is common cause of immune
complex MPGN. C3 glomerulopathy represents complement-mediated disease. Associated with
monoclonal gammopathies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Membranous Nephropathy} A glomerular disease caused by immune-complex deposition along the glomerular
basement membrane. It commonly causes proteinuria and nephrotic syndrome and may be primary or secondary to another
disease, medication, or infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Memory} The cognitive process of encoding, storing, and retrieving information, mediated by
distributed neural networks involving the hippocampus, medial temporal lobe, prefrontal
cortex, and limbic system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Memory Cell} Long-lived lymphocytes (memory B cells and memory T cells) that survive after an immune
response and mount rapid, enhanced responses to re-exposure to the same antigen
(immunological memory).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Memory Loss} Reduced ability to recall or learn information. It may result from normal aging, medical illness, medicines,
brain injury, or a neurocognitive disorder.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mendelson Syndrome} A chemical pneumonitis caused by inhalation of acidic stomach contents, classically during anesthesia
or childbirth. It can cause cough, breathing difficulty, and impaired oxygenation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meniere Disease} An inner ear disorder characterized by recurrent episodes of vertigo lasting 20 minutes
to 12 hours, with fluctuating sensorineural hearing loss, tinnitus, and aural fullness.
Due to endolymphatic hydrops. Diagnosis: clinical criteria; audiometry shows
low-frequency SNHL during attacks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meninges} The three membranes enveloping the brain and spinal cord: dura mater (outermost, tough),
arachnoid mater (middle, avascular), and pia mater (innermost, adherent to the CNS
surface). The subarachnoid space (between arachnoid and pia) contains cerebrospinal
fluid. The epidural and subdural spaces are sites of hemorrhage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myringitis} Inflammation of the tympanic membrane, usually caused by infection or irritation and sometimes associated with painful blisters on the eardrum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motor Vehicle Accident} A collision or other road-traffic event causing injury or property damage. Medical evaluation may be needed for visible or delayed injuries, including head, neck, chest, abdominal, or musculoskeletal trauma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meningioma} A usually slow-growing tumor arising from cells associated with the meninges. Most are benign, but some
are atypical or malignant and can cause symptoms by compressing the brain, spinal cord, or
cranial nerves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meningitis} Meningitis is inflammation of the membranes around the brain and spinal cord, caused by infection or noninfectious inflammation and presenting with headache, fever, neck stiffness, or altered mental status.
infection but also by bacteria, fungi, parasites, medicines, or autoimmune disease. Symptoms
may include fever, headache, neck stiffness, light sensitivity, or altered mental status and
require urgent assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meningocele} A congenital defect in which the membranes surrounding the spinal cord or brain protrude through an opening
in the skull or spine, forming a fluid-filled sac. It may occur with other neural-tube defects and
can cause neurologic problems; evaluation commonly includes imaging and specialist assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meningococcal Disease} A bacterial infection caused by Neisseria meningitidis (serogroups A, B, C, W, Y),
transmitted via respiratory droplets. Meningococcemia: sudden onset fever,
petechial/purpuric rash, hypotension, and DIC. Meningococcal meningitis: fever, nuchal
rigidity, headache, photophobia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meningococcal infection} Infection with Neisseria meningitidis, which may cause meningitis, bloodstream infection, or
localized respiratory disease. Severe illness can progress rapidly.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meningococcal Vaccine} Conjugate vaccine (MenACWY) and serogroup B vaccine (MenB) given at 11-12 years with
booster at 16 years. Protects against Neisseria meningitidis, causing meningitis and
septicemia. MenACWY booster at 16 years covers the high-risk period for serogroup C and
Y disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meningoencephalitis} Inflammation of both the meninges surrounding the brain and the brain tissue itself, usually caused by
infection or autoimmune disease. Fever, headache, neck stiffness, confusion, seizures, or reduced
consciousness requires urgent medical assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meniscal Injuries} Tears or other damage to the knee menisci, which are crescent-shaped pieces of cartilage
that help distribute load and stabilize the knee. Symptoms may include joint-line pain,
swelling, clicking, or restricted motion. Diagnosis is based on examination and, when needed, imaging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meniscal Tear} Tear of the C-shaped fibrocartilage (medial or lateral meniscus) that provides shock
absorption and stability in the knee. Medial meniscus tears more common (attached to
capsule). Mechanism: twisting injury with weight-bearing. Presents with joint line
tenderness, pain with deep squatting, catching, locking, and giving way.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menkes Disease} An X-linked recessive disorder of copper metabolism caused by ATP7A mutations, resulting
in impaired intestinal copper absorption and defective copper transport across the
blood-brain barrier. Copper accumulates in intestinal and renal cells but is deficient
in the liver and brain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menkes Kinky Hair Syndrome} An X-linked disorder of copper transport caused by ATP7A dysfunction. It produces
progressive neurodegeneration, seizures, developmental delay, hypopigmented or brittle
kinky hair, connective-tissue abnormalities, and failure to thrive.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menopausal Hormone Therapy} Menopausal hormone therapy (MHT), formerly called hormone replacement therapy (HRT), is
the use of estrogen with or without progestogen to alleviate moderate-to-severe
menopausal vasomotor symptoms (hot flashes, night sweats), genitourinary syndrome of
menopause (vaginal dryness, dyspareunia, urinary urgency), and prevent osteoporosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menopause} Menopause is the permanent end of menstrual periods after loss of ovarian follicular activity, accompanied by hormonal changes and possible vasomotor, genitourinary, sleep, and bone effects.
woman’s final period.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menarche} The first menstrual period, marking the onset of menstruation. Its timing varies with genetics, nutrition, health, and pubertal development; markedly early or delayed menarche may require assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menopause and Perimenopause} Menopause is the permanent cessation of menstruation for 12 consecutive months,
reflecting ovarian follicle depletion and estrogen decline, occurring at a median age of
51--52 years in North America.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menorrhagia} Heavy or prolonged menstrual bleeding that interferes with physical, social, emotional, or material
quality of life. Causes include fibroids, adenomyosis, endometrial polyps, bleeding disorders,
thyroid disease, and ovulatory dysfunction; it may cause iron-deficiency anemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menstrual Cramps} Cramping pain in the lower abdomen during or around menstruation, medically called dysmenorrhea. Pain may radiate to
the back or thighs and may occur with nausea, diarrhea, headache, or fatigue. Severe, worsening, or newly
developed pain requires evaluation for causes such as endometriosis or fibroids.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menstrual Cycle} The recurring hormonal and uterine changes that prepare the body for possible pregnancy. Cycle length and
bleeding pattern vary among individuals and across life stages.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Menstruation} The periodic shedding of the endometrial lining as part of the menstrual cycle when pregnancy does not occur.
The timing, duration, and amount of bleeding vary with age, hormonal state, medicines,
and health conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mental Health} Mental health encompasses emotional, psychologic, and social well-being, affecting how
individuals think, feel, behave, and handle stress, relationships, and decision-making.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mental Status Examination} A structured assessment of the patient's current psychological functioning performed
during the clinical interview. It evaluates appearance, behaviour, speech, mood, affect,
thought process, thought content, perceptions, cognition, insight, and judgment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mercury} Liquid metal combining with other metals to form amalgam. Concerns about toxicity,
proper handling essential.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mercury Poisoning} Three forms: elemental (inhalation, neurological), inorganic (renal, GI), organic
(methylmercury, neurological). Minamata disease from methylmercury. Treatment: chelation
(dimercaprol, DMSA), remove exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Merkel Cell Carcinoma} An aggressive neuroendocrine skin cancer arising from Merkel cells. It presents as a
rapidly growing, painless, red-violet nodule on sun-exposed skin of elderly patients.
Approximately 80 percent are associated with Merkel cell polyomavirus. Risk factors
include immunosuppression, UV exposure, and fair skin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meropenem-vaborbactam} A combination of the carbapenem meropenem with the cyclic boronate beta-lactamase
inhibitor vaborbactam. Vaborbactam inhibits class A and class C beta-lactamases,
including KPC carbapenemases, but does not inhibit class D or metallo-beta-lactamases.
This combination has activity against many carbapenem-resistant Enterobacteriaceae.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MERRF Syndrome} Myoclonic epilepsy with ragged-red fibres, a mitochondrial disorder usually caused by a
pathogenic mitochondrial DNA variant. It features myoclonus, generalized epilepsy,
ataxia, and skeletal-muscle mitochondrial abnormalities, with variable multisystem
involvement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mesenteric Artery Ischemia} Inadequate blood flow through the mesenteric arteries, usually caused by embolism,
thrombosis, or severe low-flow states. Acute disease presents with severe abdominal pain
out of proportion to examination and can progress to bowel infarction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mesenteric Ischemia} Reduced blood flow to intestines. Acute: embolism, thrombosis. Chronic: atherosclerosis.
CT angiography diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mesentery} The fan-shaped double layer of peritoneum attaching the small intestine (jejunum and
ileum) to the posterior abdominal wall, carrying the superior mesenteric artery, vein,
lymphatics, and autonomic nerves between its layers. Root extends from duodenojejunal
junction to ileocecal junction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mesh} A synthetic or biological implant used to provide structural support in pelvic organ
prolapse and stress urinary incontinence surgery. Types: polypropylene (most common,
mid-urethral slings for SUI, vaginal mesh for prolapse), biological (porcine, bovine, or
cadaveric dermis).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mesoderm} The middle germ layer in an embryo, which develops into muscles, bones, cartilage, blood
vessels, and the kidneys.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Messenger RNA} Single-stranded RNA molecule transcribed from DNA template, carrying genetic code from
nucleus to ribosomes for protein synthesis. Contains 5' cap (methylguanosine), 5' UTR,
coding sequence (codons), 3' UTR, and poly-A tail. Eukaryotic mRNA undergoes processing:
splicing (intron removal, exon ligation), capping, polyadenylation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meta-Analysis} A statistical method that combines quantitative results from multiple studies addressing a related question. Its validity depends on the quality and comparability of the studies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metabolic Acidosis} A primary acid-base disorder characterized by decreased serum bicarbonate (<22 mEq/L)
and pH <7.35. Causes are classified by anion gap (AG). High AG: diabetic ketoacidosis,
lactic acidosis, renal failure, starvation, toxins (methanol, ethylene glycol,
salicylates).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metabolic Alkalosis} A primary increase in plasma bicarbonate or loss of hydrogen ions. Causes include
vomiting, diuretic use, mineralocorticoid excess, and chloride depletion; respiratory
compensation increases carbon dioxide but is limited by hypoxemia and acidosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metabolic Rate} The rate at which the body uses energy to maintain life and perform activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metabolic Syndrome} Cluster of conditions: abdominal obesity, hypertension, hyperglycemia, high
triglycerides, low HDL. Increases cardiovascular disease and diabetes risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metabolic Tumor Volume} Total volume of metabolically active tumor on PET. Prognostic in lymphoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metabolism} Metabolism concerns metabolism, a body structure, substance, or clinical concept whose meaning depends on location, function, and patient context.
anabolism (synthesis, consumes energy). Central pathways: glycolysis, TCA cycle,
oxidative phosphorylation, pentose phosphate pathway. Metabolic regulation ensures
energy homeostasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metacarpals} Five long bones (I-V) forming the palm framework. Each has base, shaft, and head. The
first is shortest and most mobile, articulating with the trapezium at a saddle joint for
opposition. Second/third are firmly fixed; fourth/fifth more mobile. Bennett's fracture
affects the first metacarpal base; boxer's fracture affects the fifth neck.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metacarpophalangeal Joint} A condyloid synovial joint between a metacarpal head and the base of a proximal phalanx.
It permits flexion, extension, abduction, adduction, and limited circumduction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metagenomic Sequencing} Sequencing of nucleic acid recovered directly from a clinical or environmental specimen
to identify microbial genetic material without requiring growth in culture. It can
detect unusual or polymicrobial infections but is vulnerable to contamination and
requires clinical interpretation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metal Artifact} Image degradation from metal implants. Reduced by metal artifact reduction sequences
(MRI), iterative reconstruction (CT).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metaphyseal Corner Fractures} Bucket-handle or corner fractures at the metaphysis of long bones, characteristic of
child abuse. Caused by twisting or pulling forces on extremities. Most common in the
knee, ankle, and elbow. Seen on skeletal survey; may not be visible initially. Highly
specific for non-accidental trauma in infants.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metaphysis} The region of a long bone between the epiphysis (end) and diaphysis (shaft), containing
the epiphyseal growth plate (physis) in children—the zone of active cartilage growth
(endochondral ossification).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metaplasia} A reversible adaptive change in which one differentiated adult cell type is replaced by
another, representing the body's attempt to better withstand a chronic stress or
irritation. The process involves reprogramming of tissue stem cells to differentiate
along a different lineage that is more resilient to the new environment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastasectomy} Surgical removal of one or more metastases, which may be curative in selected
oligometastatic disease, e.g., colorectal liver and lung metastases and sarcoma
pulmonary metastases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastasis} Metastasis is spread of malignant cells from a primary tumor to noncontiguous tissues through lymphatic, blood, or body-cavity routes, forming secondary tumors.
hematogenous, direct extension, transcoelomic. Common sites: lungs, liver, bone, brain.
Distant metastasis indicates Stage IV disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastasis of colorectal cancer} Spread of colorectal malignancy to distant organs via lymphatic, hematogenous, or
peritoneal routes, most commonly involving the liver, lungs, and peritoneum. The liver
is the first site of metastasis due to portal venous drainage, and hepatic metastases
may be resectable with curative intent.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastasis-Free Survival} The time from treatment until the development of distant metastasis or death; an
important endpoint in adjuvant therapy trials.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastatic adenocarcinoma} An adenocarcinoma that has spread from its primary site to distant organs, commonly the
liver, lungs, bones, or brain. The prognosis and treatment depend on the primary tumor
origin and the extent of metastatic disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastatic Calcification} The deposition of calcium salts in otherwise normal tissues due to systemic
hypercalcemia, where elevated serum calcium levels exceed the tissue ability to maintain
calcium homeostasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastatic Cascade} Sequence of events in metastasis: local invasion, intravasation, circulation,
extravasation, colonization. Many cells die during process.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastatic colon cancer} Advanced colonic malignancy that has spread beyond the bowel to distant sites, most
commonly the liver, lungs, and peritoneum, via lymphatic and hematogenous dissemination.
Staging uses CT of chest, abdomen, and pelvis, and PET-CT may identify occult
metastases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastatic inguinal lymphadenopathy} Enlargement of inguinal lymph nodes due to metastatic cancer cells, often from primary
tumors of the lower extremities, pelvis, or anorectal region. It may indicate advanced
disease requiring systemic therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastatic lung cancer} Lung cancer that has spread beyond the lungs to other organs, such as the brain, bones,
liver, or adrenal glands. It represents advanced stage disease with limited treatment
options.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metatarsals} Five long bones (I-V) forming the forefoot. Each has base, shaft, and head. First is
shortest/thickest, bearing approximately one-third of forefoot load. Stress fractures
commonly affect the second and third (march fractures). Morton's neuroma typically
affects the nerve between 3rd/4th metatarsal heads.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metered-Dose Inhaler} A pressurized inhaler delivering a metered dose of medication. Requires proper technique
