for effective delivery. Spacer devices improve drug delivery and reduce oropharyngeal
deposition. Most common delivery device for asthma and COPD maintenance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metformin in Prediabetes} Metformin may reduce progression from prediabetes to type 2 diabetes, especially in people at
high risk. Its use depends on age, body size, prior gestational diabetes, kidney function,
other conditions, and patient preference; lifestyle intervention remains important.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Methacholine Challenge Test} A bronchoprovocation test used when asthma is suspected but routine spirometry is nondiagnostic.
Inhaled methacholine may cause bronchoconstriction, and spirometry is repeated after
increasing doses. The result must be interpreted with symptoms, treatment, and other
test findings because the test is not specific for asthma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Methanol Poisoning} Toxic ingestion or exposure to methanol, whose metabolites cause high-anion-gap
metabolic acidosis and visual or neurologic injury. Treatment requires urgent toxicology
consultation, antidotal fomepizole or selected ethanol therapy, correction of acidosis,
and hemodialysis in severe cases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Methemoglobinemia} Methemoglobinemia is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
delivery and causing functional anemia. Cyanosis, chocolate-brown blood, headache,
dyspnea, neurologic symptoms, and cardiovascular compromise may occur depending on level
and comorbidity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus} Staphylococcus aureus resistant to beta-lactam antibiotics through acquisition of the
mecA gene encoding penicillin-binding protein 2a (PBP2a). MRSA is a major cause of
healthcare-associated and community-associated infections. Treatment options include
vancomycin, daptomycin, linezolid, and trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Methotrexate in Dermatology} An immunomodulatory antimetabolite used in selected inflammatory and autoimmune skin
diseases, including psoriasis, eczema, and some connective-tissue disorders. Treatment
requires attention to liver disease, blood counts, infection risk, drug interactions,
and strict pregnancy precautions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Methylation} Addition of methyl group to DNA (cytosine) or histones. DNA methylation at CpG islands
typically silences genes. Hypomethylation associated with cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Methylene Blue} An oxidizing-reducing agent used primarily to treat clinically significant
methemoglobinemia by facilitating conversion of methemoglobin to functional hemoglobin.
It may be ineffective or hazardous in glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase deficiency and
can interact with serotonergic drugs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Methylmalonic Acid Level} A blood or urine measurement used to assess functional vitamin B12 status. Elevated
methylmalonic acid supports vitamin B12 deficiency, particularly when the serum vitamin
B12 concentration is borderline. Renal impairment can also increase the level and should
be considered during interpretation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metoclopramide} A dopamine (D2) receptor antagonist and 5-HT3 antagonist used as a prokinetic antiemetic
for gastroparesis, chemotherapy-induced and postoperative nausea, and GERD (facilitates
gastric emptying).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metronidazole} A nitroimidazole antibiotic and antiprotozoal agent that is selectively toxic to
anaerobic organisms and certain parasites. The drug is activated by reduction of its
nitro group within anaerobic cells, generating toxic free radicals that damage DNA.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metronidazole-associated encephalopathy} A rare complication of prolonged or high-dose metronidazole therapy, causing cerebellar
dysfunction (ataxia, dysarthria, nystagmus) and sometimes confusion or seizures. MRI may
show T2-hyperintense lesions in the cerebellar dentate nuclei, corpus callosum splenium,
and brainstem. Symptoms typically resolve with drug discontinuation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metrorrhagia} Uterine bleeding occurring at irregular times, including between expected menstrual periods. Causes include hormonal disorders,
structural lesions, pregnancy-related conditions, medicines, and disease of the cervix
or uterus; evaluation depends on age, pregnancy status, symptoms, and risk factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MIBG Scan} Nuclear medicine imaging using I-123 or In-111 MIBG. Detects pheochromocytoma,
neuroblastoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micafungin} An echinocandin antifungal agent used in the treatment and prophylaxis of invasive
candidiasis and esophageal candidiasis. Micafungin inhibits beta-(1,3)-D-glucan
synthase, disrupting fungal cell wall synthesis and leading to osmotic instability and
cell death.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Michaelis-Menten Kinetics} The mathematical model describing the rate of enzyme-catalyzed reactions as a function
of substrate concentration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microalbuminuria} Excretion of 30 to 300 mg of albumin daily undetectable by standard dipstick. Earliest
marker of diabetic nephropathy and cardiovascular risk predictor. Detected by urine
albumin-to-creatinine ratio on spot specimen. Normal less than 30 mg/g creatinine.
Signals early glomerular damage in diabetes warranting RAAS blockade initiation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microangiopathic hemolysis} Red-cell fragmentation and intravascular hemolysis caused by passage through abnormal
small vessels, producing schistocytes, elevated LDH, and reduced haptoglobin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microarray} Technology measuring expression of thousands of genes simultaneously or detecting copy
number variations. Gene expression microarrays: compare expression patterns. SNP arrays:
detect genetic variations. Comparative genomic hybridization (CGH): detect
deletions/duplications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microbiome} The collection of microorganisms (bacteria, archaea, fungi, viruses—the microbiota) and
their genetic material inhabiting a host's body surfaces, particularly the
gastrointestinal tract.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microcephaly} Microcephaly is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micrognathia} Micrognathia is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micrograms} A unit of mass equal to one millionth of a gram, written as \(\mu\mathrm{g}\).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micrometastasis} A small deposit of cancer cells that has spread from the primary tumor but is not
detectable clinically or on standard imaging, e.g., isolated tumor cells in a sentinel
lymph node; relevant to staging and prognosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micromolar} A concentration unit equal to one millionth of a mole per liter, written as \(\mu\mathrm{mol}/L\).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micronutrients} Vitamins and minerals required in small amounts for enzymatic, hormonal, structural,
hematologic, neurologic, and immune functions. They include fat-soluble vitamins (A, D,
E, and K), water-soluble vitamins, and essential minerals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microphthalmia} A congenitally small eye, ranging from mild (reduced axial length) to severe with
structural anomalies. Associated with coloboma, congenital cataract, and genetic
syndromes; can cause vision loss and requires optical correction, amblyopia therapy, and
sometimes socket rehabilitation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MicroRNA} Small non-coding RNA regulating gene expression. Post-transcriptional regulation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microsatellite Instability} A molecular pattern caused by deficient DNA mismatch-repair function, resulting in
length changes in short repetitive DNA sequences. MSI-high tumors may have increased
mutation burden and can be eligible for particular immunotherapies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microscope} Magnifies small structures. Types: light, electron, fluorescent. Essential for
histology, hematology, microbiology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microscopic Colitis} Umbrella term encompassing both collagenous colitis and lymphocytic colitis which share
the clinical presentation of chronic watery non-bloody diarrhea and the
pathophysiological feature of colonic mucosal inflammation. Endoscopic appearance is
typically normal necessitating random biopsies for diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microscopic Examination} The pathologic evaluation of tissue or cells under the light or electron microscope,
complementing the gross examination to form the complete morphologic diagnosis. Routine
light microscopy uses hematoxylin and eosin sections to assess architecture, cellular
morphology, mitotic activity, and relation to margins.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microscopic Polyangiitis} A necrotizing vasculitis affecting small blood vessels (capillaries, venules,
arterioles) with few or no immune deposits (pauci-immune). Part of the ANCA-associated
vasculitis group. Strongly associated with MPO-ANCA (p-ANCA, 80\%).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microwave Ablation} A procedure that uses microwave energy to heat and destroy selected tissue, usually under imaging guidance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micturition} The process of urination, coordinated by the pontine micturition center, parasympathetic
(pelvic nerve, S2-S4) contraction of the detrusor muscle, and relaxation of the internal
(involuntary) and external (voluntary, pudendal nerve) urethral sphincters.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mid-Systolic Click} A sharp, high-pitched sound occurring in mid-to-late systole, associated with mitral
valve prolapse. The click represents sudden tensing of the elongated chordae tendineae
as the prolapsing leaflet billows into the left atrium. It may be followed by a late
systolic murmur of mitral regurgitation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mid-Upper-Arm Circumference} The circumference of the upper arm measured at the midpoint between the acromion and
olecranon processes. It is a practical anthropometric measure of muscle and fat stores,
particularly useful when body weight or height cannot be measured reliably.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Midaxillary Line} An imaginary vertical line on the side of the body passing through the apex of the
axilla (armpit).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Midbrain} Most superior brainstem segment connecting pons to diencephalon. Contains cerebral
peduncles (corticospinal/corticobulbar tracts), substantia nigra (dopaminergic neurons
degenerated in Parkinson's), red nucleus (rubrospinal tract for coordination), and
periaqueductal gray (pain modulation).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Midclavicular Line} An imaginary vertical line on the front of the body passing through the midpoint of the
clavicle (collarbone).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Middle Ear} An air-filled space between the eardrum and inner ear that contains the malleus, incus, and stapes. These ossicles transmit
sound vibrations to the inner ear, and the auditory tube helps equalize middle-ear
pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Middle East Respiratory Syndrome} A severe respiratory infection caused by Middle East respiratory syndrome coronavirus,
usually acquired through close contact with infected camels or people. It can cause
pneumonia, respiratory failure, and renal or multiorgan complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Middle Lobe Syndrome} Chronic or recurrent atelectasis and infection of the right middle lobe due to bronchial
obstruction. The long, narrow right middle lobe bronchus is susceptible to compression
by lymph nodes, tumors, or mucous plugging. It may be caused by bronchiectasis,
malignancy, or granulomatous disease. Treatment depends on the underlying cause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Midtrimester} The middle third of pregnancy, from 14 to 28 weeks gestation. The midtrimester is when
many women feel their best: nausea and fatigue of the first trimester resolve, and the
physical discomforts of the third trimester have not yet developed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Migraine} Migraine is a neurologic disorder causing recurrent attacks of moderate or severe headache with nausea, light or sound sensitivity, and sometimes aura or focal neurologic symptoms.
typically unilateral, pulsating headache lasting 4-72 hours, associated with nausea,
vomiting, photophobia, and phonophobia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Migraine With Aura} A primary headache disorder characterized by recurrent attacks of fully reversible
visual, sensory, or speech symptoms that develop gradually over at least five minutes
and last no more than sixty minutes, followed by headache with the characteristics of
migraine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Migraine Without Aura} A primary headache disorder characterized by recurrent attacks of moderate to severe,
pulsating, unilateral headache lasting four to seventy-two hours, associated with
nausea, vomiting, photophobia, and phonophobia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Migrating Motor Complex} Cyclical patterns of intense gastrointestinal motility occurring during fasting
(interdigestive period), sweeping residual food, bacteria, and debris from the stomach
through the small intestine to the colon.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mild Cognitive Impairment} A transitional cognitive state between normal aging and dementia, characterized by
subjective cognitive complaints confirmed by objective cognitive impairment that is not
severe enough to significantly interfere with daily activities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Milia} Milia is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Miliaria crystallina} A benign, asymptomatic eruption of small, clear, superficial vesicles caused by
obstruction of eccrine sweat ducts in the stratum corneum, occurring in hot, humid
environments or during high fevers. The vesicles resemble dewdrops on the skin and
rupture easily, leaving fine scales.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Miliary TB} Hematogenous dissemination of M. tuberculosis causing innumerable 1--3 mm granulomas
throughout lungs and other organs. CXR shows ``millet seed'' pattern. More common in
immunocompromised patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Miliary Tuberculosis} Disseminated tuberculosis caused by hematogenous spread of Mycobacterium tuberculosis,
producing numerous tiny granulomatous lesions in the lungs and potentially multiple
organs. Symptoms may include prolonged fever, weight loss, night sweats, cough, and
organ-specific manifestations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Milk-Alkali Syndrome} Hypercalcemia, metabolic alkalosis, and renal dysfunction caused by excessive intake of
calcium and absorbable alkali. It may occur with high-dose calcium supplements or
antacid use.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Milliequivalent} A chemical-equivalent unit used to express the amount of charged substances such as electrolytes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Millimolar} A concentration unit equal to one thousandth of a mole per liter, written as mmol/L.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Milrinone} A phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitor with positive inotropic and vasodilatory (inodilator)
actions, increasing cAMP in cardiac and vascular smooth muscle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Milroy Disease} An inherited primary lymphedema caused most often by pathogenic variants affecting
VEGFR3 signaling. It typically presents with chronic swelling of the lower limbs from
birth or early infancy and may be associated with papillomatosis, hydrocele, or
recurrent skin infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mindful Eating} Paying attention to hunger cues, eating slowly, savoring food. May reduce overeating,
improve digestion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mineralocorticoid Receptor} An intracellular steroid receptor activated primarily by aldosterone in the kidney,
colon, sweat glands, and other tissues. Its activation increases sodium reabsorption and
potassium and hydrogen secretion and contributes to blood-pressure regulation and tissue
remodeling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mineralocorticoid Receptor Antagonist} A drug that blocks aldosterone receptors, promoting natriuresis while limiting potassium
loss and adverse cardiac remodeling. Spironolactone and eplerenone improve outcomes in
selected heart-failure patients; hyperkalemia and renal dysfunction require monitoring.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mini-Mental State Examination} A brief structured cognitive screening instrument assessing orientation, registration,
attention, calculation, recall, language, and visuospatial ability. Scores are affected
by education, language, sensory impairment, culture, and delirium and do not establish a
dementia diagnosis alone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Minimal Change Disease} A cause of nephrotic syndrome, especially in children, in which the glomeruli appear
nearly normal by light microscopy but podocyte foot processes are effaced on electron microscopy. It causes marked
protein loss in the urine and edema.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Minimal Residual Disease} The small number of malignant cells that remain after treatment and are below detection
by conventional clinical examination or routine microscopy. Sensitive flow-cytometric,
molecular, or imaging methods may detect MRD and refine relapse-risk assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Minimum Alveolar Concentration} The alveolar concentration of an inhaled anesthetic that prevents
movement in half of people exposed to a standardized painful surgical stimulus. It is used to compare the potency of
volatile anesthetics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Minimum Bactericidal Concentration} The lowest concentration of an antimicrobial that kills a defined proportion of the
original bacterial inoculum, commonly demonstrated by subculture without growth. MBC is
distinct from MIC and is not routinely required for every infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Minimum Effect Concentration} The lowest concentration of a drug in the blood or at its site of action that produces
a defined measurable effect.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Minimum inhibitory concentration} The lowest concentration of an antimicrobial that prevents visible growth of a microorganism
under standardized laboratory conditions. It helps characterize susceptibility, but results
must be interpreted with the organism, specimen, assay, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Minimum Lethal Concentration} The lowest concentration of a substance associated with death in a defined test
population or experimental setting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Minute Ventilation} The total volume of air breathed in one minute (tidal volume x respiratory rate). Normal
is 6--8 L/min. Elevated in increased metabolic demand, pain, anxiety, and sepsis.
Reduced in neuromuscular disease and sedation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Miosis} Constriction of the pupil, produced mainly by parasympathetic activity through the oculomotor nerve and
sphincter pupillae muscle. It may be physiologic or caused by medicines, toxins, neurologic
disease, or ocular conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MIRD Schema} Medical Internal Radiation Dose. Method for calculating radiation absorbed doses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mirizzi Syndrome} Condition where a gallstone impacted in the cystic duct or Hartmann pouch extrinsic
compression of the common hepatic duct causing obstructive jaundice. Classified into
four types based on the degree of erosion and fistula formation. Type one is extrinsic
compression without fistula.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mirror Therapy} Mirror reflection of unaffected limb movement creates visual illusion of affected limb
moving. Reduces phantom limb pain, improves stroke recovery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Miscarriage} Miscarriage is spontaneous loss of a pregnancy before fetal viability, with symptoms ranging from bleeding and cramping to passage of tissue or no symptoms on ultrasound.
percent of clinically recognized pregnancies. The majority (80 percent) occur in the
first trimester, with risk decreasing significantly after visualization of fetal cardiac
activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mismatch Repair Deficiency} Loss of DNA mismatch-repair function that produces microsatellite instability and a high
tumor mutational burden. Seen in Lynch syndrome and some sporadic colorectal,
endometrial, and other cancers, and associated with responsiveness to immune checkpoint
inhibitors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Misoprostol (Cytotec)} A synthetic prostaglandin analogue used for selected gynecologic and obstetric indications, including cervical ripening,
labor induction, and medical management of some pregnancies. It can cause uterine
contractions, bleeding, gastrointestinal effects, or uterine tachysystole; use depends on
the clinical setting and gestational age.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Cytotec

\medterm{Missense Variant} A single-nucleotide substitution that changes one amino acid in a protein. Its clinical
effect ranges from benign to pathogenic and must be assessed using molecular,
functional, population, and clinical evidence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitochondria} Double-membrane organelles known as the "powerhouse of the cell," generating ATP through
oxidative phosphorylation. Outer membrane is permeable; inner membrane is highly folded
(cristae) and contains the electron transport chain and ATP synthase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitochondrial Diabetes} Diabetes caused by mutations in mitochondrial DNA particularly the m.3243A>G mutation in
the MT-TL1 gene. Maternally inherited. Often associated with sensorineural hearing loss
termed maternally inherited diabetes and deafness. Patients are typically lean with
impaired insulin secretion. Diagnosis requires genetic testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitochondrial Disease} A group of genetic disorders caused by impaired mitochondrial energy production. Symptoms vary and
may affect the brain, muscles, nerves, heart, eyes, or multiple organs; onset and severity can
differ widely even within one family.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitochondrial Inheritance} Inheritance of mitochondrial DNA, which is transmitted predominantly through the egg and therefore usually
from the mother. Examples include Leber hereditary optic neuropathy and MELAS. Heteroplasmy
means that mutant and normal mitochondrial DNA coexist in a cell.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitosis} Cell division that produces two genetically similar daughter cells with the same chromosome number as the parent
cell.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitotic Figure} A cell undergoing nuclear division that can be identified microscopically. Mitotic count
contributes to assessment of proliferative activity and tumor grade, although counting
method and area must be standardized.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MitraClip} A transcatheter device that creates a double orifice in the mitral valve by clipping the
anterior and posterior leaflets together, reducing mitral regurgitation. It is indicated
for patients with significant (3+ or 4+) mitral regurgitation who are at high risk for
surgical mitral valve repair.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitral Regurgitation} Incomplete closure of the mitral valve allowing blood to flow backward from the left
ventricle to the left atrium during systole. Causes include mitral valve prolapse,
papillary muscle dysfunction, rheumatic disease, and ischemic heart disease. Murmur:
pansystolic (holosystolic) blowing murmur at the apex radiating to the axilla.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitral Stenosis} Narrowing of the mitral valve opening, obstructing left atrial to left ventricular blood
flow. Most commonly caused by rheumatic heart disease. Symptoms include dyspnea,
fatigue, and atrial fibrillation. Murmur: low-pitched, rumbling mid-diastolic murmur at
the apex with opening snap.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitral Stenosis Murmur} Low-pitched, rumbling mid-diastolic murmur at the apex, best heard with the bell of the
stethoscope in the left lateral decubitus position. Accompanied by an opening snap
(early diastolic high-pitched click) and loud S1 in pliable valves. Severe MS may show a
diastolic murmur without a preceding S2 gap.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitral Valve} Left atrioventricular (bicuspid) valve with anterior and posterior cusps supported by
chordae tendineae and papillary muscles. Anterior cusp is larger and proximate to the
aortic valve. Mitral valve prolapse is the most common valvular abnormality. Mitral
stenosis (most commonly rheumatic) and regurgitation are significant.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitral Valve Prolapse} A valvular heart disease where the mitral valve leaflets prolapse into the left atrium
during systole. Most patients are asymptomatic; some develop palpitations, atypical
chest pain, or dyspnea. Diagnosis by echocardiography. Complications include mitral
regurgitation, infective endocarditis, and arrhythmias.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitral-valve repair} A surgical or transcatheter procedure to restore mitral valve competence without
replacement, typically for regurgitation from prolapse, chordal rupture, or ischemic
dysfunction. Repair preserves native valve tissue, maintains left ventricular function,
and avoids lifelong anticoagulation required by mechanical prostheses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mixed Connective Tissue Disease} An overlap syndrome with features of systemic lupus erythematosus, systemic sclerosis,
and polymyositis/dermatomyositis. Highly associated with anti-U1 RNP antibodies.
Presents with Raynaud phenomenon (nearly universal), puffy hands, arthritis, myositis,
esophageal dysmotility, and serositis. Renal disease is less common than in SLE.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mixed Hearing Loss} A combination of both conductive and sensorineural hearing loss occurring in the same
ear, resulting in a hearing deficit that involves impaired sound transmission through
the outer or middle ear in addition to cochlear or neural dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mixed Urinary Incontinence} Involuntary leakage of urine, significantly affecting psychological, social, and
physical well-being. See urinary incontinence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MMR Vaccine} A live attenuated vaccine against measles, mumps, and rubella. It is given according to the
immunization schedule and is generally avoided during pregnancy and in people with certain severe immune deficiencies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mobile genetic elements} Genetic elements that can move within and between genomes. These include plasmids,
transposons, and integrons. Mobile genetic elements can carry antibiotic resistance
genes and virulence factors. They facilitate horizontal gene transfer and contribute to
the rapid evolution of bacteria.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mobile Health (mHealth)} Use of mobile devices for health. Examples: health apps, text message reminders,
wearable devices.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
mHealth

\medterm{Modafinil} A wakefulness-promoting agent (mechanism not fully understood, likely dopaminergic) used
for narcolepsy, obstructive sleep apnea residual sleepiness, and shift work sleep
disorder; used off-label for fatigue and ADHD. Less abuse potential and fewer
sympathomimetic effects than amphetamines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Modified Biophysical Profile (Modified BPP)} A simplified antepartum test combining the non-stress test (NST) with the amniotic fluid
index (AFI). The NST assesses acute fetal status (heart rate reactivity over 20-40
minutes) and the AFI assesses chronic uteroplacental function (oligohydramnios suggests
chronic hypoxia or placental insufficiency).



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Modified BPP

\medterm{Mohs Surgery} A micrographically controlled technique in which skin cancer is excised in thin layers
and examined margin-by-margin, achieving high cure rates for basal cell and squamous
cell carcinoma while sparing normal tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Molar Pregnancy} A type of gestational trophoblastic disease characterized by abnormal proliferation of
trophoblastic tissue. See Hydatidiform Mole.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mole} Nevus: a benign proliferation of melanocytes, the most common skin lesion; also used for
other nevi (moles are melanocytic nevi). Types: junctional (flat, brown—childhood),
compound (slightly raised), intradermal (dome-shaped, flesh/pigmented).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Molecular Pathology} The investigation of disease using nucleic-acid, genomic, epigenomic, or molecular
protein alterations in cells and tissues. It may establish diagnosis, estimate
prognosis, identify inherited risk, or select targeted therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Molecular Testing} Analysis of DNA, RNA, or other molecular features to identify variants, fusions, expression patterns, or infectious agents for diagnosis, prognosis, or treatment selection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Molecular Weight} Mass of molecule. Important for dosing, toxicity calculations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Molluscum Contagiosum} A viral skin infection caused by a poxvirus, presenting as firm, dome-shaped,
umbilicated papules with central dimpling, 2 to 5 mm in diameter. It is transmitted by
direct skin contact, autoinoculation, and sexual contact in adults. Immunocompetent
hosts clear lesions spontaneously within 6 to 12 months.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Molybdenum-99} Parent isotope of Tc-99m, half-life 66 hours. Produced in nuclear reactors. Generator
systems provide Tc-99m for 1 week.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mondor’s disease} Superficial thrombophlebitis of the anterior chest wall veins, typically the lateral
thoracic, thoracoepigastric, or superior epigastric veins. Presents as a tender,
cord-like subcutaneous structure with skin retraction. Usually self-limited; associated
with breast surgery, trauma, or hypercoagulable states.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Monitored Anesthesia Care} A distinct service where an anesthesia professional provides premedication,
administration of analgesics and/or sedatives, continuous monitoring of the patient's
cardiorespiratory status, and intervention for any emergent or unavoidable complications
that may arise during a diagnostic or therapeutic procedure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Monitoring Test} Test to track disease progression or treatment response.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Monoamine Oxidase Inhibitors} Antidepressants that inhibit monoamine oxidase, the enzyme responsible for breaking down
serotonin, norepinephrine, and dopamine. MAOIs include phenelzine, tranylcypromine,
isocarboxazid, and selegiline (transdermal). They are effective for atypical depression
and treatment-resistant depression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Monoclonal Antibody} An antibody produced by a single cloned B-cell line, specific for one epitope, generated
by hybridoma technology or recombinant methods (name suffix -mab).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Monocyte} A circulating leukocyte (2-8 percent) that differentiates into macrophages and dendritic
cells in tissues. Monocytes phagocytose pathogens, present antigens, and secrete
cytokines (TNF, IL-1, IL-6); participate in innate immunity, inflammation, and tissue
repair. Subsets differ in function (classical, nonclassical, intermediate).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mononucleosis} Mononucleosis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations. adolescents and young adults. Classic triad: fever, pharyngitis/exudative tonsillitis, and cervical lymphadenopathy. Associated with splenomegaly, fatigue, and atypical lymphocytosis.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Monosodium urate} The crystalline salt that precipitates in tissues when serum uric acid levels exceed the
saturation point, forming needle-shaped, negatively birefringent crystals under
polarized light microscopy. It is the pathological hallmark of gout, depositing in
joints, soft tissues, and kidneys.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Monosomy} Presence of one copy of a chromosome instead of the usual two. Complete autosomal monosomies are generally
not compatible with life; monosomy X and some mosaic or structural forms can survive and
have variable features.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Monospot (EBV Heterophile Antibody)} A rapid blood test that detects heterophile antibodies associated with infectious mononucleosis. It may be
negative early in illness or in young children and does not directly detect Epstein-Barr
virus; specific antibody testing may be needed.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
EBV Heterophile Antibody

\medterm{Monounsaturated Fat} A fat whose fatty acids contain one carbon-carbon double bond. It occurs in foods such as olive oil, avocados, nuts, and some seeds.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Montevideo Units} A quantitative measure of uterine contraction intensity, calculated by summing the peak
contraction pressures (in mm Hg) minus the resting tone for each contraction in a
10-minute window. Adequate labor: \(\geq 200\) MVU (spontaneous) or \(\geq 250\) MVU (augmented).
Measured with an intrauterine pressure catheter (IUPC).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Montgomery Tubercles} Small, raised sebaceous glands visible on the areola of the breast during pregnancy and
lactation. They appear as small bumps surrounding the nipple and secrete an oily
substance that lubricates the nipple and areola, protecting against infection. Their
prominence increases during pregnancy under hormonal influence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Montreal Cognitive Assessment} A brief cognitive screening instrument with greater emphasis on executive function,
visuospatial ability, attention, memory, language, and orientation than many shorter
screens. Interpretation requires consideration of education, language, sensory deficits,
and acute illness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mood Stabilizers} Medications used to treat mood episodes in bipolar disorder and prevent recurrence. The
primary mood stabilizers are lithium, valproate, carbamazepine, and lamotrigine. Each
has a different mechanism and side effect profile. Lithium is the only agent with
demonstrated anti-suicide properties.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Morbid Obesity} Severe obesity defined clinically as BMI \(\geq 40\) kg/m\(^2\) or BMI \(\geq 35\) kg/m\(^2\) with
obesity-related comorbidities (type 2 diabetes, hypertension, obstructive sleep apnoea,
non-alcoholic fatty liver disease, osteoarthritis, cardiovascular disease).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Morbidity} Illness, disease, or disability in an individual or population. Morbidity measures may describe prevalence, incidence, complications, or functional effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Morgagni Hernia} A congenital diaphragmatic hernia occurring through the anterior retrosternal foramen of
Morgagni, resulting from incomplete fusion of parts of the diaphragm. Abdominal contents
may move into the chest and cause respiratory or gastrointestinal symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Morning Sickness} Nausea and vomiting of pregnancy (NVP): nausea (and sometimes vomiting) commonly in the
first trimester (6-12 weeks), typically resolving by 14-16 weeks; multifactorial (hCG,
estrogen, progesterone, odor sensitivity).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Moro Reflex} A primitive reflex present from birth to 3-6 months, elicited by suddenly startling the
infant (loud noise, head drop). The infant extends both arms outward with fingers
spread, then draws arms inward as if embracing. Absence at birth suggests central
nervous system injury or brachial plexus palsy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mortality} Death rate in population. Elderly mortality: higher rates, common causes: cardiovascular
disease, cancer, dementia, respiratory disease, fall-related injuries. Life expectancy
varies by sex, race, socioeconomic status.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mortality Rate} Deaths from disease in population. Crude: total deaths / total population. Specific:
disease-specific deaths / population at risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Morton Neuroma} A perineural fibrosis of the plantar digital nerve, most commonly between the third and
fourth metatarsal heads. It presents as burning pain, numbness, and a feeling of walking
on a pebble. It is not a true neuroma but a mechanical entrapment neuropathy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mosaicism} Mosaicism is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motion Analysis} Assessment of movement patterns. 3D gait analysis, video analysis. Identifies
abnormalities, guides treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motion Artifact} Image distortion or blurring caused by movement of the patient, organs, or equipment during image acquisition.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motion Correction} Correction for patient motion during acquisition. Respiratory gating, navigation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motion Sickness} Nausea, dizziness, sweating, or vomiting caused by sensory mismatch during travel or other motion. It can occur in cars,
boats, aircraft, amusement rides, or virtual-reality environments and usually improves when motion stops.
Severe or persistent symptoms require assessment for other causes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motivational Interviewing} A client-centered, directive counseling method for enhancing intrinsic motivation to
change by exploring and resolving ambivalence. Developed by Miller and Rollnick, it uses
the spirit of partnership, acceptance, compassion, and evocation. Core skills include
open-ended questions, affirmations, reflections, and summaries.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motor Cortex} The frontal-lobe regions that plan and initiate voluntary movement. The primary motor
cortex lies in the precentral gyrus and controls the opposite side of the body in an
orderly somatotopic map.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motor Learning} A relatively lasting change in the ability to perform a motor skill resulting from
practice, experience, feedback, and adaptation. Rehabilitation uses task-specific
repetition and meaningful practice to promote motor learning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Motor Unit} A single alpha motor neuron and all the skeletal muscle fibers it innervates. The number
of muscle fibers per motor unit determines precision of control: small motor units (few
fibers, e.g., extraocular and hand muscles) provide fine movement; large motor units
(hundreds to thousands of fibers, e.g., gastrocnemius) provide gross power.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mouth} The oral cavity that serves as the entry point of the digestive tract, used for eating,
breathing, and speaking.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mouth Ulcers} Open sores affecting the oral mucosa, commonly caused by aphthous ulcers, trauma,
infection, irritation, or systemic inflammatory disease. Most resolve within two weeks;
persistent, recurrent, unusually large, or indurated ulcers require evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mouthwash} A liquid rinsed around the mouth and then expelled to freshen breath, deliver an active ingredient, or reduce selected oral microorganisms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Movement Disorder} A neurologic condition causing excessive, reduced, or otherwise abnormal movement.
Hyperkinetic disorders include tremor, dystonia, chorea, myoclonus, tics, and
dyskinesia; hypokinetic disorders include bradykinesia and parkinsonism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Moyamoya Disease} A progressive occlusive arteriopathy of the terminal internal carotid arteries and their
proximal branches, with development of fragile collateral vessels at the brain base. It
can cause transient ischemic attacks, strokes, seizures, headaches, or cognitive
impairment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mpox} Mpox is a zoonotic infection caused by monkeypox virus, producing fever, lymph-node enlargement, and a characteristic rash or lesions that can occur on skin, mouth, or genital areas.
nodes, and a distinctive rash that may include deep-seated vesicles or pustules.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mpox (monkeypox)} A zoonotic viral disease caused by the monkeypox virus, an orthopoxvirus in the same
genus as smallpox (variola) and vaccinia. The virus has two clades: clade I (formerly
Congo Basin, Central African) with higher virulence and mortality (up to 10 percent),
and clade II (formerly West African) with lower mortality (less than 1 percent).



\textbf{Synonyms}:
monkeypox

\medterm{MR Angiography} Vascular imaging using MRI without ionizing radiation. Time-of-flight (TOF) or
contrast-enhanced (CE-MRA). Used for cerebral aneurysms, renal artery stenosis,
peripheral arterial disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MR Enterography} MRI of the small bowel after oral contrast administration. Superior soft tissue contrast
for evaluating Crohn disease activity, fistulas, and abscesses without ionizing
radiation. Demonstrates mural thickening, edema, enhancement patterns, and
extraintestinal complications. Diffusion-weighted imaging helps assess disease activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MR Neurography} Magnetic resonance imaging optimized to show peripheral nerves and abnormalities such as swelling, compression, or injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MRCP (Magnetic Resonance Cholangiopancreatography)} Non-invasive MRI of the biliary and pancreatic ducts using heavily T2-weighted sequences
that highlight static fluid. Used for: choledocholithiasis (CBD stones), biliary
strictures, primary sclerosing cholangitis, pancreatic duct anatomy, and congenital
biliary anomalies.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Magnetic Resonance Cholangiopancreatography

\medterm{MRI} MRI examines mri; interpretation depends on the specimen or body region, test method, reference range, and clinical question.
images of body tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MRI (Magnetic Resonance Imaging)} Imaging using strong magnetic fields and radiofrequency pulses to generate detailed
cross-sectional images. No ionizing radiation. Superior soft-tissue contrast compared to
CT.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Magnetic Resonance Imaging

\medterm{MRI Brain} Excellent for brain pathology: tumors, stroke, demyelination, infection, degeneration.
Sequences: T1, T2, FLAIR, DWI, SWI, contrast-enhanced. DWI detects acute ischemia within
minutes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MRI Contrast} A contrast agent administered during magnetic resonance imaging to improve visibility of selected tissues or abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MRI Joint} Evaluation of musculoskeletal structures: cartilage, ligaments, tendons, menisci,
labrum. Knee: ACL, meniscus tears. Shoulder: rotator cuff, labrum. Hip: labral tears,
FAI.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MRI Sequences} T1-weighted: anatomy, fat bright. T2-weighted: pathology, fluid bright. FLAIR: suppresses CSF, lesions bright. DWI: restricted diffusion bright (acute stroke, abscess). STIR: fat suppression, fluid bright.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mRNA Vaccine} A vaccine delivering messenger RNA encoding a pathogen antigen that host cells translate
into protein, triggering an immune response. Approved for COVID-19 (Pfizer-BioNTech,
Moderna) and RSV; under study for influenza, cancer, and other infections.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MRSA} Methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus, resistant to beta-lactam antibiotics
through acquisition of the mecA gene encoding PBP2a. MRSA is a major cause of
healthcare-associated and community-associated infections. Treatment options include
vancomycin, daptomycin, and linezolid.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MRSA decolonization} Measures intended to reduce MRSA carriage on the skin or in the nose. Protocols may include topical
agents and antiseptic bathing, but the indication, regimen, and duration depend on the
person's risk, infection history, setting, and local guidance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MRV (Magnetic Resonance Venography)} MRI technique to visualize veins, using time-of-flight (TOF) or contrast-enhanced
sequences. Indications: cerebral venous sinus thrombosis, dural sinus stenosis,
abdominal/pelvic venous thrombosis, portal vein thrombosis, and renal vein thrombosis.
Differentiates acute vs. chronic thrombus.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Magnetic Resonance Venography

\medterm{mTOR} Mammalian target of rapamycin. Controls cell growth, proliferation. Inhibited by
rapamycin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mucocele} Mucus extravasation cyst of minor salivary gland. Lower lip most common. Treatment:
excision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mucolytic Therapy} Medications that reduce mucus viscosity and elasticity, facilitating clearance.
N-acetylcysteine (NAC) breaks disulfide bonds in mucus glycoproteins. Dornase alfa
(Pulmozyme) cleaves extracellular DNA in CF sputum. Hypertonic saline aerosol draws
water into the airway surface liquid.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mucormycosis} Mucormycosis is an invasive infection by Mucorales fungi, typically affecting immunocompromised people and causing rapidly destructive sinus, orbital, pulmonary, cutaneous, or disseminated disease.
Mucor, and Rhizomucor species. The infection primarily affects immunocompromised
patients, particularly those with uncontrolled diabetes mellitus and hematologic
malignancies. The fungi invade blood vessels causing thrombosis and tissue infarction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mucosal Irritation} Chemical injury to mucous membranes. Eyes, nose, throat, GI tract. Treatment:
irrigation, supportive care.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mucositis} Inflammation and ulceration of the oral or gastrointestinal mucosa caused by
chemotherapy or radiation, causing pain, dysphagia, and poor intake and predisposing to
local infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mucus Plugging} Accumulation of thick, tenacious mucus within airways causing obstruction. It is common
in asthma, CF, bronchiectasis, and chronic bronchitis. It impairs gas exchange, promotes
infection, and causes atelectasis. Treatment includes airway clearance techniques,
mucolytics, and bronchodilators.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muenke Syndrome} A craniosynostosis syndrome caused by a pathogenic FGFR3 variant. Premature fusion of
one or more skull sutures may produce an abnormal head shape and can be accompanied by
developmental, hearing, limb, or neurological findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MUGA Scan (Multigated Acquisition Scan)} ECG-gated radionuclide imaging using technetium-labeled red blood cells to
measure ventricular ejection fraction and wall motion. It can be used when reproducible
ejection-fraction measurement is needed, although echocardiography and cardiac MRI are also
important alternatives.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Multigated Acquisition Scan

\medterm{Multicystic Dysplastic Kidney} A congenital renal anomaly where one or both kidneys are replaced by multiple
non-communicating cysts of varying sizes, with no functioning renal parenchyma. The most
common cause of an abdominal mass in a newborn (unilateral). The ureter is typically
atretic. The contralateral kidney may have VUR (20\%) or UPJ obstruction (10\%).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multidisciplinary Disaster Victim Identification} The coordinated process of identifying victims of mass-fatality incidents (natural
disasters, transport crashes, terrorist attacks) using multiple disciplines:
fingerprinting, dental records, DNA profiling, forensic anthropology/radiology, and
personal effects, with ante-mortem data collection and comparison.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multidisciplinary Team} Rehabilitation team includes: physiatrist, physical therapist, occupational therapist,
speech-language pathologist, psychologist, social worker, case manager, nursing,
recreation therapist.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multidrug-Resistant TB} TB resistant to at least isoniazid and rifampin. Requires second-line drugs for 18--24
months. Worse outcomes and higher mortality than drug-susceptible TB.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multifocal Atrial Tachycardia} An atrial tachyarrhythmia with at least three distinct P-wave morphologies, irregular
rhythm, and a rate above 100 beats per minute. It is strongly associated with severe
pulmonary disease, hypoxia, and metabolic stress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multimodal Analgesia} Use of analgesic techniques with different mechanisms to improve pain control while
reducing reliance on any single drug, especially opioids. Components may include
acetaminophen, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, regional anesthesia, local
anesthetics, and selected adjuvants.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multimorbidity} The coexistence of two or more chronic health conditions in the same person, without
requiring one to be considered the primary disease. It complicates treatment, increases
polypharmacy risk, and requires coordinated patient-centered care.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiparametric MRI of the Prostate} MRI protocol combining T2-weighted, diffusion-weighted, and dynamic contrast-enhanced
sequences to detect and characterize clinically significant prostate cancer. Findings
are commonly reported using the PI-RADS system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple bacterial abscesses} Numerous circumscribed collections of pus within an organ or tissue, caused by
hematogenous seeding or direct extension of bacterial infection. They appear as
multiple, low-attenuation lesions on imaging, often with peripheral enhancement. Common
sites include the liver, brain, and kidneys.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple biliary hamartomas} Benign, congenital malformations of the intrahepatic bile ducts appearing as multiple,
small, well-defined cystic lesions scattered throughout the liver parenchyma on imaging.
They are typically asymptomatic and discovered incidentally. They must be distinguished
from metastatic disease and Caroli disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia Type 1} An autosomal dominant tumor syndrome caused by MEN1 gene mutation (menin).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia Type 2} An inherited tumor syndrome caused by pathogenic variants in the RET gene.
Type 2A is associated with medullary thyroid carcinoma, pheochromocytoma, and hyperparathyroidism. Type 2B is associated
with medullary thyroid carcinoma, pheochromocytoma, mucosal neuromas, and a marfanoid habitus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2B} An inherited RET-associated syndrome characterized by medullary thyroid
carcinoma, pheochromocytoma, mucosal neuromas, and a marfanoid habitus. The thyroid-cancer risk is high and requires
early specialist genetic and surgical assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple Myeloma} A cancer of plasma cells that usually develops in the bone marrow and often produces a monoclonal immunoglobulin. It can
cause bone lesions, anemia, kidney dysfunction, high calcium, infection, or other organ
damage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple Pregnancy} Gestation with more than one fetus: twins (most common), triplets, higher order. Types:
dizygotic (two eggs, two placentas—fraternal, more common, familial and increased with
IVF) vs. monozygotic (one egg split—identical, sharing determined by timing:
dichorionic-diamniotic, monochorionic-diamniotic, monoamniotic, conjoined).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple Sclerosis} A chronic autoimmune demyelinating disease of the central nervous system, most commonly
presenting in young adults. Characterized by relapsing-remitting episodes of
neurological dysfunction (optic neuritis, transverse myelitis, brainstem syndromes).
Diagnosis: McDonald criteria with MRI dissemination in space and time.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple System Atrophy} A progressive, fatal neurodegenerative disorder characterized by varying combinations of
autonomic failure, cerebellar ataxia, parkinsonism, and pyramidal signs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiplex PCR (BioFire, FilmArray)} A molecular test that detects genetic material from multiple organisms in one clinical sample.
Different panels are designed for respiratory, gastrointestinal, bloodstream, or other
specimens. Results require interpretation with the symptoms and may detect colonization
or nonviable organisms as well as active infection.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
BioFire, FilmArray

\medterm{Mummification} Postmortem alteration occurring in hot, dry, arid, ventilated environments where rapid
desiccation of tissues occurs before putrefaction can proceed, resulting in dry,
leathery, dark brown skin clinging to underlying skeleton.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mumps} Mumps is a contagious paramyxovirus infection causing painful salivary-gland swelling and fever, with possible meningitis, orchitis, pancreatitis, or hearing complications.
include orchitis (rarely causing infertility), meningitis, pancreatitis, and
sensorineural hearing loss. Prevention by MMR vaccination. Outbreaks occur in
unvaccinated populations. Diagnosis by serology or RT-PCR from saliva.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mumps virus} A paramyxovirus that causes mumps, a contagious respiratory illness. The virus is transmitted by respiratory droplets. Symptoms include fever, headache, and parotitis. Complications include orchitis, oophoritis, meningitis, and deafness. Diagnosis involves serology and PCR.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mural Thrombus} A mural thrombus is a blood clot that forms on the wall of a heart chamber or large
blood vessel, typically in areas of damaged endothelium, stasis, or turbulent flow.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Murmur} An extra or unusual heart sound caused by turbulent blood flow. Murmurs are described by timing, location,
intensity, quality, radiation, and response to position; their significance ranges from
innocent flow sounds to valve or structural disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Murmur Classification} Heart murmurs are classified by timing, grade, shape, location, radiation, and pitch.
Timing: systolic (between S1 and S2), diastolic (between S2 and S1), or continuous.
Grade: I (barely audible) to VI (heard without stethoscope). Shape: crescendo,
decrescendo, or plateau.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscle} A specialized contractile tissue responsible for movement, posture, and heat production.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscle Atrophy} Loss or wasting of muscle tissue caused by disuse, nerve injury, inflammation, malnutrition, or systemic disease.
It may lead to weakness and reduced function; treatment focuses on the cause, nutrition, and
appropriately supervised rehabilitation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscle Contraction} The shortening or tension generation of muscle produced by the interaction of actin and
myosin myofilaments. In skeletal and cardiac muscle, contraction follows
excitation-contraction coupling: calcium binds troponin, tropomyosin moves aside, and
myosin cross-bridges cycle against actin (sliding filament mechanism) using ATP.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscle Pain (Myalgia)} Myalgia—muscle pain—is a common symptom arising from skeletal muscle injury,
inflammation, overuse, infection, or systemic disease.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Myalgia

\medterm{Muscle Tissue} A highly specialized tissue capable of contracting to produce body movement, posture,
and heat.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscle Weakness} Reduced ability to generate force with one or more muscles. Causes include nerve or muscle disease, pain, inactivity,
medicines, metabolic disorders, and aging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscular Dystrophy} A group of inherited disorders characterized by progressive skeletal-muscle weakness and
degeneration. The pattern, age of onset, and complications vary by genetic type; some
forms also affect the heart, respiratory muscles, or other organs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Musculocutaneous Nerve} From lateral cord (C5-C7), piercing coracobrachialis, running between biceps and
brachialis to supply these muscles and lateral forearm skin (lateral antebrachial
cutaneous nerve). Sole motor supply to the anterior arm compartment. Injury causes
weakness of elbow flexion/forearm supination with lateral forearm sensation loss.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Musculoskeletal Pain} Pain arising from muscles, bones, joints, ligaments, tendons, or related soft tissues. Common causes
include injury, overuse, arthritis, and inflammation; persistent pain, weakness, swelling, or
neurologic symptoms should be assessed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Music Therapy} Use of music for therapeutic purposes. Pain reduction, emotional expression, cognitive
stimulation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mutagen} A chemical, physical agent, or biological factor capable of causing a genetic mutation. Mutagenicity does not by itself prove that an agent causes cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mutation} A heritable or acquired alteration in the DNA sequence. Variants may be
single-nucleotide substitutions, insertions, deletions, copy-number changes, or larger
rearrangements and may be benign, pathogenic, or of uncertain significance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mutation Rate} Frequency of new mutations per generation or per base pair per replication. Varies by
gene, genomic region, organism. High mutation rate in repetitive sequences.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myalgic Encephalomyelitis} A complex illness characterized by substantial reduction in activity, post-exertional
malaise, and unrefreshing sleep, often with cognitive or autonomic symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myalgic Encephalomyelitis/Chronic Fatigue Syndrome} A complex illness characterized by substantial reduction in
activity, post-exertional malaise, unrefreshing sleep, and cognitive or autonomic symptoms. Management is individualized
and focuses on pacing, symptom relief, and treatment of coexisting conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myasthenia Gravis} An autoimmune disorder of neuromuscular transmission causing fluctuating, fatigable muscle
weakness. It may affect the eyes, facial and swallowing muscles, limbs, or breathing muscles; antibodies and other tests
help classify the disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myasthenic (Lambert-Eaton) syndrome} An autoimmune presynaptic neuromuscular-junction disorder caused by antibodies against
voltage-gated calcium channels, producing proximal weakness and autonomic symptoms.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Lambert-Eaton

\medterm{Mycobacterium abscessus} A rapidly growing nontuberculous mycobacterium that can cause pulmonary, skin, and
soft-tissue infections and is often difficult to treat because of multidrug resistance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mycobacterium avium complex} A group of nontuberculous mycobacteria that can cause chronic pulmonary disease
or disseminated infection, particularly in people with impaired immunity. Treatment depends on the species, site,
severity, susceptibility, and patient factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mycobacterium leprae} An acid-fast bacillus that causes leprosy (Hansen's disease), a chronic infection
affecting the skin and peripheral nerves. The organism is transmitted through
respiratory droplets, though the exact mechanism is not fully understood. The incubation
period is long (2-5 years).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mycobacterium marinum} A nontuberculous mycobacterium associated with fish tanks and aquatic exposure that can cause
chronic skin and soft-tissue infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mycobacterium tuberculosis} An acid-fast bacillus that causes tuberculosis, a chronic respiratory infection. The
organism is transmitted by respiratory droplets and primarily affects the lungs but can
disseminate to other organs. Latent TB infection is asymptomatic and non-transmissible.
Active TB presents with cough, haemoptysis, fever, and weight loss.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mycology} The branch of microbiology that studies fungi, including yeasts, moulds, and mushrooms.
Medical mycology focuses on fungal pathogens that cause disease in humans. Fungal
infections (mycoses) are classified as superficial, cutaneous, subcutaneous, or
systemic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mycoplasma genitalium} A sexually transmitted bacterium that can cause urethritis, cervicitis, pelvic
inflammatory disease, and, less commonly, proctitis. Many infections are asymptomatic.
Diagnosis is usually by a nucleic acid amplification test, and macrolide resistance is
common, so treatment should follow current susceptibility-informed guidance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mycoplasma Pneumonia} Atypical pneumonia most common in ages 5--35. Dry cough, low-grade fever, headache.
Patchy infiltrates on CXR disproportionate to symptoms (``walking pneumonia'').

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mycoplasma pneumoniae-associated mucositis} Inflammatory lesions of the oral, ocular, or genital mucosa occurring as an
extrapulmonary manifestation of Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection, often presenting with
target-like skin lesions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mycosis Fungoides} The most common cutaneous T-cell lymphoma, presenting with progressive patches, plaques,
and tumors on the skin with an indolent course. Staging and therapy depend on skin
involvement and extracutaneous spread.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mydriasis} Dilation of the pupil, produced mainly by sympathetic activity through the dilator pupillae muscle or by
reduced parasympathetic activity. Causes include darkness, medicines, toxins, neurologic
disease, and ocular injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myelin} Myelin is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
and speeding up electrical signal transmission.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myelin Sheath} The lipid-rich insulating layer around axons formed by Schwann cells (peripheral nervous
system) and oligodendrocytes (central nervous system), which wrap the axon in concentric
membrane layers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myelodysplastic Syndrome} MDS: a group of clonal stem cell disorders characterized by ineffective hematopoiesis,
cytopenias, and risk of transformation to acute myeloid leukemia (AML). Features: anemia
(most common), neutropenia, thrombocytopenia; dysplasia (abnormal morphology) in marrow
and blood; cytogenetic abnormalities (del5q, trisomy 8, del7q).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myelofibrosis} Myelofibrosis is a myeloproliferative neoplasm in which abnormal marrow signaling causes fibrosis, disordered blood production, anemia, enlarged spleen, and variable constitutional symptoms.
hematopoiesis, and JAK2, CALR, or MPL mutations. Presents with anemia, massive
splenomegaly, constitutional symptoms (fever, night sweats, weight loss), and
leukoerythroblastic blood smear.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myelography} Contrast injected into subarachnoid space for spinal imaging. Used when MRI
contraindicated. CT myelography more common now.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myeloma} Multiple myeloma: plasma cell malignancy. Features: bone pain, anemia, renal failure,
hypercalcemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myeloma Cast Nephropathy} Light chain cast formation in tubules causing AKI in multiple myeloma. Friable waxy
casts with giant cells. Bence Jones proteinuria. Treat myeloma and consider
plasmapheresis to reduce light chain burden.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myelomeningocele} Myelomeningocele is an open neural-tube defect in which spinal cord and meninges protrude through a vertebral opening, causing variable paralysis, sensory loss, bladder dysfunction, and hydrocephalus.
through a vertebral defect into a sac on the back. Presents with a visible, fluid-filled
sac at birth. Associated with hydrocephalus, Arnold-Chiari malformation, and lower
extremity paralysis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myelopathy} A general term for any neurological deficit resulting from spinal cord pathology,
encompassing a wide range of causes and clinical presentations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myelosuppression} Reduced bone marrow production of red cells, white cells, and platelets, causing anemia,
neutropenia, and thrombocytopenia; a common dose-limiting toxicity of chemotherapy and
radiation that increases infection and bleeding risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myiasis} Infestation of living tissue by larvae (maggots) of various fly species, which feed on
host tissue and can cause pain, secondary infection, and tissue destruction. It may be
cutaneous, involving the skin and subcutaneous tissue, or affect other body sites
including the nose, ears, and wounds.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myocardial Infarction} Irreversible death of heart-muscle tissue caused by prolonged inadequate blood flow. See Heart Attack.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myocardial Perfusion Imaging} Nuclear cardiology technique using radiotracers (Tc-99m sestamibi, Tl-201) to assess
blood flow to the myocardium. Performed at rest and during stress (exercise or
pharmacological) to detect reversible ischemia (stress defect with rest redistribution)
or fixed defects (scar from prior MI).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myocardial Viability} PET assessment of hibernating myocardium. FDG uptake indicates viable myocardium.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myocarditis} Myocarditis is inflammation of heart muscle, often infectious or immune-mediated, that can cause chest pain, arrhythmias, heart failure, or sudden cardiac dysfunction.
(coxsackievirus, adenovirus, SARS-CoV-2). Presents with chest pain, dyspnea,
arrhythmias, and elevated troponin. Diagnosis: ECG, echocardiography, cardiac MRI with
Lake Louise criteria, and endomyocardial biopsy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myocarditis Diagnosis} Cardiac MRI (Lake Louise criteria) shows myocardial edema (T2-weighted imaging), late
gadolinium enhancement (typically epicardial/mid-wall in non-ischemic distribution), and
increased myocardial T1/T2 mapping values.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myocarditis Treatment} No specific antiviral therapy for most cases. Treatment is supportive: heart failure
therapy (diuretics, ACE inhibitors, beta-blockers) for reduced EF, arrhythmia
management, and avoidance of exercise during acute phase (risk of sudden death).
Immunosuppression may be considered in autoimmune myocarditis or cardiac sarcoidosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myocardium} The thick muscular middle layer of the heart wall composed of cardiac muscle cells
(cardiomyocytes). The myocardium is responsible for the contractile function of the
heart. It contains intercalated discs with gap junctions that allow electrical coupling
between cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myopia} A refractive error in which light focuses in front of the retina, causing blurred distance vision. See Nearsightedness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myositis} Myositis is inflammation or immune-mediated injury of skeletal muscle, producing weakness, pain, elevated muscle enzymes, and sometimes swallowing, lung, or skin involvement.
myositis, immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy. Diagnosis: CK elevation, EMG, muscle
MRI, muscle biopsy. Treatment: immunosuppression with corticosteroids, methotrexate,
azathioprine, mycophenolate, IVIG, rituximab.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myotonia Congenita} An inherited skeletal-muscle channelopathy causing delayed relaxation after voluntary
contraction. Stiffness often improves with repeated movement and may be accompanied by
muscle hypertrophy; onset and severity vary between genetic forms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myotonic Dystrophy} The most common adult-onset muscular dystrophy, an autosomal dominant multisystem
disorder caused by CTG trinucleotide repeat expansions in the DMPK gene (type 1, DM1) or
CCTG tetranucleotide repeat expansions in the CNBP gene (type 2, DM2) on chromosome
19q13.3. DM1 is more common and more severe than DM2.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myringotomy with Tympanostomy Tube} Incision of the tympanic membrane with placement of a ventilation tube to equalize
middle-ear pressure and permit drainage. It is used selectively for persistent
middle-ear effusion, recurrent acute otitis media, or associated hearing and
developmental problems.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myxedema Coma} Severe decompensated hypothyroidism characterized by altered mental status, hypothermia,
bradycardia, hypoventilation, hyponatremia, and hypoglycemia. Most commonly occurs in
elderly patients with long-standing untreated hypothyroidism precipitated by infection,
surgery, or sedative medications. Mortality rates exceed fifty percent.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myxoma} A usually benign primary cardiac tumour composed of myxoid tissue, most often arising in
the left atrium from the interatrial septum. It may cause positional obstruction of the
mitral valve, constitutional symptoms, or systemic embolism; echocardiography is the
usual initial diagnostic test.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ménétrier’s disease} A rare acquired gastropathy characterized by massive hypertrophy of the gastric mucosal
folds, particularly in the body and fundus, with foveolar hyperplasia, cystic dilation
of gastric glands, and glandular atrophy. It presents with epigastric pain, nausea,
vomiting, weight loss, and protein-losing enteropathy causing hypoalbuminemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macrocytic Anemia} Anemia in which red blood cells are larger than normal, commonly because of vitamin B12 or folate deficiency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macular Edema} Swelling of the macula caused by fluid leaking from retinal blood vessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maintenance of Wakefulness Test} A sleep test that measures how long a person can remain awake in a quiet setting during the day.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mastoid Bone} The portion of the temporal bone located behind the ear.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Megaloblastic Anemia} Anemia caused by impaired DNA synthesis, usually related to vitamin B12 or folate deficiency, with abnormally large red blood cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microaneurysm} A small bulge in the wall of a blood vessel.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microvascular Disease} Disease affecting small blood vessels, which may reduce blood flow to organs or tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitral Valve Stenosis} Narrowing of the mitral valve that restricts blood flow from the left atrium to the left ventricle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Morton's Neuroma} Thickening of tissue around a nerve in the forefoot, causing burning pain or numbness between the toes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple Sleep Latency Test} A daytime sleep study that measures how quickly a person falls asleep during scheduled naps.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myasthenia} Abnormal muscle weakness that worsens with use.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myocardial Rupture} A tear in the heart wall or other heart structure, usually a severe complication of myocardial infarction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ménière's Disease} An inner-ear disorder causing episodes of vertigo, fluctuating hearing loss, tinnitus, and ear pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Maxillary Sinusitis} Inflammation or infection of the air-filled sinus cavities behind the cheekbones.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meningomyelocele} A severe form of spina bifida in which the spinal cord and its membranes protrude through an opening in the spine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mesothelioma} A cancer arising from the mesothelium, most often affecting the lining of the lungs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microcytic Anemia} Anemia in which red blood cells are smaller than normal, commonly because of iron deficiency or a thalassemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitochondrial Myopathy} Muscle weakness or dysfunction caused by abnormalities of mitochondria, the energy-producing structures of cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mitral Valve Insufficiency} Leakage of the mitral valve that allows blood to flow back into the left atrium.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mittelschmerz} Mittelschmerz is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mobius Syndrome} A rare congenital disorder causing facial weakness or paralysis and limited outward eye movement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mucopolysaccharidosis} A group of inherited disorders in which glycosaminoglycans accumulate in tissues because of enzyme deficiency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia} An inherited disorder causing tumors or overactivity in two or more endocrine glands.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myoclonus} Sudden, brief, involuntary muscle jerks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myopathy} A disease or disorder that primarily affects skeletal muscle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myxedema} Severe hypothyroidism causing accumulation of mucin in tissues, with swelling, slowed body functions, and other symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Male Breast Cancer} Cancer that develops in breast tissue in a man.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Malignant Peripheral Nerve Sheath Tumor} A cancer arising from cells associated with peripheral nerves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mammary Duct Ectasia} Widening and inflammation of a milk duct beneath the nipple.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Marginal Zone Lymphoma} A slow-growing non-Hodgkin lymphoma that begins in immune cells in the marginal zone of lymphoid tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MCAD Deficiency} An inherited disorder in which the body cannot properly break down certain medium-chain fats for energy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastatic Castration-Resistant Prostate Cancer} Prostate cancer that has spread and continues to grow despite treatments that lower testosterone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Median Arcuate Ligament Syndrome} Compression of the celiac artery by a ligament near the diaphragm, sometimes causing abdominal pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medication Overuse Headaches} Frequent headaches caused or worsened by regular overuse of headache medicines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meralgia Paresthetica} Numbness, tingling, or burning on the outer thigh caused by pressure on a sensory nerve.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macrocephaly}An unusually large head size for a person's age and sex, which may be familial or associated with hydrocephalus, brain growth, or other disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macrodactyly}Abnormally large fingers or toes, usually caused by overgrowth of bone and soft tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Macropsia}A visual disturbance in which objects appear larger than they actually are.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Madura Foot (Mycetoma)}A chronic localized infection of the skin and deeper tissues of the foot, caused by fungi or certain bacteria and characterized by swelling, draining sinuses, and granules.






\textbf{Synonyms}:
Mycetoma

\medterm{Metal Fume Fever}A short-lived influenza-like illness caused by inhalation of fumes from heated metals, especially zinc; symptoms include fever, chills, headache, muscle aches, and cough.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Methaemoglobinaemia}A condition in which haemoglobin is oxidized to methaemoglobin and cannot release oxygen effectively, causing tissue hypoxia and sometimes cyanosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microbiology}The study of microorganisms, including bacteria, viruses, fungi, and parasites, and their relationships with humans and the environment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microtia}A congenital abnormality in which the external ear is unusually small or incompletely formed; it may occur with narrowing or absence of the ear canal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Midwife}A trained healthcare professional who provides care during pregnancy, labour, birth, and the postnatal period, including care of the newborn.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Milroy's Disease}An inherited form of primary lymphoedema, usually present from birth or early life and caused by abnormal development of lymphatic vessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Molar Pregnancy (Hydatidiform Mole)}An abnormal pregnancy in which placental tissue proliferates excessively and the embryo is absent or abnormal; it is a form of gestational trophoblastic disease.








\textbf{Synonyms}:
Hydatidiform Mole

\medterm{Myometrium}The muscular middle layer of the uterine wall, which contracts during menstruation and labour.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mesenteric Lymphadenitis} Inflammation of lymph nodes in the membrane that attaches the intestines to the abdominal wall.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metachromatic Leukodystrophy} An inherited disorder in which fatty substances accumulate and damage myelin in the nervous system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metastatic Breast Cancer} Breast cancer that has spread from the breast to distant parts of the body.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metatarsalgia} Pain and inflammation in the ball of the foot near the metatarsal bones.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Manometry} Measurement of pressure within a body organ or passage, such as the esophagus or rectum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mastication} Chewing food to prepare it for swallowing and digestion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meatus} A natural body opening or passage, such as the external ear canal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micropenis} An unusually small penis caused by inadequate androgen effect or other developmental conditions, defined clinically by a stretched penile length more than 2.5 standard deviations below the mean for age and population. Evaluation may identify endocrine or genetic causes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Medulla} The inner region of an organ; in the kidney it contains the renal pyramids, and in the brainstem it regulates vital functions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microcalcification} A very small calcium deposit in tissue, sometimes an early sign of breast cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myelogram} An imaging study of the spinal canal performed after contrast material is introduced around the spinal cord.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myoglobin} An oxygen-binding protein found mainly in skeletal and cardiac muscle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myomectomy} Surgical removal of uterine fibroids while preserving the uterus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myringotomy} A small incision in the eardrum to drain middle-ear fluid or relieve pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mallet} A surgical hammer used with instruments such as osteotomes to cut or shape bone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mayo Scissors} Heavy surgical scissors used to cut fascia, muscle, sutures, and other relatively tough material.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metzenbaum Scissors} Fine, lightweight surgical scissors used to dissect and cut delicate soft tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mosquito Clamp} A small hemostatic clamp used to compress small blood vessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
