\medterm{Mallet Finger} An injury to the extensor tendon at the distal finger joint, causing inability to actively straighten the fingertip. It may result from a forced flexion injury and sometimes includes an avulsion fracture.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mediastinal Mass} An abnormal growth or collection of tissue in the mediastinum, the central compartment of the chest. Causes include tumors, enlarged lymph nodes, cysts, and vascular abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Morphea} A localized sclerosing skin disorder that produces areas of thickened, hardened, or discolored skin and differs from systemic sclerosis by usually lacking internal-organ involvement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Myofascial Pain Syndrome} A regional pain disorder involving sensitive trigger points in muscle and fascia, with pain that may be referred to other areas and often worsens with pressure or movement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Microangiopathic Hemolytic Anemia} An anemia caused by mechanical fragmentation of red blood cells as they pass through damaged or narrowed small blood vessels, producing schistocytes and often elevated hemolysis markers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Multisystem Inflammatory Syndrome in Children} A serious delayed inflammatory illness occurring in some children after infection with SARS-CoV-2, involving fever and two or more organ systems and potentially causing shock or cardiac dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Threatened Miscarriage} Vaginal bleeding during early pregnancy with a closed cervix and no passage of pregnancy tissue; the pregnancy may remain viable.
Evaluation commonly includes clinical assessment, serial human chorionic gonadotropin testing when indicated, and ultrasound.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Threatened abortion.

\medterm{Missed Miscarriage} A nonviable intrauterine pregnancy that has not been expelled and may produce few or no symptoms of miscarriage.
Diagnosis requires appropriate ultrasound or other clinical evaluation before management is selected.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Missed abortion; delayed miscarriage.

\medterm{Incomplete Miscarriage} Partial expulsion of products of conception, with some pregnancy tissue remaining in the uterus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
\medterm{M phase} The mitotic phase of the cell cycle, during which duplicated chromosomes are separated and the cell divides into two daughter cells; it includes mitosis and cytokinesis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{machine learning} A computational approach in which algorithms learn patterns from data and use them to make predictions or decisions without being explicitly programmed for every rule; medical uses include image analysis and risk prediction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{macroautophagy} The major form of autophagy, in which portions of cytoplasm, damaged organelles, or proteins are enclosed in double-membrane autophagosomes and delivered to lysosomes for degradation and recycling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{macrococcus} macrococcus is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\medterm{macroglobulin} A plasma globulin with a high molecular mass, generally formed by the association of several immunoglobulin or other protein subunits; immunoglobulin M is a principal example.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{macrolide} A class of antibiotics containing a large macrocyclic lactone ring that generally inhibit bacterial protein synthesis by binding the 50S ribosomal subunit; examples include erythromycin, azithromycin, and clarithromycin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{macrolide antibiotic} An antibiotic of the macrolide class that binds the bacterial 50S ribosomal subunit and usually inhibits protein synthesis; activity and resistance vary by organism and drug.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{macronucleus} The larger, transcriptionally active nucleus of a ciliate protozoan that controls routine cellular functions; it differs from the smaller micronucleus involved in genetic reproduction.

\medterm{macrophage activation} The process by which macrophages respond to signals such as microbial products or cytokines and increase phagocytosis, antigen presentation, and production of inflammatory or tissue-repair mediators.

\medterm{macrophage count} The measured number or proportion of macrophages in a tissue, fluid, or laboratory sample; interpretation depends on the specimen, method, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{macroscopic hematuria} Visible blood in the urine, also called gross hematuria; it may arise from the kidneys, ureters, bladder, prostate, or urethra and requires evaluation for causes such as infection, stones, trauma, or malignancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{macrostomia} An abnormally wide mouth or oral opening, usually a congenital facial cleft caused by incomplete fusion of the embryonic facial processes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{macular drusen} Yellowish deposits of extracellular material between the retinal pigment epithelium and Bruch membrane in the macula; larger or numerous drusen are associated with age-related macular degeneration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{maculopapular lesion} A skin lesion combining flat areas of altered color (macules) with small raised solid lesions (papules); it can occur in infections, drug eruptions, inflammatory diseases, and other conditions.

\medterm{mafosfamide} An activated oxazaphosphorine alkylating compound related to ifosfamide and cyclophosphamide, studied for antineoplastic activity and capable of cross-linking DNA.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{magnesium chloride} A magnesium salt used in some oral supplements and medical preparations to provide magnesium or, in concentrated solutions, to help correct magnesium deficiency; dosing depends on the elemental magnesium content.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{magnesium hydroxide} An inorganic compound used orally as an antacid and as an osmotic laxative; it is the active ingredient in Milk of Magnesia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{magnesium oxide} An inorganic magnesium compound used in some supplements and antacids; it can also have a laxative effect, and excessive use may cause hypermagnesemia, especially with kidney impairment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{magnesium sulfate} A magnesium salt used intravenously or intramuscularly to treat selected magnesium deficiencies and conditions such as eclampsia, and used orally as an osmotic laxative; it is also known as Epsom salt in its hydrated form.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{magnet} An object that produces a magnetic field and attracts ferromagnetic materials; magnets are used in devices such as magnetic-resonance imaging systems, while unproven magnet therapies should not replace effective treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{magnetic field} The region around a magnet or electric current in which magnetic forces act; in medicine, controlled magnetic fields are used in MRI and some other technologies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{magnetoencephalography} A noninvasive technique that records the weak magnetic fields produced by neuronal electrical activity, using sensors outside the head to localize and study brain function and some epileptic discharges.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{magnetosomes} Intracellular, membrane-bound structures in certain bacteria that contain magnetic minerals, commonly magnetite or greigite, and help the bacteria orient along magnetic-field lines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{magnusiomyces capitatus} An opportunistic yeast formerly known as \textit{Geotrichum capitatum} or \textit{Saprochaete capitata}; it can cause invasive infections, especially in people with severe immunosuppression or hematologic malignancy.

\medterm{maintenance} Ongoing care or treatment intended to preserve health, prevent relapse or deterioration, or sustain a therapeutic result; maintenance therapy is continued after initial control of a condition.

\medterm{malabsorption syndrome} malabsorption syndrome describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\medterm{malaria vaccine} A vaccine intended to reduce malaria caused by \textit{Plasmodium} parasites, especially \textit{P. falciparum}; the RTS,S/AS01 and R21/Matrix-M vaccines are used in recommended childhood immunization programs in some endemic settings, alongside other preventive measures.

\medterm{malathion} An organophosphate insecticide that inhibits acetylcholinesterase; topical formulations have also been used to treat head lice, but exposure or ingestion can cause cholinergic toxicity.

\medterm{male} Describing a sex associated with reproductive anatomy that produces or is organized around small gametes (sperm); chromosomes, hormones, anatomy, and gender identity do not always fit a simple binary pattern.

\medterm{malignant ameloblastoma} malignant ameloblastoma describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\medterm{malignant glioma} An aggressive primary tumor arising from glial or glial-like cells of the central nervous system; high-grade examples include anaplastic astrocytoma and glioblastoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{malignant histiocytosis} An obsolete term formerly used for an aggressive neoplasm of histiocytic or dendritic-cell type, now classified more specifically under entities such as histiocytic sarcoma or certain high-grade lymphomas.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{malignant mastocytosis} An older term for an aggressive systemic mast-cell neoplasm characterized by abnormal mast-cell proliferation and organ infiltration; modern classification distinguishes aggressive systemic mastocytosis and mast-cell leukemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{malignant myoepithelioma} A malignant tumor showing myoepithelial differentiation, arising most often in a salivary gland or other glandular tissue and capable of local invasion and metastasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{malignant thymoma} An older term for an invasive thymic epithelial tumor; modern usage distinguishes invasive thymoma from thymic carcinoma, which is a separate, more aggressive thymic malignancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mallory body} An eosinophilic cytoplasmic inclusion in a hepatocyte, composed largely of damaged intermediate filaments and associated proteins; it is classically seen in alcohol-associated liver disease but is not specific to it.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{malondialdehyde} A reactive aldehyde produced during oxidation of polyunsaturated lipids; it is commonly measured as an indirect marker of lipid peroxidation and oxidative stress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{malposition} An abnormal position of an organ, body part, implanted device, or fetus; in obstetrics, fetal malposition refers to an abnormal orientation of the presenting part during labor.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{maltodextrin} A mixture of short glucose polymers produced by partial hydrolysis of starch, used as a rapidly digestible carbohydrate and as a thickener, filler, or carrier in foods and medicines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{maltose} A reducing disaccharide composed of two glucose molecules joined by an alpha-1,4 glycosidic bond, produced during starch digestion and hydrolyzed by maltase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mammaglobin A} A secretory protein expressed predominantly in mammary tissue and used as an immunohistochemical marker that can help identify breast origin in some metastatic carcinomas.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mammals} Warm-blooded vertebrate animals that have hair or fur, three middle-ear bones, and mammary glands that produce milk; humans are mammals.

\medterm{mammaplasty} mammaplasty is used in relation to mammaplasty during assessment, diagnosis, treatment, monitoring, or patient support.

\medterm{mammary disease} mammary disease describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\medterm{mammary epithelium} The epithelial lining of the mammary ducts and secretory alveoli; these cells help produce, secrete, and transport milk and can give rise to many breast carcinomas.

\medterm{mammillary body} One of a pair of rounded nuclei on the underside of the hypothalamus that participate in memory circuits connecting the hippocampus with the thalamus; injury can contribute to memory impairment.

\medterm{managed care} A system of organizing and financing health care through provider networks, utilization review, care coordination, and negotiated payment arrangements intended to control costs and maintain quality.

\medterm{mandatory reporting} A legal or regulatory duty to report specified findings or circumstances—such as suspected child or elder abuse, certain communicable diseases, or some injuries—to designated authorities; requirements vary by jurisdiction.

\medterm{mandibular condyle} The rounded superior end of each mandibular ramus that articulates with the temporal bone at the temporomandibular joint and permits jaw movement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mandibular prosthesis} An artificial implant or device used to replace, reconstruct, stabilize, or restore part of the mandible after trauma, tumor removal, congenital deformity, or other loss of jaw structure.

\medterm{mandibulofacial dysostosis} A congenital craniofacial disorder, classically Treacher Collins syndrome, involving underdevelopment of the mandible and cheekbones with variable abnormalities of the ears, eyes, and palate.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{manic} Relating to mania, a pathological episode of abnormally elevated, expansive, or irritable mood with increased energy or activity and associated changes in thought, speech, sleep, or judgment.

\medterm{manic disorder} manic disorder describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\medterm{manic mood} An abnormally elevated, expansive, or persistently irritable mood accompanied by increased energy or activity, reduced need for sleep, rapid speech, racing thoughts, inflated self-esteem, or risky behavior; it is characteristic of a manic episode.

\medterm{mannomustine} mannomustine is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mannose} A six-carbon monosaccharide related to glucose that forms part of glycoproteins and glycolipids and is important in protein glycosylation and cell recognition.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mannosidosis} An inherited lysosomal storage disorder caused by deficient alpha-mannosidase activity, leading to accumulation of mannose-rich oligosaccharides and variable intellectual, skeletal, immune, hearing, and neurologic abnormalities.

\medterm{mansonella perstans} mansonella perstans identifies mansonella perstans, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\medterm{mansonella streptocerca} mansonella streptocerca is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\medterm{mansonelliasis} Infection with filarial nematodes of the genus \textit{Mansonella}, transmitted by biting midges or blackflies; many infections are asymptomatic, but some cause itching, skin lesions, fever, or eosinophilia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mantle field} A historical radiation-treatment field encompassing lymph-node regions in the neck, mediastinum, and upper chest, formerly used for Hodgkin lymphoma; modern treatment generally uses more limited involved-site fields.

\medterm{Mantoux test} Mantoux test is a diagnostic or laboratory method used to evaluate a clinical question; interpretation depends on the specimen or body site, method, reference range, and patient context.

\medterm{marginal ulcer} An ulcer occurring at or near a gastrojejunal anastomosis, often after gastric bypass or other gastric surgery, where acid, ischemia, smoking, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, or other factors may contribute.

\medterm{masoprocol} A synthetic derivative of nordihydroguaiaretic acid that inhibits lipoxygenase pathways and has been investigated for topical treatment of actinic keratoses and for antineoplastic activity.

\medterm{mass screening} mass screening is a diagnostic or laboratory method used to evaluate a clinical question; interpretation depends on the specimen or body site, method, reference range, and patient context.

\medterm{mass spectrometer} An instrument that ionizes chemical substances and separates or detects the resulting ions according to their mass-to-charge ratios, producing a mass spectrum.

\medterm{massage} Manual manipulation of soft tissues using techniques such as stroking, kneading, or pressure, used for relaxation or symptom relief; it is not a substitute for diagnosis or treatment of an underlying disease.

\medterm{masticate} To chew food, using the teeth, tongue, and muscles of mastication to mechanically break it down and mix it with saliva before swallowing.

\medterm{masticatory muscle} One of the muscles that moves the mandible during chewing, chiefly the masseter, temporalis, medial pterygoid, and lateral pterygoid muscles.

\medterm{maytansine} Maytansine is a potent antimitotic compound that binds tubulin and inhibits microtubule assembly. Its derivatives are used as cytotoxic payloads in some antibody--drug conjugates.

\medterm{mazindol} Mazindol is a sympathomimetic appetite suppressant that affects central catecholamine signaling. It has been used for short-term obesity treatment in some countries and may cause stimulant-related adverse effects.

\medterm{mean} The mean is the arithmetic average, calculated by adding all values and dividing by the number of values. It is commonly used to summarize clinical measurements but can be distorted by extreme values.

\medterm{mean diameter} Mean diameter is the average diameter of a structure or lesion, usually calculated from one or more measured dimensions. The method used should be specified when measurements are clinically compared.

\medterm{mebendazole} mebendazole is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mecamylamine} Mecamylamine is an orally active ganglionic nicotinic-receptor blocker that reduces sympathetic nerve transmission and lowers blood pressure. It has been used for severe hypertension and may cause postural hypotension, dizziness, constipation, and dry mouth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mecarzole} Mecarzole is a synonym for carbendazim, a benzimidazole fungicide that interferes with fungal microtubule formation. It is an agricultural chemical rather than a routine human medicine and may be toxic if exposure occurs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mechanical ventilator} A mechanical ventilator is a machine that supports or replaces spontaneous breathing by delivering controlled or assisted breaths, usually through a mask or an endotracheal tube. Settings are adjusted to oxygenation, ventilation, mechanics, and patient needs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mechanoreceptor} mechanoreceptor is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mechlorethamine} Mechlorethamine is an alkylating antineoplastic agent that cross-links DNA and inhibits cell division. Topical gel is used for cutaneous T-cell lymphoma; systemic use is limited by toxicities such as myelosuppression and tissue injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meclizine} Meclizine is a first-generation antihistamine with anticholinergic and vestibular-suppressant effects. It is used to prevent or treat motion sickness and vertigo and may cause drowsiness, dry mouth, and blurred vision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medial meniscus} The medial meniscus is a crescent-shaped fibrocartilage pad between the medial femoral condyle and medial tibial plateau. It distributes load and contributes to knee stability; tears may cause joint-line pain, swelling, or locking.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{median} The median is the middle value after observations are ordered. With an even number of observations it is the average of the two middle values, and it is less affected by extreme values than the arithmetic mean.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mediastinal cyst} A mediastinal cyst is a fluid- or tissue-filled cystic lesion in the mediastinum. Common types include bronchogenic, thymic, and pericardial cysts; symptoms depend on size and compression, and imaging guides evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mediastinal disease} mediastinal disease describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mediastinal Emphysema} Mediastinal emphysema, or pneumomediastinum, is the presence of free air in the mediastinum. It can follow alveolar rupture, trauma, surgery, or esophageal injury and may cause chest pain, neck crepitus, or respiratory compromise.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mediastinal hemorrhage} mediastinal hemorrhage describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mediastinal neoplasm} mediastinal neoplasm describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mediastinal pleura} The mediastinal pleura is the portion of parietal pleura lining the lateral surfaces of the mediastinum. It separates the mediastinal contents from the pleural cavity and reflects around the lung root.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mediastinal shift} Mediastinal shift is displacement of the mediastinal structures away from their usual position. It may result from increased pressure on one side, such as a tension pneumothorax, or loss of volume on the other, such as lung collapse.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mediastinoscopes} Mediastinoscopes are slender endoscopic instruments used during mediastinoscopy to inspect the mediastinum and obtain tissue samples, especially from mediastinal lymph nodes. The procedure is performed through a small incision near the sternum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medical device} A medical device is an instrument, apparatus, implant, software, or similar article intended for diagnosis, prevention, monitoring, treatment, or alleviation of disease or injury. Its primary action is not achieved by chemical or metabolic means.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medical economics} Medical economics is the study of how health-care resources are produced, distributed, financed, and used. It evaluates costs, outcomes, efficiency, access, and value in health-care decisions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medical history} A medical history is a structured account of a person’s current concerns, past illnesses and operations, medications, allergies, family history, social history, and relevant exposures. It helps guide examination, diagnosis, and treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medical indigency} Medical indigency is inability to obtain needed medical care because of insufficient financial resources or lack of adequate coverage. The term describes a social and economic circumstance, not a disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medical pin} A medical pin is a slender metal fixation device inserted into bone or across a joint to stabilize a fracture or support orthopedic treatment. Pins may be temporary or permanent and require monitoring for infection and displacement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medical record} A medical record is the documented account of a patient’s health information, including history, findings, diagnoses, test results, treatments, and clinical decisions. It supports continuity, communication, quality improvement, and legal documentation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medicine} Medicine is the science and practice of preventing, diagnosing, treating, and managing disease and injury. The term can also mean a drug or other therapeutic preparation used to treat a condition.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meditation} Meditation is a practice of intentionally directing attention, often toward the breath, a sensation, or an object, while observing thoughts without judgment. It may promote relaxation and stress management but does not replace indicated medical treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medroxyprogesterone} medroxyprogesterone is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medroxyprogesterone acetate} Medroxyprogesterone acetate is a synthetic progestin used in hormonal contraception, treatment of endometriosis or abnormal uterine bleeding, and hormone therapy. It suppresses ovulation and produces progestational effects; formulation-specific risks and contraindications apply.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medullary carcinoma} medullary carcinoma describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{medulloepithelioma} A medulloepithelioma is a rare embryonal tumor arising most often from the nonpigmented ciliary epithelium of the eye in children. It may cause visual symptoms, neovascular glaucoma, or a visible mass and is treated according to its extent and pathology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mefenamic acid} mefenamic acid is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mefloquine} Mefloquine is an antimalarial drug used for prevention and treatment of susceptible malaria. It has a long half-life but can cause neuropsychiatric, vestibular, and cardiac adverse effects, so patient-specific contraindications and warnings are important.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{megabecquerel} A megabecquerel (MBq) is a unit of radioactivity equal to one million nuclear disintegrations per second. It is used to state the activity of radiopharmaceuticals and other radioactive materials.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{megacalycosis} Megacalycosis is congenital, nonobstructive dilatation of the renal calyces, usually with otherwise preserved renal architecture and function. It is often incidental but must be distinguished from obstructive hydronephrosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{megacolon} Megacolon is abnormal, marked dilation of the colon that may be acute or chronic. Causes include severe constipation, obstruction, motility disorders, and toxic megacolon associated with colitis; complications include ischemia and perforation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{megakaryocyte} A megakaryocyte is a large bone-marrow cell with a multilobed polyploid nucleus that produces platelets by shedding cytoplasmic fragments into the circulation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{megakaryocytopoiesis} Megakaryocytopoiesis is the formation and maturation of megakaryocytes in the bone marrow, followed by platelet production. It is regulated in large part by thrombopoietin and other hematopoietic signals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Megamonas} Megamonas is a genus of anaerobic, gram-negative bacteria found mainly in the intestinal microbiota of humans and animals. Some species have been recovered from clinical specimens, although their disease-causing role depends on the clinical setting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Megamonas funiformis} Megamonas funiformis is an anaerobic bacterium of the genus Megamonas, commonly associated with the intestinal microbiota. Its detection in a clinical specimen requires interpretation alongside symptoms, specimen quality, and other microbiologic findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Megamonas hypermegale} Megamonas hypermegale is an anaerobic bacterium in the genus Megamonas, primarily associated with the gastrointestinal microbiota. Its presence in a specimen is interpreted in relation to the site, clinical symptoms, and other microbiologic results.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Megasphaera} Megasphaera is a genus of anaerobic bacteria found in the oral cavity and gastrointestinal and genital microbiota. Species identification may be relevant in microbiome studies or when recovered from a clinical specimen.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Megasphaera elsdenii} Megasphaera elsdenii is an anaerobic bacterium commonly found in the rumen and other microbial communities. It ferments organic acids and is mainly of relevance in microbiology and animal or microbiome research.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mega-trial} A mega-trial is a very large clinical trial designed to enroll enough participants to detect clinically important differences, including relatively uncommon outcomes or modest treatment effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{megestrol} Megestrol is a synthetic progestin used in some settings to treat hormone-responsive cancers and, in certain formulations, anorexia or severe weight loss. It can cause glucocorticoid-like effects, thromboembolic risk, and adrenal suppression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{megestrol acetate} Megestrol acetate is the acetate salt of the progestin megestrol. Depending on formulation and indication, it is used for advanced endometrial or breast cancer and for appetite stimulation in selected patients with severe weight loss.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meibomian glands} Meibomian glands are modified sebaceous glands in the eyelids that secrete the lipid layer of the tear film. Dysfunction or blockage can cause evaporative dry eye, meibomian gland dysfunction, or a chalazion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meiotic recombination} Meiotic recombination is exchange of DNA between homologous chromosomes during meiosis, usually through crossing over. It creates new combinations of alleles and contributes to genetic variation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melancholia} Melancholia is a severe depressive state characterized by pervasive anhedonia or loss of pleasure, lack of mood reactivity, psychomotor change, early-morning awakening, and excessive or inappropriate guilt. In modern classification it is a melancholic feature pattern of major depressive disorder.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melanocyte} A melanocyte is a pigment-producing cell derived from the neural crest. It makes melanin in specialized organelles called melanosomes and transfers the pigment to neighboring keratinocytes, helping protect skin from ultraviolet radiation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melanocytic nevus} A melanocytic nevus is a usually benign, localized proliferation of melanocytes that appears as a macule, papule, or nodule. Changes in size, color, border, or symptoms may require assessment for atypia or melanoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melanophore} A melanophore is a pigment-containing cell, especially one in lower vertebrates, whose melanin granules can disperse or aggregate to alter visible coloration. In mammals, melanocytes are the principal melanin-producing cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melanosome} A melanosome is a specialized organelle in a melanocyte in which melanin is synthesized, stored, and transported. Melanosomes are transferred to keratinocytes and influence skin, hair, and eye pigmentation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MELAS syndrome} MELAS syndrome describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melatonin receptor} melatonin receptor is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melengestrol acetate} Melengestrol acetate is a synthetic progestin used primarily in veterinary medicine to suppress estrus and improve feed efficiency in cattle. It is not approved for use in humans.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melitten} Melitten, more commonly spelled melittin, is the principal peptide toxin in honeybee venom. It disrupts cell membranes and contributes to pain, inflammation, hemolysis, and other effects of envenomation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meloxicam} Meloxicam is a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug that inhibits cyclooxygenase enzymes and reduces prostaglandin synthesis. It is used for pain and inflammation, but can cause gastrointestinal, renal, cardiovascular, and hypersensitivity adverse effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{melphalan} Melphalan is an alkylating chemotherapy agent that cross-links DNA and inhibits cell proliferation. It is used in selected hematologic malignancies, including multiple myeloma, and may cause dose-limiting myelosuppression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{memantine hydrochloride} Memantine hydrochloride is an NMDA-receptor antagonist used to help manage moderate-to-severe Alzheimer disease. It reduces pathologic glutamate-mediated excitotoxicity; common adverse effects include dizziness, headache, constipation, and confusion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{membrane channel} A membrane channel is a protein-forming pore across a cell membrane that permits selected ions or molecules to pass down an electrochemical or concentration gradient. Many channels are gated by voltage, ligands, or mechanical forces.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{membrane fusion} Membrane fusion is merging of two lipid bilayers into one continuous membrane. It is required for processes such as synaptic vesicle release, enveloped-virus entry, fertilization, and intracellular vesicle trafficking.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{membrane lipids} membrane lipids is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{membrane microdomain} A membrane microdomain is a small, specialized region of a cell membrane with a distinct composition of lipids and proteins. It can organize receptors, signaling molecules, transporters, and cytoskeletal interactions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{membrane part} A membrane part is a structural or molecular component of a biological membrane, such as a lipid, protein, carbohydrate, channel, or receptor. These components determine membrane integrity, transport, signaling, and cell recognition.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{membrane protein} membrane protein is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{membrane raft} A membrane raft is a cholesterol- and sphingolipid-enriched membrane microdomain that helps cluster selected proteins and signaling molecules. Raft organization is dynamic and may change during receptor signaling or membrane trafficking.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{memory disorder} memory disorder describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{memory impairment} Memory impairment is reduced ability to learn, store, retrieve, or recognize information. It may be transient or persistent and can result from normal aging, medications, sleep problems, neurologic disease, psychiatric illness, or other medical conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{memory training} Memory training consists of structured exercises or strategies intended to improve encoding, storage, retrieval, or compensatory organization of information. Benefits vary by method and condition and do not establish treatment for an underlying disorder.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meniere's disease} Meniere's disease describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meningeal carcinomatosis} meningeal carcinomatosis describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meningeal leukemia} meningeal leukemia describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meningeal lymphoma} meningeal lymphoma describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meningeal neoplasm} meningeal neoplasm describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meningism} Meningism is a syndrome of neck stiffness, headache, and sometimes photophobia or vomiting that resembles meningeal irritation without necessarily indicating meningitis. Serious causes, including infection or subarachnoid hemorrhage, require prompt evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meniscus} A meniscus is a crescent-shaped fibrocartilaginous structure, especially one of the medial or lateral knee, that distributes load, improves joint congruence, and contributes to stability. A meniscal tear may cause pain, swelling, or mechanical symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{menogaril} Menogaril is a semisynthetic anthracycline derivative of nogalamycin. It intercalates into DNA and inhibits topoisomerase II, thereby impairing DNA replication and repair; it was investigated as an antineoplastic agent.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{menopause status} Menopause status describes whether a person is premenopausal, perimenopausal, or postmenopausal. Natural menopause is clinically defined after 12 consecutive months without menstruation when no other cause explains the amenorrhea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{menotropin} menotropin is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{menses duration} Menses duration is the number of days of menstrual bleeding in a cycle, counted from the first day of bleeding until bleeding stops. Persistently prolonged, heavy, or irregular bleeding warrants clinical assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{menstruation disturbance} A menstruation disturbance is an abnormality in the timing, regularity, duration, amount, or associated symptoms of menstrual bleeding. Causes include pregnancy, endocrine disorders, structural disease, medications, systemic illness, and perimenopause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mental disorders} mental disorders describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mental fatigue} Mental fatigue is a subjective feeling of reduced mental energy, concentration, or cognitive endurance after sustained effort or illness. Persistent or severe symptoms may accompany sleep disorders, depression, neurologic disease, medication effects, or other medical conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mental process} A mental process is a cognitive or affective activity of the mind, such as perception, attention, memory, reasoning, language, emotion, or decision-making.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mental recall} Mental recall is retrieval of previously learned or stored information from memory. It may be assessed by asking a person to remember facts, events, words, or experiences after a delay.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mental retardation} This outdated and stigmatizing term historically referred to intellectual disability, a neurodevelopmental condition involving limitations in intellectual functioning and adaptive behavior beginning during the developmental period. Current clinical usage is \textit{intellectual disability}.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{menthol} Menthol is a naturally occurring or synthetic compound that activates cold-sensitive TRPM8 receptors, producing a cooling sensation. It is used in topical analgesics, cough preparations, and flavoring; concentrated exposure can be harmful.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meperidine} Meperidine is an opioid analgesic used for short-term treatment of moderate-to-severe pain in selected circumstances. Its metabolite can accumulate and cause neurotoxicity, especially with renal impairment; respiratory depression and dependence are important risks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meperidine hydrochloride} Meperidine hydrochloride is the hydrochloride salt of the opioid analgesic meperidine. It produces analgesia through opioid-receptor activity and carries risks of sedation, respiratory depression, seizures, dependence, and serotonin toxicity with interacting drugs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mephenytoin} Mephenytoin is a hydantoin anticonvulsant formerly used to treat seizures. It is metabolized partly to ethotoin and has limited modern use because of potentially serious adverse effects, including hypersensitivity and blood dyscrasias.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mepivacaine} mepivacaine is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meprobamate} Meprobamate is a sedative and anxiolytic that depresses central nervous system activity. It has misuse, dependence, withdrawal, and overdose risks and has largely been replaced by safer treatments for anxiety and insomnia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mercaptopurine} Mercaptopurine is a thiopurine antimetabolite that inhibits purine synthesis and is incorporated into nucleic acids. It is used in selected leukemias and inflammatory diseases, with risks including myelosuppression, hepatotoxicity, and drug--gene or drug--drug interactions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meridian} In traditional Chinese medicine, a meridian is a proposed pathway through which qi is said to flow and along which acupuncture points are located. Meridians are not recognized as discrete anatomic structures in modern biomedical anatomy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{merkel cells} merkel cells is an anatomical or histologic term describing a body structure, tissue, or region; its clinical meaning depends on its location, function, and associated findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meropenem} meropenem is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mersalyl} Mersalyl is an organomercury compound formerly used as a diuretic. Because mercury compounds can cause serious renal and neurologic toxicity, it has been replaced by safer diuretics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesalamine} Mesalamine, or 5-aminosalicylic acid, is an anti-inflammatory drug used to induce and maintain remission in ulcerative colitis and some other inflammatory bowel conditions. It acts mainly topically in the intestinal mucosa and can rarely affect renal function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesangiolysis} Mesangiolysis is dissolution or disruption of the mesangial tissue within a renal glomerulus. It may occur in severe endothelial injury and can be associated with microvascular lesions and glomerular capillary aneurysmal changes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesangium} The mesangium is the supportive tissue between glomerular capillary loops, composed of mesangial cells and extracellular matrix. Mesangial cells provide structural support, regulate capillary flow, and clear certain substances; abnormal expansion occurs in several glomerular diseases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesencephalon} The mesencephalon, or midbrain, is the part of the brainstem between the pons and diencephalon. It contains pathways and nuclei involved in eye movements, motor control, arousal, and auditory and visual reflexes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesenchymal cell} mesenchymal cell is an anatomical or histologic term describing a body structure, tissue, or region; its clinical meaning depends on its location, function, and associated findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesenchymal chondrosarcoma} mesenchymal chondrosarcoma describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesenchyme} Mesenchyme is embryonic connective tissue, derived largely from mesoderm, whose cells and matrix give rise to bone, cartilage, connective tissue, blood vessels, and other structures. Some mesenchymal tissues persist in adult organs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesenchymoma} A mesenchymoma is a rare tumor containing more than one type of mesenchymal tissue, sometimes including cartilage, bone, fat, or vascular elements. Behavior depends on the site, age, histology, and presence of malignant components.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesenteric artery} A mesenteric artery supplies blood to portions of the intestine and associated abdominal organs. The superior and inferior mesenteric arteries are the major vessels; obstruction or embolism can cause acute mesenteric ischemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesenteric cyst} A mesenteric cyst is a fluid-filled cystic lesion within the mesentery. It may be congenital, lymphatic, enteric, or traumatic; symptoms depend on size and complications such as infection, hemorrhage, torsion, or bowel obstruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesenteric veins} Mesenteric veins drain blood from the small and large intestines and carry it mainly through the superior and inferior mesenteric veins to the portal venous system. Thrombosis or impaired flow can cause intestinal congestion and ischemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesial surface} The mesial surface is the surface of a tooth or other structure oriented toward the midline. In dentistry, mesial surfaces contact adjacent teeth and can be involved in caries, periodontal disease, or restoration margins.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesna} Mesna is a sulfhydryl-containing uroprotective drug that binds the toxic acrolein metabolite of ifosfamide and cyclophosphamide in the urine. It is used to reduce hemorrhagic cystitis during treatment with these oxazaphosphorine chemotherapy agents.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesonephric} Mesonephric means relating to the mesonephros, a temporary embryonic kidney, or to structures derived from its ducts and tubules. Mesonephric remnants can give rise to uncommon benign or malignant lesions of the genital tract.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesonephric tumor} mesonephric tumor describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesonephroma} Mesonephroma is an older, context-dependent term for a tumor formerly thought to arise from mesonephric remnants, including some clear-cell or mesonephric tumors of the female genital tract and historically some yolk-sac tumors. Modern pathology uses more specific diagnostic names.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesothelial neoplasm} mesothelial neoplasm describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesothelin} Mesothelin is a cell-surface glycoprotein expressed mainly by mesothelial cells and overexpressed in several cancers, including mesothelioma and some ovarian and pancreatic cancers. It is being studied as a biomarker and therapeutic target.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mesothelium} The mesothelium is a single layer of specialized cells lining serous body cavities and covering their organs, including the pleura, peritoneum, and pericardium. It produces lubricating fluid and participates in inflammation and repair.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mestranol} Mestranol is a synthetic estrogen prodrug converted in the body to ethinyl estradiol. It was formerly used in some hormonal contraceptives; estrogen-related risks include venous thromboembolism and cardiovascular complications in susceptible people.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metabolic disease} metabolic disease describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metabolic response} A metabolic response is a change in energy use, substrate metabolism, or biochemical pathways in response to conditions such as fasting, exercise, infection, trauma, or medication. The response may be adaptive or contribute to illness when excessive or prolonged.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metabolite} A metabolite is a substance produced, modified, or consumed by a metabolic reaction in a cell or organism. Metabolites may be normal intermediates, end products, signaling molecules, or products of drug metabolism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metabolomics} Metabolomics is the comprehensive study of small-molecule metabolites in cells, tissues, or biological fluids. It uses analytical methods such as mass spectrometry or nuclear magnetic resonance to investigate physiology, disease, and treatment effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metacarpal bone} A metacarpal bone is one of the five long bones of the hand between the wrist bones and the proximal phalanges. Each has a base, shaft, and head; fractures can affect alignment, grip, and joint function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metadata} Metadata is information describing other data, such as its source, format, time, units, author, processing history, or quality. In health care it helps identify, interpret, exchange, and govern clinical and research data.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metagenome} A metagenome is the total genetic material recovered from a microbial community or other mixed biological sample, including genomes from organisms that may not be cultured.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metagenomics} Metagenomics is the study of genetic material collected directly from an environmental or clinical sample containing multiple organisms. It can characterize microbial composition and functional genes without isolating each organism in culture.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Metagonimus yokogawai} Metagonimus yokogawai is a small intestinal fluke acquired by eating raw or inadequately cooked freshwater fish. Infection can cause abdominal symptoms and, rarely, ectopic migration of eggs or adult worms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metal} A metal is an element or alloy with characteristic physical and chemical properties such as electrical conductivity and metallic bonding. In medicine, metals may be nutrients, toxic exposures, laboratory analytes, or components of implants and devices.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metalloendopeptidase} A metalloendopeptidase is an endopeptidase whose catalytic activity depends on a bound metal ion, commonly zinc. It cleaves peptide bonds within proteins and includes enzymes involved in extracellular matrix remodeling and signaling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metalloporphyrins} Metalloporphyrins are porphyrin molecules containing a centrally coordinated metal ion. Heme, which contains iron, is a biologically important example and forms the functional group of hemoglobin, myoglobin, and many enzymes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metalloprotease} A metalloprotease is a proteolytic enzyme that requires a metal ion, commonly zinc, for catalytic activity. Metalloproteases regulate extracellular matrix turnover, cell signaling, inflammation, and tissue remodeling; dysregulation contributes to disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metalloprotein} metalloprotein is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metallothionein} Metallothionein is a small, cysteine-rich protein that binds metals such as zinc, copper, and cadmium. It helps regulate essential metals and may protect cells from metal toxicity and oxidative stress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metamorphosis} Metamorphosis is a marked change in body form during development, as in the transformation of a larva into an adult. In pathology, the related concept of metaplasia describes an adaptive change from one mature cell type to another.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metanephrine} Metanephrine is a metabolite of epinephrine formed mainly by catechol-O-methyltransferase. Plasma or urinary metanephrine measurements are used in evaluation for catecholamine-secreting tumors such as pheochromocytoma and paraganglioma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metaphase} Metaphase is the stage of mitosis or meiosis in which condensed chromosomes align at the cell equator before separating to opposite poles. Spindle attachments to kinetochores help ensure accurate chromosome segregation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metaphase plate} The metaphase plate is the plane at the center of a dividing cell where chromosomes align during metaphase. It is a geometric arrangement rather than a discrete anatomical structure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metaphyseal widening} Metaphyseal widening is enlargement of the growth-plate or metaphyseal region of a developing long bone. It may be seen in disorders such as rickets, skeletal dysplasia, infection, or healing injury and must be interpreted with imaging and clinical findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metaplastic carcinoma} metaplastic carcinoma describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metapneumovirus} metapneumovirus is a virus or viral group classified by its genetic and biologic features. Clinical importance depends on the strain, host, route of transmission, tissue tropism, and ability to cause disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metastin} Metastin is an older name for kisspeptin, a peptide derived from the KISS1 gene. It stimulates hypothalamic gonadotropin-releasing hormone secretion and helps regulate puberty, reproductive function, and tumor-cell signaling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metatarsal bone} A metatarsal bone is one of the five long bones of the forefoot between the tarsal bones and proximal phalanges. Metatarsal fractures and alignment abnormalities can affect weight bearing and gait.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metatarsal valgus} Metatarsal valgus is outward deviation of one or more metatarsal bones relative to the foot’s normal axis. It may contribute to forefoot deformity and altered load distribution and is assessed clinically and radiographically.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metatarsus varus} Metatarsus varus is inward deviation of the forefoot caused by medial angulation of the metatarsal bones. It is often recognized in infancy and may be flexible or rigid; management depends on severity and persistence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metencephalon} The metencephalon is the embryonic brain region that develops into the pons and cerebellum. These structures participate in motor coordination, balance, cranial-nerve functions, and relay of signals between the brain and spinal cord.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{meter} A meter is the SI base unit of length, equal to 100 centimeters. In medicine, the term also appears in names of instruments that measure quantities, such as a glucose meter or spirometer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metestrus} metestrus is a diagnostic or laboratory method used to evaluate a clinical question; interpretation depends on the specimen or body site, method, reference range, and patient context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metformin} Metformin is a biguanide antihyperglycemic drug that lowers hepatic glucose production and improves insulin sensitivity. It is commonly used for type 2 diabetes; gastrointestinal effects are common, and rare lactic acidosis risk increases with certain severe conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metformin hydrochloride} Metformin hydrochloride is the hydrochloride salt of metformin, an oral biguanide used chiefly for type 2 diabetes. It lowers blood glucose without directly stimulating pancreatic insulin release and is available in immediate- and extended-release formulations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methacholine chloride} Methacholine chloride is a muscarinic cholinergic agonist used in bronchoprovocation testing. In susceptible people it causes airway narrowing, allowing assessment for bronchial hyperresponsiveness under controlled medical supervision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methadone} Methadone is a long-acting opioid agonist used for pain in selected cases and for medication treatment of opioid use disorder. It can cause respiratory depression, drug interactions, and dose-related QT-interval prolongation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methadone hydrochloride} Methadone hydrochloride is the hydrochloride salt of methadone. It provides long-lasting opioid-receptor activity for selected analgesic and opioid-use-disorder indications, with risks of respiratory depression, dependence, overdose, and cardiac arrhythmia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methamidophos} Methamidophos is a highly toxic organophosphorus insecticide that inhibits acetylcholinesterase. Exposure can cause cholinergic poisoning with sweating, salivation, vomiting, bronchospasm, bradycardia, muscle weakness, and potentially respiratory failure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methamphetamine} Methamphetamine is a potent central nervous system stimulant that increases synaptic catecholamines. It has limited medical use in some settings but is commonly misused; toxicity and chronic use can cause cardiovascular complications, psychosis, dependence, and neurocognitive harm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methane} Methane is a colorless, odorless, highly flammable gas produced by anaerobic decomposition and normal intestinal microbial activity. It can displace oxygen in enclosed spaces and cause asphyxiation; it is not itself a direct systemic poison at ordinary exposure levels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methane sulfonate} Methane sulfonate, or mesylate, is the anion or ester derived from methanesulfonic acid. Mesylate salts are commonly used to improve the formulation of medicines; some methanesulfonate esters are potent alkylating agents and toxic impurities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methanobacteriaceae} methanobacteriaceae is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methanol} Methanol is a toxic alcohol used as an industrial solvent and fuel. Its metabolism to formaldehyde and formic acid can cause metabolic acidosis, visual loss, neurologic injury, and death; poisoning is a medical emergency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methaqualone} Methaqualone is a sedative-hypnotic formerly used for insomnia and anxiety. It is no longer widely marketed because of misuse, dependence, dangerous respiratory and central nervous system depression, and overdose risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methazolamide} Methazolamide is a carbonic anhydrase inhibitor used mainly to lower intraocular pressure in glaucoma. It reduces aqueous humor production and can cause metabolic acidosis, electrolyte abnormalities, kidney stones, or sulfonamide-related reactions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methemoglobin} Methemoglobin is hemoglobin in which the iron is oxidized to the ferric state and cannot effectively bind oxygen. Increased methemoglobin causes tissue hypoxia and may result from oxidant drugs, chemicals, or inherited enzyme defects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methenamine} Methenamine is a urinary antiseptic that decomposes in acidic urine to release formaldehyde, which inhibits bacterial growth. It is used for prevention of recurrent urinary tract infection in selected patients, not for treatment of active upper-tract infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methicillin} methicillin is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methidathion} Methidathion is an organophosphorus insecticide and acaricide that inhibits acetylcholinesterase. Exposure can produce a cholinergic toxidrome with secretions, bronchospasm, bradycardia, gastrointestinal symptoms, weakness, and respiratory failure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methimazole} methimazole is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methiocarb} Methiocarb is a carbamate pesticide that inhibits acetylcholinesterase reversibly. Poisoning causes excessive muscarinic and nicotinic cholinergic activity and may require urgent supportive care and antidotal treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methionine} Methionine is an essential sulfur-containing amino acid used in protein synthesis and as a precursor of S-adenosylmethionine, cysteine, and other metabolites. Excessive or disordered methionine metabolism can contribute to metabolic disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methionine enkephalin} Methionine enkephalin is an endogenous opioid pentapeptide that binds opioid receptors and participates in modulation of pain, stress, reward, and neuroendocrine function. It is one of the products of proenkephalin processing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methisazone} Methisazone is an older antiviral compound investigated mainly for prevention or treatment of smallpox and some other viral infections. It is not a standard contemporary treatment for most viral diseases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methocarbamol} Methocarbamol is a centrally acting skeletal-muscle relaxant used with rest and physical measures for acute painful musculoskeletal conditions. It can cause drowsiness, dizziness, impaired coordination, and additive central nervous system depression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methodology} Methodology is the systematic set of principles, methods, procedures, and assumptions used to conduct and evaluate an investigation or clinical study. Sound methodology addresses design, measurement, bias, analysis, and reproducibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methohexital} Methohexital is an ultra-short-acting barbiturate anesthetic used for induction of general anesthesia and, in selected settings, procedural sedation. It depresses central nervous system activity and may cause respiratory or cardiovascular depression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methomyl} Methomyl is a carbamate insecticide that reversibly inhibits acetylcholinesterase. Poisoning produces cholinergic symptoms such as salivation, sweating, vomiting, bronchospasm, bradycardia, weakness, and respiratory compromise.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methotrexate} Methotrexate is an antimetabolite that inhibits dihydrofolate reductase and other folate-dependent pathways. It is used in cancer and selected autoimmune or inflammatory diseases; toxicity can include myelosuppression, mucositis, hepatotoxicity, pneumonitis, and teratogenicity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methotrimeprazine} Methotrimeprazine, also called levomepromazine, is a phenothiazine with antipsychotic, sedative, antiemetic, and analgesic properties. It is used in some countries for psychiatric illness and palliative-care symptoms and can cause sedation, hypotension, and anticholinergic effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methoxamine} Methoxamine is a predominantly alpha-adrenergic agonist that increases vascular tone and blood pressure. It has been used as a vasopressor for hypotension but is not a routine first-line agent in many current practice settings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methoxsalen} Methoxsalen is a psoralen that intercalates into DNA and, after exposure to UVA, forms cross-links that suppress abnormal lymphocyte or skin-cell activity. It is used with UVA phototherapy for selected skin diseases; photosensitivity and skin-cancer risk require precautions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methoxyamine hydrochloride} Methoxyamine hydrochloride is the hydrochloride salt of methoxyamine, a sympathomimetic compound with predominantly alpha-adrenergic activity. It has been used experimentally or historically as a vasopressor and pharmacologic probe.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methoxyflurane} Methoxyflurane is a volatile inhaled anesthetic formerly used for general anesthesia and, in low-dose formulations in some countries, short-term analgesia. Prolonged or high exposure can cause nephrotoxicity and other anesthetic adverse effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methoxyhydroxyphenylglycol} Methoxyhydroxyphenylglycol (MHPG) is a major metabolite of norepinephrine. Measurement in blood, urine, or cerebrospinal fluid has been used in research and selected neuroendocrine evaluations of sympathetic or central noradrenergic activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyclothiazide} Methyclothiazide is a thiazide diuretic that inhibits sodium and chloride reabsorption in the distal convoluted tubule. It is used for hypertension or edema and may cause hypokalemia, hyponatremia, hyperuricemia, and glucose intolerance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyl bromide} Methyl bromide, or bromomethane, is a volatile fumigant and alkylating chemical. Inhalation can cause severe neurologic, respiratory, renal, and cardiovascular toxicity, including delayed seizures and pulmonary edema.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyl chloride} Methyl chloride, or chloromethane, is a volatile industrial gas formerly used in refrigerants and other applications. High-level inhalation exposure can depress the central nervous system and cause headache, ataxia, seizures, or cardiac effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyl green} Methyl green is a cationic dye used in histology, often to stain DNA and nuclei. It is a laboratory reagent rather than a therapeutic drug, and handling should follow appropriate chemical-safety procedures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyl group} A methyl group is a chemical group consisting of one carbon atom bonded to three hydrogen atoms, written --CH$_3$. Methyl transfer reactions are important in metabolism, epigenetic regulation, and drug biotransformation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyl isocyanate} Methyl isocyanate is a highly volatile and reactive industrial chemical. Inhalation or eye and skin exposure can cause severe irritation, pulmonary edema, and delayed respiratory injury; it was the toxic agent in the Bhopal disaster.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyl methanesulfonate} Methyl methanesulfonate is an alkylating agent that methylates DNA and other cellular molecules. It is used experimentally to induce mutagenesis and is hazardous because of its genotoxic and carcinogenic potential.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyl parathion} Methyl parathion is an organophosphorus insecticide that inhibits acetylcholinesterase. Exposure can produce severe cholinergic poisoning with secretions, bronchospasm, bradycardia, weakness, seizures, and respiratory failure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylcellulose} methylcellulose is an anatomical or histologic term describing a body structure, tissue, or region; its clinical meaning depends on its location, function, and associated findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylcholanthrene} Methylcholanthrene is a polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbon used experimentally as a carcinogen. It can form reactive metabolites that damage DNA and is used in research on tumor initiation and chemical carcinogenesis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyldopa} Methyldopa is a centrally acting antihypertensive that is converted to an alpha-2 adrenergic agonist in the central nervous system. It lowers sympathetic outflow and is used especially in pregnancy-related hypertension; adverse effects include sedation, liver injury, and hemolytic anemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylene chloride} Methylene chloride, or dichloromethane, is a volatile industrial solvent. Inhalation can cause central nervous system depression and metabolism to carbon monoxide can produce tissue hypoxia; high exposure may be fatal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylguanidine} Methylguanidine is a guanidine compound formed during protein and amino-acid metabolism. It can accumulate in advanced kidney failure and is studied as a uremic toxin that may contribute to oxidative and cellular injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylmalonic aciduria} methylmalonic aciduria is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylmercury compounds} Methylmercury compounds are organic mercury compounds that can accumulate in aquatic food chains and cross the blood--brain and placental barriers. Exposure primarily injures the nervous system and can impair fetal neurodevelopment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylmethacrylate} Methyl methacrylate is a volatile monomer used to make acrylic plastics and dental or orthopedic bone cement. Vapors may irritate the eyes and airways, and liquid exposure can cause dermatitis or sensitization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylnaltrexone} Methylnaltrexone is a peripherally acting mu-opioid-receptor antagonist used to treat opioid-induced constipation when laxatives are insufficient. It generally preserves central analgesia but can cause abdominal pain, diarrhea, and rarely gastrointestinal perforation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylnaltrexone bromide} Methylnaltrexone bromide is the bromide salt of methylnaltrexone, a peripherally restricted opioid antagonist. It reverses opioid effects in the gastrointestinal tract and is used for opioid-induced constipation under indication-specific precautions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylnitrosourea} Methylnitrosourea is a potent alkylating and mutagenic compound used experimentally to induce DNA damage and tumors. It is not a therapeutic drug and requires strict laboratory handling because of its carcinogenicity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylobacteriaceae} methylobacteriaceae is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylobacterium} methylobacterium is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylphenidate} Methylphenidate is a central nervous system stimulant used primarily for attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder and narcolepsy. It increases catecholaminergic signaling and can cause appetite loss, insomnia, cardiovascular effects, misuse, and dependence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylprednisolone} Methylprednisolone is a synthetic glucocorticoid with anti-inflammatory and immunosuppressive effects. It is used for many inflammatory, allergic, autoimmune, and other conditions; prolonged or high-dose use can cause infection risk, hyperglycemia, osteoporosis, and adrenal suppression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyltestosterone} methyltestosterone is a diagnostic or laboratory method used to evaluate a clinical question; interpretation depends on the specimen or body site, method, reference range, and patient context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methylthioinosine} Methylthioinosine is a sulfur-containing purine nucleoside involved in cellular methylthio and purine metabolism. Its derivatives and related metabolites are studied in biochemical pathways and cancer biology rather than used routinely as medicines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{methyltransferase} A methyltransferase is an enzyme that transfers a methyl group from a donor, commonly S-adenosylmethionine, to DNA, RNA, proteins, lipids, or small molecules. Methylation can alter gene expression, protein activity, or metabolism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metolazone} Metolazone is a thiazide-like diuretic that inhibits sodium and chloride reabsorption in the distal nephron. It is used for hypertension and edema, often with a loop diuretic, and can cause significant electrolyte depletion and volume contraction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metolcarb} Metolcarb is a carbamate insecticide that can inhibit acetylcholinesterase. Exposure may cause cholinergic poisoning with excessive secretions, gastrointestinal symptoms, bronchospasm, bradycardia, weakness, or respiratory compromise.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metoprolol} metoprolol is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metric system} The metric system is a decimal system of measurement based on units such as the meter, kilogram, and liter. Modern medicine generally uses the International System of Units (SI), with conventional units retained for some measurements.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metronomic chemotherapy} metronomic chemotherapy is used in relation to metronomic chemotherapy during assessment, diagnosis, treatment, monitoring, or patient support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metschnikowia} metschnikowia identifies metschnikowia, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{metyrapone} Metyrapone inhibits adrenal 11-beta-hydroxylase, reducing cortisol synthesis and increasing upstream steroid precursors. It is used in selected tests of the hypothalamic--pituitary--adrenal axis and sometimes in treatment of hypercortisolism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mevinphos} Mevinphos is a highly toxic organophosphorus insecticide that inhibits acetylcholinesterase. Exposure can rapidly cause cholinergic crisis, including bronchorrhea, bronchospasm, muscle weakness, seizures, and respiratory failure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mexacarbate} Mexacarbate is a carbamate insecticide that interferes with acetylcholinesterase. Toxic exposure may produce muscarinic and nicotinic cholinergic symptoms and requires prompt decontamination and medical evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mexiletine} Mexiletine is an oral class IB antiarrhythmic and sodium-channel blocker used for selected ventricular arrhythmias and sometimes neuropathic pain. It can cause gastrointestinal and neurologic effects and may worsen or provoke arrhythmias in some patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Meyerozyma guilliermondii} Meyerozyma guilliermondii is a yeast formerly classified as Candida guilliermondii. It is usually an environmental or colonizing organism but can cause opportunistic bloodstream or other infections, particularly in immunocompromised patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mezlocillin} mezlocillin is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{micelle} micelle is an anatomical or histologic term describing a body structure, tissue, or region; its clinical meaning depends on its location, function, and associated findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{miconazole} miconazole is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microabscess} A microabscess is a small, localized collection of pus consisting of neutrophils, necrotic material, and microorganisms or inflammatory debris. It may occur in skin, glands, organs, or tissue affected by infection or inflammatory disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microarray analysis} Microarray analysis measures the abundance or hybridization of many nucleic-acid sequences simultaneously using an array of immobilized probes. It can assess gene expression, copy-number changes, or selected genetic variants.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microbacterium} microbacterium is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microbial biofilms} Microbial biofilms are organized communities of microorganisms attached to a surface and enclosed in a self-produced extracellular matrix. Biofilms can form on tissues and medical devices and often show increased tolerance to antimicrobials and host defenses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microbicide} A microbicide is a chemical or biological agent that kills microorganisms or inactivates them. The term may refer to disinfectants, topical agents, or investigational products intended to reduce sexual transmission of infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microbody} A microbody is a small membrane-bound cytoplasmic organelle, a term that includes peroxisomes and related organelles. Microbodies contain enzymes involved in oxidation, detoxification, and lipid metabolism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microchip site} A microchip site is the anatomical location where an implanted identification or monitoring microchip is placed, commonly in subcutaneous tissue. The site should be recorded for identification and assessed for migration, irritation, or infection when relevant.
