\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microcirculation} The microcirculation is blood flow through arterioles, capillaries, venules, and small connecting vessels. It regulates tissue perfusion, oxygen and nutrient exchange, fluid balance, and removal of metabolic waste.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micrococcaceae} Micrococcaceae is a family of generally gram-positive, catalase-positive cocci that includes Micrococcus and related genera. These organisms commonly inhabit skin and the environment, although some can cause opportunistic infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micrococcus} Micrococcus is a genus of aerobic, gram-positive cocci commonly found on skin and in the environment. These organisms are often harmless colonizers but can occasionally cause opportunistic infection, especially in people with medical devices or impaired immunity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micrococcus luteus} Micrococcus luteus is a gram-positive, aerobic coccus commonly present on human skin and in the environment. It is usually a contaminant or colonizer but can rarely cause bacteremia or device-associated infection in susceptible patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Micrococcus lylae} Micrococcus lylae is a gram-positive, aerobic coccus associated mainly with skin flora and environmental samples. Its recovery from a clinical specimen requires correlation with symptoms and the possibility of contamination or colonization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microcolon} Microcolon is an abnormally small-caliber colon, often identified on contrast imaging. It may result from reduced fetal intestinal use or obstruction and is associated with conditions such as small-left-colon syndrome, meconium ileus, or distal intestinal obstruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microcornea} Microcornea is an abnormally small cornea, usually defined in adults by a horizontal diameter below about 10 mm. It may be isolated or associated with microphthalmia, glaucoma, cataract, or genetic syndromes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microcurie} A microcurie ($\mu$Ci) is a unit of radioactivity equal to one-millionth of a curie, or approximately 37 kilobecquerels. It may be used to express the activity of radioactive tracers or sources.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microdialysis} Microdialysis is a sampling technique in which a semipermeable probe perfused with fluid collects small molecules from extracellular tissue fluid. It can monitor local concentrations of metabolites or drugs, including in the brain or subcutaneous tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microdissection} Microdissection is separation or isolation of very small tissue regions or structures under microscopic or highly magnified visualization. It is used in surgery, pathology, and molecular research to obtain selected cells or tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microelectrode} A microelectrode is a very small electrode used to record electrical activity from, or deliver stimulation to, individual cells, nerves, or small tissue regions. It is common in electrophysiology and neuroscience research.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microfibril} A microfibril is a fine, threadlike structural unit composed of proteins such as fibrillin or cellulose, depending on the tissue. In connective tissue, microfibrils help organize elastic fibers and provide extracellular support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microfilament} A microfilament is a thin cytoskeletal filament composed mainly of actin, about 7 nm in diameter. Microfilaments support cell shape, motility, contraction, cytokinesis, and intracellular transport.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microfilament proteins} microfilament proteins is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microfluidics} Microfluidics is the manipulation of very small volumes of fluid in channels or systems with dimensions typically in the micrometer range. It enables compact assays, cell sorting, diagnostics, and laboratory-on-a-chip devices.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microglandular adenosis} Microglandular adenosis is an uncommon benign proliferation of small glands in the breast, often associated with hormonal influences such as pregnancy or progestin use. It can mimic invasive carcinoma and is diagnosed histologically.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microglia} Microglia are resident immune cells of the central nervous system. They survey neural tissue, remove debris, and respond to injury or infection; persistent activation can contribute to neuroinflammation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microgliosis} Microgliosis is proliferation or increased activation of microglia in the central nervous system, usually in response to injury, infection, degeneration, or other inflammation. It is a histopathologic finding rather than a specific disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{micrognathism} Micrognathism is abnormal smallness of the jaw, usually referring to the mandible. It may be congenital or acquired and can affect feeding, dentition, airway patency, speech, or facial development.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microinjection} Microinjection is delivery of a very small volume of fluid into a cell, tissue, or other microscopic target using a fine needle or micropipette. It is used in assisted reproduction, experimental biology, and some therapeutic procedures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{micromole} A micromole ($\mu$mol) is an amount of substance equal to one-millionth of a mole, or approximately $6.022\times10^{17}$ entities. It is commonly used for small quantities and concentrations of biochemical substances.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microneme} A microneme is a small secretory organelle in many apicomplexan parasites. It releases proteins that support host-cell attachment, motility, and invasion during the parasite life cycle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{micronization} Micronization is a process that reduces particles to micrometer-scale dimensions, often to increase surface area and improve dissolution or absorption of a drug. The resulting particle size and formulation still determine clinical performance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{micronutrient} A micronutrient is a vitamin or mineral required in small amounts for normal growth, metabolism, and physiologic function. Deficiency or excess can cause disease, and requirements vary with age, pregnancy, illness, and other factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microorganism} A microorganism is a microscopic living organism, such as a bacterium, fungus, protozoan, or archaeon. Some are harmless or beneficial, while others cause infection; viruses are acellular infectious agents commonly studied alongside microorganisms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microorganism genetics} microorganism genetics is a biological organism or infectious agent; its clinical importance depends on the species, host, site, exposure, and evidence of infection or colonization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{micropexophagy} Micropexophagy is a form of selective autophagy in which a cell degrades peroxisomes by enclosing them in an autophagic structure and delivering them to the vacuole or lysosome. It is studied mainly in fungal and cellular biology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microphthalmos} Microphthalmos, or microphthalmia, is congenital underdevelopment resulting in an abnormally small eye. It may be isolated or associated with orbital, retinal, lens, genetic, or systemic abnormalities and can substantially impair vision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{micropsia} Micropsia is a visual distortion in which objects appear smaller than they are. It may result from retinal disease, migraine, seizures, drugs, or other neurologic conditions and is sometimes called an ``Alice in Wonderland'' visual symptom.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microradiography} Microradiography is radiographic imaging of very small specimens or structures, often using high magnification and fine-detail techniques. It can demonstrate microarchitecture in bone, tissue, teeth, or experimental samples.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microscopic} Microscopic means too small to be seen clearly with the unaided eye or relating to observation with a microscope. In clinical reports it may describe cells, organisms, crystals, or tissue features identified by microscopy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microscopy} Microscopy is examination of cells, tissues, organisms, or materials with a microscope. Light, fluorescence, electron, and other microscopy methods provide different information about structure and composition.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsome} A microsome is a small membrane vesicle formed from fragments of endoplasmic reticulum when cells are disrupted and centrifuged. Microsomes are laboratory preparations used to study membrane enzymes, including drug-metabolizing cytochrome P450 systems.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microspectrophotometry} Microspectrophotometry measures the absorption or emission spectrum of a microscopic region or individual cell. It can quantify pigments, nucleic acids, proteins, or other chromophores in small biological samples.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsphere} A microsphere is a small spherical particle, often made of polymer, glass, or another material, used in drug delivery, imaging, embolization, or laboratory assays. Its size, composition, and surface properties determine its biologic behavior.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microspherophakia} Microspherophakia is an abnormally small, spherical crystalline lens. It can occur in inherited disorders such as Weill--Marchesani syndrome and may cause lens displacement, pupillary block, angle-closure glaucoma, or reduced vision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsporidia} microsporidia identifies microsporidia, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsporidiosis} microsporidiosis identifies microsporidiosis, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsporum} microsporum identifies microsporum, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsporum audouinii} microsporum audouinii identifies microsporum audouinii, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsporum distortum} microsporum distortum identifies microsporum distortum, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsporum equinum} microsporum equinum identifies microsporum equinum, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsporum ferrugineum} microsporum ferrugineum identifies microsporum ferrugineum, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsporum gypseum} microsporum gypseum identifies microsporum gypseum, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microstomia} Microstomia is abnormal narrowing or smallness of the mouth opening. It may be congenital or result from scarring, burns, surgery, trauma, or connective-tissue disease and can impair eating, speech, oral hygiene, and dental treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microsurgery} microsurgery is used in relation to microsurgery during assessment, diagnosis, treatment, monitoring, or patient support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microtechnology} Microtechnology is the design and manufacture of devices or structures with micrometer-scale features. Medical applications include microfluidic diagnostics, sensors, drug-delivery systems, and minimally invasive instruments.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microtubule} A microtubule is a hollow cytoskeletal polymer made of tubulin dimers. Microtubules maintain cell shape, organize intracellular transport, form the mitotic spindle, and support cilia and flagella.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microtubule bundle} A microtubule bundle is an organized group of parallel or otherwise coordinated microtubules within a cell. Bundles provide structural support and guide transport or movement, including in axons, cilia, and dividing cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microvillus} A microvillus is a microscopic, actin-supported projection of a cell membrane that increases surface area. Microvilli are abundant on intestinal epithelial cells, where they support nutrient absorption, and on renal tubular cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microvolt} A microvolt ($\mu$V) is one-millionth of a volt. It is used to express small bioelectric signals, such as those recorded by electroencephalography, electromyography, electrocardiography, or evoked-potential testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microwave therapy} microwave therapy is used in relation to microwave therapy during assessment, diagnosis, treatment, monitoring, or patient support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{microwell plate} A microwell plate is a laboratory plate containing an array of small wells used to hold separate samples or reactions. It supports high-throughput assays, cell culture, immunoassays, and biochemical testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{midazolam} Midazolam is a short-acting benzodiazepine used for procedural sedation, anesthesia, and treatment of acute seizures in appropriate settings. It enhances GABA-A receptor activity and can cause profound sedation and respiratory depression, especially with opioids or other depressants.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{midazolam hydrochloride} Midazolam hydrochloride is the hydrochloride salt of the short-acting benzodiazepine midazolam. It is used for procedural sedation, anesthesia, and selected seizure emergencies and can cause dose-related sedation, hypotension, and respiratory depression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{middle insomnia} Middle insomnia is repeated or prolonged awakening during the middle portion of the sleep period, often with difficulty returning to sleep. It may occur with stress, mood disorders, sleep apnea, pain, substances, medications, or other sleep disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{midodrine} Midodrine is an oral prodrug whose active metabolite stimulates alpha-1 adrenergic receptors and increases vascular tone. It is used for symptomatic orthostatic hypotension and may cause supine hypertension, piloerection, urinary retention, or scalp tingling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{midomafetamine} Midomafetamine is another name for 3,4-methylenedioxymethamphetamine (MDMA), a psychoactive amphetamine derivative. It is being studied in controlled psychotherapy-assisted research but can cause hyperthermia, hyponatremia, cardiovascular toxicity, serotonin toxicity, and misuse.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{midostaurin} Midostaurin is a multikinase inhibitor used with chemotherapy for newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia with a susceptible FLT3 mutation and for advanced systemic mastocytosis. It can cause nausea, cytopenias, infection, and other treatment-related toxicities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mifepristone} Mifepristone is a progesterone-receptor antagonist and, at higher doses, a glucocorticoid-receptor antagonist. It is used with misoprostol for medication abortion under applicable protocols and for selected cases of hyperglycemia associated with Cushing syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mikulicz disease} Mikulicz disease describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{milatuzumab} milatuzumab is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mild} Mild describes relatively low intensity, severity, or extent of a symptom, sign, disease, or abnormality. The meaning depends on the condition and the clinical scale used and does not by itself indicate that no treatment or evaluation is needed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mile} A mile is a unit of distance equal to 1,609.344 meters. It may be used in descriptions of walking or running distance, although scientific and clinical measurements generally use SI units.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{miliaria} Miliaria is a skin eruption caused by blockage of eccrine sweat ducts, leading to sweat retention and inflammation. It includes heat-rash forms such as miliaria crystallina, rubra, and profunda and is promoted by heat and humidity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{milieu therapy} milieu therapy is used in relation to milieu therapy during assessment, diagnosis, treatment, monitoring, or patient support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{military personnel} Military personnel are members of the armed forces, including active-duty, reserve, and related service members. Their health care may address occupational exposures, injuries, infectious risks, combat-related trauma, and deployment-related mental-health conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{milk} Milk is a nutrient-containing fluid produced by mammary glands after childbirth or, in other mammals, during lactation. It contains water, proteins, fats, carbohydrates, vitamins, minerals, and immune factors; composition varies by species and processing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{millicurie} A millicurie (mCi) is one-thousandth of a curie, equal to approximately 37 megabecquerels. It is used to express the activity of radioactive sources and some radiopharmaceutical doses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{milliliter} A milliliter (mL) is a unit of volume equal to one-thousandth of a liter and, for ordinary water-based measurements, approximately one cubic centimeter. It is widely used for liquid medicines, specimens, and laboratory solutions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{millimeter} A millimeter (mm) is a unit of length equal to one-thousandth of a meter. It is commonly used to report small anatomic dimensions, lesion sizes, imaging findings, and pressure differences in some clinical contexts.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{millimole} A millimole (mmol) is one-thousandth of a mole, the amount of a substance containing a specified number of elementary entities. It is commonly used to report laboratory concentrations and electrolyte quantities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{milliosmole} A milliosmole (mOsm) is one-thousandth of an osmole, a unit reflecting the number of osmotically active particles in a solution. Osmolality and osmolarity are commonly reported in mOsm/kg or mOsm/L.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{millisecond} A millisecond (ms) is one-thousandth of a second. It is used to measure brief physiologic events, reaction times, nerve conduction intervals, and timing of electrical or imaging signals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{milliunit} A milliunit (mU) is one-thousandth of a unit of biologic or enzymatic activity. Because a unit is defined according to the substance or assay, the clinical meaning of a milliunit depends on the specific test or medication.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{millivolt} A millivolt (mV) is one-thousandth of a volt. It is commonly used to express electrical potentials recorded from the heart, brain, muscles, nerves, and other biologic tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mineral} A mineral is an inorganic element required or utilized by the body, such as calcium, iron, sodium, potassium, magnesium, or zinc. Minerals support structure, fluid balance, nerve and muscle function, enzyme activity, and other processes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mineral oil} Mineral oil is a purified mixture of liquid hydrocarbons derived from petroleum. It is used in some topical products, cosmetics, and laxatives; aspiration of liquid mineral oil can cause lipoid pneumonia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mineralocorticoid} A mineralocorticoid is a steroid hormone that regulates sodium, potassium, and extracellular-fluid balance, chiefly through receptors in the renal distal nephron. Aldosterone is the principal endogenous mineralocorticoid; excess causes hypertension and hypokalemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{minisatellite repeat} A minisatellite repeat is a tandemly repeated DNA sequence with repeat units commonly ranging from about 10 to 100 base pairs. Variation in repeat number can serve as a genetic marker and, in some loci, influence gene regulation or disease risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{minor response} A minor response is a limited improvement after treatment that does not meet the predefined criteria for a partial or complete response. The exact threshold depends on the disease, study protocol, and outcome being measured.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{minority health} Minority health concerns the health status, health determinants, access, quality of care, and outcomes of populations that experience social, economic, cultural, or structural marginalization. Differences in outcomes often reflect modifiable inequities rather than inherent biology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{minoxidil} Minoxidil is a potassium-channel opener and vasodilator used topically for some forms of hair loss and orally, with other therapy, for severe hypertension. Topical use may cause irritation or unwanted hair growth; oral use can cause fluid retention, tachycardia, and hypertrichosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{minretumomab} minretumomab is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{miosis disorder} miosis disorder describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mirage syndrome} mirage syndrome describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mirex} Mirex is a persistent organochlorine insecticide and industrial chemical. It bioaccumulates and is environmentally hazardous; exposure has been associated with toxic effects in experimental studies and is controlled or prohibited in many jurisdictions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mirtazapine} Mirtazapine is a noradrenergic and specific serotonergic antidepressant used for major depressive disorder. It commonly causes sedation and increased appetite or weight and can rarely cause neutropenia or other serious adverse effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{misalignment} Misalignment is abnormal positioning or orientation of two or more structures relative to their intended relationship. It may affect bones, joints, teeth, eyes, implants, or medical devices and can impair function or healing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{misdiagnosis} Misdiagnosis is assignment of an incorrect diagnosis or failure to recognize the correct condition. It can lead to inappropriate treatment, delayed care, avoidable harm, or unnecessary testing and may result from atypical presentation, incomplete information, or system factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mismatch repair} Mismatch repair is a DNA-repair pathway that recognizes and corrects incorrectly paired bases or small insertion--deletion loops generated during replication. Defects in mismatch-repair genes cause microsatellite instability and increase risk of several cancers, including Lynch-syndrome cancers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{misonidazole} misonidazole is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{misoprostol} Misoprostol is a synthetic prostaglandin E$_1$ analog used to reduce NSAID-related gastric-ulcer risk and, in specified protocols, to induce labor, manage miscarriage, or terminate pregnancy with mifepristone. It commonly causes uterine contractions, cramping, diarrhea, and bleeding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{missed dose} A missed dose is a scheduled dose of a medicine that was not taken or administered. The appropriate response depends on the drug and timing; doubling a dose can be dangerous, so the product instructions or a clinician or pharmacist should guide the next dose.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{missense mutation} A missense mutation is a DNA sequence change that substitutes one amino acid for another in a protein. Its effect may be harmful, beneficial, or neutral depending on the affected residue, protein function, and genetic context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mite} A mite is a tiny arachnid. Some mites cause skin infestation or dermatitis, such as scabies mites, while others trigger allergic disease through inhaled or contact allergens; a few can transmit infectious agents.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mithramycin} mithramycin is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitigate} To mitigate means to lessen the severity, impact, or likelihood of a symptom, risk, injury, or disease complication. Mitigation may involve prevention, treatment, environmental changes, or supportive care.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitobronitol} Mitobronitol is an alkylating antineoplastic compound formerly investigated or used in selected hematologic malignancies. Its cytotoxic effects can damage DNA and cause myelosuppression and other treatment-related toxicities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitochondrial cristae} Mitochondrial cristae are folds of the inner mitochondrial membrane that increase surface area for the electron-transport chain and ATP synthase. Their structure supports oxidative phosphorylation and changes with cellular energy demands.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitochondrial DNA} mitochondrial DNA is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitochondrial fission} Mitochondrial fission is division of one mitochondrion into two or more mitochondria. It helps distribute mitochondria, remove damaged portions through mitophagy, and respond to cell-cycle or metabolic signals; excessive fission may contribute to disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitochondrial fusion} Mitochondrial fusion is joining of mitochondria into a connected network. It permits exchange of mitochondrial contents and helps maintain bioenergetic function and genome integrity; fusion and fission are normally balanced dynamically.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitochondrial matrix} The mitochondrial matrix is the protein-rich compartment enclosed by the inner mitochondrial membrane. It contains mitochondrial DNA, ribosomes, and enzymes for the citric-acid cycle, fatty-acid oxidation, and other metabolic processes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitochondrial membrane} The mitochondrial membrane system consists of an outer membrane and a highly folded inner membrane separated by an intermembrane space. It controls transport and houses the respiratory chain and ATP-producing machinery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitochondrial ribosome} A mitochondrial ribosome is a ribosome within mitochondria that translates proteins encoded by mitochondrial DNA. Its structure differs from cytosolic ribosomes and defects in mitochondrial translation can cause energy-production disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitochondrial swelling} mitochondrial swelling describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitochondrion} A mitochondrion is a membrane-bound organelle that generates much of a cell’s ATP through oxidative phosphorylation. It also participates in apoptosis, calcium handling, metabolism, and production or regulation of reactive oxygen species.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitogen} A mitogen is a substance that stimulates a cell, especially a lymphocyte, to enter the cell cycle and proliferate. Mitogens include growth factors, lectins, and other signals that activate proliferative pathways.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitoguazone} Mitoguazone, also called methylglyoxal bis(guanylhydrazone), is an experimental antineoplastic agent that interferes with polyamine metabolism and cell proliferation. It has been investigated in hematologic and other cancers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitolactol} Mitolactol is an alkylating antineoplastic compound formerly used or investigated in cancer treatment. It damages DNA and can cause cytotoxic effects such as myelosuppression and gastrointestinal toxicity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitomycin} mitomycin is a medication or pharmacologic agent; its indication, dose, interactions, contraindications, and adverse effects depend on the specific product and patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitomycins} Mitomycins are antitumor antibiotics that become activated in cells and cross-link DNA, inhibiting replication and transcription. Mitomycin C is the best-known member and is used in selected cancers and ophthalmic or surgical applications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitophagy} Mitophagy is selective autophagic removal of damaged, excess, or otherwise targeted mitochondria. It maintains mitochondrial quality and cellular homeostasis; impaired or excessive mitophagy is implicated in some neurodegenerative and metabolic disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitosporic fungi} mitosporic fungi is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitotane} Mitotane is an adrenolytic drug used for selected adrenocortical carcinomas and to suppress excess adrenal steroid production. It can cause gastrointestinal and neurologic effects, adrenal insufficiency, and numerous drug interactions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitotic anaphase} Mitotic anaphase is the stage of mitosis in which sister chromatids separate and move toward opposite poles of the cell. Accurate spindle attachment and chromosome segregation are required for formation of genetically similar daughter cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitotic metaphase} Mitotic metaphase is the stage of mitosis in which duplicated chromosomes align at the cell equator with sister kinetochores attached to opposite spindle poles. A checkpoint helps prevent premature chromosome separation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitotic prophase} Mitotic prophase is the early stage of mitosis in which chromatin condenses into visible chromosomes, the nucleolus disappears, and the mitotic spindle begins to form. In late prophase or prometaphase, the nuclear envelope breaks down.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitotic recombination} Mitotic recombination is exchange or rearrangement of DNA between homologous chromosomes during or around mitotic cell division. It can repair DNA or generate loss of heterozygosity and is relevant to tumor evolution and genetic disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitotic telophase} Mitotic telophase is the stage after chromosome separation when chromosomes reach opposite poles, decondense, and become enclosed in newly forming nuclear envelopes. Cytokinesis usually follows or overlaps with telophase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitoxantrone} mitoxantrone describes an infectious or toxic process involving the site or exposure named by the term. Diagnosis considers the cause, route of exposure, affected tissues, severity, and host response.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitral atresia} Mitral atresia is congenital absence or complete closure of the mitral valve opening, preventing normal blood flow from the left atrium to the left ventricle. It is a critical congenital heart defect requiring early specialist management and usually associated with other left-heart abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitral valvuloplasty} mitral valvuloplasty is used in relation to mitral valvuloplasty during assessment, diagnosis, treatment, monitoring, or patient support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitsuokella} mitsuokella identifies mitsuokella, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mitsuokella multacida} mitsuokella multacida identifies mitsuokella multacida, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mixed glioma} Mixed glioma is an older term for a glial tumor showing features of more than one glial lineage, historically including oligoastrocytoma. Modern classification relies mainly on molecular findings and generally assigns such tumors to a more specific entity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mobile health unit} A mobile health unit is a vehicle or transportable facility equipped to provide health services at changing locations. It may deliver screening, vaccination, primary care, diagnostic testing, emergency support, or outreach to underserved communities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mobiluncus} mobiluncus identifies mobiluncus, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mobiluncus curtisii} mobiluncus curtisii identifies mobiluncus curtisii, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mobiluncus mulieris} mobiluncus mulieris identifies mobiluncus mulieris, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mocetinostat} Mocetinostat is an investigational histone deacetylase inhibitor studied as an antineoplastic agent. By altering histone acetylation and gene expression, it may inhibit tumor-cell growth; clinical use depends on regulatory approval and indication.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{modality} A modality is a type or method of treatment, diagnostic test, imaging technique, or therapeutic intervention. Examples include a medication, surgery, radiation therapy, ultrasound, or magnetic resonance imaging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moderation} Moderation is reduction or control of the intensity, amount, or severity of a process, symptom, behavior, or treatment effect. In clinical research, a moderator is a factor that changes the strength or direction of an association.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moellerella} moellerella identifies moellerella, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moellerella wisconsensis} moellerella wisconsensis identifies moellerella wisconsensis, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mogibacterium} mogibacterium is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molar} Molar refers to a back tooth used chiefly for grinding food; humans normally have premolars and molars in each quadrant. In chemistry, molar describes a concentration or quantity expressed per mole, such as molar mass or molarity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molecular biology} Molecular biology studies the molecular basis of biological processes, including the structure and function of DNA, RNA, proteins, and their interactions. It underlies genetics, biotechnology, and many diagnostic and therapeutic methods.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molecular chaperone} A molecular chaperone is a protein that assists other proteins with folding, assembly, transport, or refolding without becoming part of the final structure. Chaperones can also help prevent or resolve protein aggregation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molecular diagnosis} Molecular diagnosis uses analysis of DNA, RNA, proteins, or other molecular markers to identify a disease, pathogen, inherited variant, tumor subtype, or treatment-relevant characteristic. Results require appropriate assay validation and clinical interpretation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molecular dynamic} Molecular dynamics is a computational method that simulates movement and interactions of atoms or molecules over time using physical force fields. It is used to study biomolecular structure, flexibility, binding, and drug interactions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molecular imaging} molecular imaging is a diagnostic or laboratory method used to evaluate a clinical question; interpretation depends on the specimen or body site, method, reference range, and patient context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molecular medicine} Molecular medicine applies molecular and cellular biology to understand, diagnose, prevent, and treat disease. It includes genomics, precision oncology, molecular biomarkers, targeted therapy, and other mechanism-based approaches.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molecular probe} A molecular probe is a labeled or otherwise detectable molecule designed to bind a particular nucleic-acid sequence, protein, metabolite, or other target. Probes are used in imaging, assays, microscopy, and molecular diagnostics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molecular response} A molecular response is a measurable change in a molecular marker, such as a pathogen load, oncogenic transcript, mutation burden, or biomarker, after disease progression or treatment. Its definition and clinical significance depend on the disease and assay.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molecule} molecule is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mollusca} mollusca identifies mollusca, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molluscipoxvirus} molluscipoxvirus is a virus or viral group classified by its genetic and biologic features. Clinical importance depends on the strain, host, route of transmission, tissue tropism, and ability to cause disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molt} A molt is shedding and replacement of an outer covering, such as the exoskeleton of an arthropod or the skin, feathers, or hair of an animal. The process is controlled by developmental and hormonal signals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{molybdenum} Molybdenum is an essential trace element incorporated into enzymes involved in sulfite, purine, and aldehyde metabolism. Deficiency is rare, while excessive exposure can be toxic; clinical significance depends on the chemical form and dose.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mometasone} Mometasone is a potent synthetic glucocorticoid used topically or by inhalation for inflammatory skin or airway disease. It reduces local inflammation but may cause skin atrophy, oral candidiasis, adrenal effects, or other corticosteroid adverse effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mometasone furoate} Mometasone furoate is the furoate ester of the corticosteroid mometasone. Depending on formulation, it is used for inflammatory skin disease, allergic rhinitis, or asthma; local and systemic corticosteroid precautions apply.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monascus} monascus identifies monascus, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monascus ruber} monascus ruber identifies monascus ruber, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monensin} Monensin is a polyether ionophore antibiotic used mainly in veterinary and agricultural settings to alter rumen fermentation or prevent coccidiosis. It can be toxic to animals and humans, especially through effects on cardiac and skeletal muscle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mongolian spot} A Mongolian spot, now preferably called congenital dermal melanocytosis, is a flat blue-gray patch caused by melanocytes in the dermis, usually present at birth. It is benign and often fades during childhood but should be documented to distinguish it from bruising.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monilethrix} Monilethrix is an inherited hair-shaft disorder in which hair has regularly spaced narrow segments and breaks easily, producing a beaded appearance. It commonly causes short, brittle scalp hair and may involve follicles elsewhere.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monitoring device} A monitoring device measures and displays or records a physiologic variable, such as heart rhythm, oxygen saturation, blood pressure, glucose, temperature, or respiratory rate. Accuracy, calibration, and clinical context determine how results should be interpreted.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monkeypox} Monkeypox, now generally called mpox, is a zoonotic infection caused by monkeypox virus, an orthopoxvirus. It can cause fever, lymphadenopathy, and a characteristic rash or lesions and spreads through close contact, including skin-to-skin or respiratory contact.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monobactams} Monobactams are beta-lactam antibiotics containing a single beta-lactam ring, rather than the fused rings of penicillins or cephalosporins. Aztreonam is the principal clinical example and primarily targets aerobic gram-negative bacteria.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monoblast count} A monoblast count is the number or proportion of monoblasts, immature precursors of monocytes, in a blood or bone-marrow sample. Increased blasts may support evaluation for acute myeloid leukemia or another marrow disorder and require expert morphologic or immunophenotypic interpretation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monocrotophos} Monocrotophos is a highly toxic organophosphorus insecticide that inhibits acetylcholinesterase. Exposure can cause acute cholinergic poisoning with secretions, bronchospasm, vomiting, bradycardia, weakness, seizures, and respiratory failure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monocytopenia} Monocytopenia is an abnormally low number of circulating monocytes. It may occur with bone-marrow failure or suppression, chemotherapy, infection, corticosteroid use, or other disorders and is interpreted with the complete blood count and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monocytosis} Monocytosis is an increased absolute number of circulating monocytes. It may be reactive to infection or inflammation or associated with a hematologic neoplasm; persistent or marked monocytosis requires appropriate evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monogenic obesity} Monogenic obesity is severe or early-onset obesity caused primarily by a pathogenic variant in a single gene involved in appetite, energy balance, or leptin--melanocortin signaling. Genetic evaluation may guide counseling and selected targeted treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monograph} A monograph is a detailed, authoritative document devoted to one subject. In health care it may describe a drug’s identity, quality, pharmacology, indications, dosing, safety, or regulatory standards.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monoiodotyrosine} Monoiodotyrosine is an iodinated tyrosine residue formed during thyroid-hormone synthesis. Coupling of iodinated tyrosine residues within thyroglobulin contributes to production of thyroxine and triiodothyronine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monokines} Monokines are cytokines produced chiefly by monocytes and macrophages, including mediators such as tumor necrosis factor and interleukin-1. They regulate inflammation, immune responses, fever, and communication among cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monomer} A monomer is a relatively small molecule that can chemically bond with other monomers to form a polymer. Biological examples include amino acids and nucleotides; some industrial monomers can irritate tissues or be toxic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monomethylhydrazine} Monomethylhydrazine is a volatile, corrosive, highly toxic chemical used as a rocket propellant. Exposure can injure the eyes, skin, lungs, liver, and nervous system and may cause seizures or systemic poisoning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mononegavirales} mononegavirales is a virus or viral group classified by its genetic and biologic features. Clinical importance depends on the strain, host, route of transmission, tissue tropism, and ability to cause disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mononeuropathy} Mononeuropathy is dysfunction or injury of a single peripheral nerve. Common causes include compression, trauma, ischemia, inflammation, and metabolic disease; symptoms follow the nerve’s distribution and may include pain, numbness, weakness, or autonomic changes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mononuclear leukocyte} A mononuclear leukocyte is a white blood cell with a single, non-lobed nucleus, chiefly a lymphocyte or monocyte. These cells participate in adaptive immunity, innate immune responses, antigen presentation, and inflammation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monosaccharide} A monosaccharide is a single-unit carbohydrate that cannot be hydrolyzed into a simpler sugar. Glucose, fructose, and galactose are examples and serve as energy sources or building blocks for larger carbohydrates.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monoterpenes} Monoterpenes are terpenoid compounds built from two isoprene units and containing 10 carbon atoms. They occur widely in plant essential oils and may have aromatic, biologic, or toxic effects depending on the compound and exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{monozygotic twin} A monozygotic twin develops when one fertilized egg divides into two embryos. The twins share nearly identical nuclear genomes but can differ through developmental, epigenetic, mitochondrial, and environmental factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{montelukast} montelukast is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{montelukast sodium} Montelukast sodium is the sodium salt of montelukast, a leukotriene-receptor antagonist used for asthma prevention and allergic rhinitis. It is not a rescue bronchodilator and carries an important warning for possible neuropsychiatric adverse effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mood disorder} mood disorder describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mood swing} A mood swing is a noticeable change in emotional state or affect over time. Occasional changes can be normal, while frequent, severe, or impairing swings may occur with mood disorders, substance use, medical illness, medications, or sleep disruption.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mopidamol} Mopidamol is a phosphodiesterase inhibitor that inhibits platelet aggregation. It was formerly investigated or used as an antineoplastic and is not a routine contemporary treatment in most clinical settings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MOPP protocol} The MOPP protocol is a chemotherapy regimen consisting of mechlorethamine, vincristine (Oncovin), procarbazine, and prednisone. It was historically used for Hodgkin lymphoma and has largely been replaced by regimens with different efficacy and toxicity profiles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moral} Moral refers to principles or judgments concerning right and wrong conduct. In health care, moral reasoning may inform ethical decisions but is distinct from clinical diagnosis and from legally enforceable rules.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morale} Morale is the confidence, motivation, and sense of shared purpose within a person or group. It can affect teamwork and occupational well-being but is not itself a specific medical diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moraxella} moraxella identifies moraxella, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moraxella atlantae} moraxella atlantae identifies moraxella atlantae, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\medterm{moraxella bovis} moraxella bovis identifies moraxella bovis, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\medterm{moraxella canis} moraxella canis identifies moraxella canis, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\medterm{moraxella lacunata} moraxella lacunata identifies moraxella lacunata, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\medterm{moraxella oblonga} moraxella oblonga identifies moraxella oblonga, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moraxellaceae} moraxellaceae identifies moraxellaceae, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morbillivirus} morbillivirus is a virus or viral group classified by its genetic and biologic features. Clinical importance depends on the strain, host, route of transmission, tissue tropism, and ability to cause disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morcellation} morcellation is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morganella} morganella identifies morganella, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morganella morganii} morganella morganii identifies morganella morganii, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morning} Morning is the early part of the day after waking or sunrise. In clinical descriptions, timing in the morning can help characterize symptoms, medication effects, hormone patterns, sleep disorders, or the timing of diagnostic measurements.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morphinans} Morphinans are a group of chemical compounds with a fused-ring structure related to morphine. The class includes opioid analgesics and other compounds with varying receptor activity, potency, and clinical effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morphine} Morphine is a potent opioid analgesic used for selected moderate-to-severe pain and palliative-care indications. It activates opioid receptors and can cause respiratory depression, sedation, constipation, nausea, tolerance, dependence, and overdose.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morphine sulfate} Morphine sulfate is a salt formulation of the opioid analgesic morphine. It is available in immediate- and extended-release preparations for selected pain indications and carries risks of respiratory depression, misuse, dependence, and fatal overdose.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morphogenesis} morphogenesis is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morpholino} A morpholino is a synthetic antisense oligomer with a morpholine-ring backbone that binds complementary RNA sequences and alters splicing or translation. Morpholinos are used mainly in research and selected experimental applications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morphologic response} A morphologic response is a treatment-related change in the visible or microscopic structure of a lesion, tumor, tissue, or cell population. It may include shrinkage, necrosis, differentiation, or altered cellular features and is interpreted using disease-specific criteria.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{morula} A morula is an early embryo consisting of a compact ball of blastomeres formed after several cleavage divisions. It develops into a blastocyst before implantation in the uterine lining.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{motexafin gadolinium} Motexafin gadolinium is an experimental texaphyrin compound designed to catalyze redox reactions and increase oxidative stress in tumor cells. It was investigated as a radiosensitizer and antineoplastic agent but is not a routine standard therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{motexafin lutetium} Motexafin lutetium is an investigational texaphyrin compound containing lutetium that can participate in redox chemistry and has been studied for photodynamic or other antineoplastic applications. It is not a routinely used approved treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{motion perception} Motion perception is the ability of the visual, vestibular, and proprioceptive systems to detect and interpret movement of the body or surrounding objects. Disturbance may cause vertigo, oscillopsia, imbalance, or visual-motion sensitivity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{motivation} Motivation is the set of processes that initiate, direct, and sustain goal-directed behavior. Reduced or excessive motivation may occur with mood disorders, neurologic disease, substance use, medication effects, or other conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{motor aphasia} Motor aphasia, often called expressive or Broca aphasia, is impaired ability to produce spoken or written language despite relatively preserved motivation to communicate. It commonly follows injury to dominant frontal language networks, especially from stroke.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{motor delay} Motor delay is slower-than-expected acquisition of gross- or fine-motor skills in a child. It may reflect neuromuscular, developmental, genetic, sensory, environmental, or systemic causes and warrants age-appropriate developmental assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{motor stereotypy} A motor stereotypy is a repetitive, patterned, seemingly purposeless movement, such as hand flapping, rocking, or body waving. It may occur in typical development or with autism, intellectual disability, neurologic disease, or other conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{motor tic} A motor tic is a sudden, brief, repetitive movement such as blinking, facial grimacing, or shoulder shrugging. Tics are often temporarily suppressible and may occur in transient tic disorder, persistent tic disorder, or Tourette syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mouth diseases} mouth diseases describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mouth neoplasm} mouth neoplasm describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Movement} Movement is a change in position of the body or a body part produced by coordinated activity of the nervous system, muscles, and joints. Abnormal movement may be excessive, reduced, involuntary, poorly coordinated, or restricted.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{movement disorders} movement disorders describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moxalactam} Moxalactam is a beta-lactam antibiotic of the oxacephem group with activity against many gram-negative bacteria and some gram-positive organisms. It is rarely used today and may cause allergic reactions, diarrhea, or bleeding related to impaired coagulation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moxibustion} Moxibustion is a traditional practice in which dried mugwort (moxa) is burned near or on acupuncture points to apply heat. Evidence for clinical benefit varies by condition, and burns, smoke exposure, and allergic reactions are possible harms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{moxifloxacin} moxifloxacin is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mrna precursor} mrna precursor is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mu-Chain disease} mu-Chain disease describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucin} Mucin is a high-molecular-weight glycoprotein that forms mucus and helps lubricate and protect epithelial surfaces. Abnormal mucin production or accumulation occurs in some respiratory, gastrointestinal, and neoplastic diseases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucinous} Mucinous means containing, producing, or resembling mucin. In pathology, the term describes lesions or tumors with extracellular or intracellular mucin and must be interpreted with the organ, architecture, and other histologic features.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucinous adenocarcinoma} mucinous adenocarcinoma describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucinous cystadenoma} A mucinous cystadenoma is a usually benign cystic epithelial tumor that produces mucin, commonly arising in the ovary or pancreas. Large lesions may cause pain or pressure, and pathology is needed to distinguish benign from borderline or malignant mucinous tumors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucoepidermoid carcinoma} mucoepidermoid carcinoma describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucolipidoses} mucolipidoses is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucopolysaccharidosis VII} Mucopolysaccharidosis VII, or Sly syndrome, is a lysosomal storage disorder caused by deficient beta-glucuronidase activity. Accumulation of glycosaminoglycans can cause skeletal, connective-tissue, visceral, developmental, and sometimes neurologic abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucor} mucor identifies mucor, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucor ramosissimus} mucor ramosissimus identifies mucor ramosissimus, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucorales} mucorales identifies mucorales, a biological taxon or infectious-disease concept whose clinical importance depends on the organism, host, and site involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucosal immunity} Mucosal immunity is immune defense at surfaces lining the respiratory, gastrointestinal, genitourinary, and other tracts. It includes epithelial barriers, mucus, secretory IgA, antimicrobial molecules, innate cells, and locally regulated adaptive responses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucosal infection} mucosal infection describes an infectious or toxic process involving the site or exposure named by the term. Diagnosis considers the cause, route of exposure, affected tissues, severity, and host response.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mucus} Mucus is a viscoelastic secretion containing water, mucin glycoproteins, salts, antimicrobial factors, and cellular material. It lubricates and protects epithelial surfaces and traps particles and microorganisms for clearance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mulibrey nanism} Mulibrey nanism is a rare inherited multisystem disorder, usually caused by biallelic variants in \textit{TRIM37}. It may cause severe growth restriction, characteristic facial features, pericardial or hepatic disease, insulin resistance, and other organ involvement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muller cells} muller cells is an anatomical or histologic term describing a body structure, tissue, or region; its clinical meaning depends on its location, function, and associated findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multicenter study} A multicenter study is a clinical or research investigation conducted at two or more sites under a coordinated protocol. It can improve enrollment and generalizability but requires harmonized methods, quality control, and site-specific oversight.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multi-drug resistance} Multidrug resistance is resistance of a microorganism, cancer, or other disease process to multiple drugs that would otherwise be active. Mechanisms include target changes, drug inactivation, reduced uptake, efflux, and altered repair or survival pathways.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multienzyme complexes} multienzyme complexes is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multimodal imaging} multimodal imaging is a diagnostic or laboratory method used to evaluate a clinical question; interpretation depends on the specimen or body site, method, reference range, and patient context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multinucleated hepatocyte} A multinucleated hepatocyte is a liver cell containing two or more nuclei, usually from nuclear division without complete cytokinesis. It may be a normal or reactive finding, but its significance depends on the number, morphology, and associated liver abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multiple exostoses} Multiple exostoses, or hereditary multiple osteochondromas, is an inherited disorder causing multiple cartilage-capped bony projections near growth plates. Lesions can deform bones, restrict motion, compress nerves or vessels, and rarely undergo malignant transformation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multiple fibrofolliculomas} Multiple fibrofolliculomas are benign hair-follicle tumors appearing as numerous small skin-colored papules, especially on the face and trunk. They are characteristic of Birt--Hogg--Dub\'e syndrome, which also carries risks of renal tumors and spontaneous pneumothorax.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multiple organ failure} Multiple organ failure, also called multiple organ dysfunction syndrome, is progressive dysfunction of two or more organ systems, usually during severe critical illness. Causes include sepsis, shock, trauma, and major inflammation; treatment is supportive and directed at the underlying cause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multiplicity} Multiplicity is the state of having more than one occurrence, copy, lesion, tumor, or pregnancy component. Its interpretation depends on context, such as multiple gestation, multifocal disease, or copy number in genetics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multipolar neuron} A multipolar neuron is a nerve cell with one axon and multiple dendrites. It is the most common neuronal form in the central nervous system and includes many motor neurons and interneurons.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{multivesicular body} A multivesicular body is an endosomal compartment containing many small intraluminal vesicles. It can fuse with the plasma membrane to release exosomes or with a lysosome to degrade its cargo.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muparfostat} Muparfostat (PI-88) is a mixture of highly sulfated, phosphorylated mannose oligosaccharides investigated as a heparanase inhibitor and antiangiogenic agent. It has been studied experimentally in cancer and is not a routine approved therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mupirocin} Mupirocin is a topical antibiotic that inhibits bacterial isoleucyl-tRNA synthetase. It is used for selected skin infections and nasal eradication of certain staphylococci; resistance can develop with inappropriate or prolonged use.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muramidase} Muramidase, or lysozyme, is an enzyme that cleaves the peptidoglycan backbone of bacterial cell walls. It is present in secretions and innate immune cells and contributes to defense against susceptible bacteria.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muromonab-CD3} Muromonab-CD3 is a murine monoclonal antibody directed against the CD3 complex on T cells. It was formerly used to treat acute organ-transplant rejection but is largely obsolete because of cytokine-release and other immune-mediated adverse effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscarine} Muscarine is a mushroom-derived agonist of muscarinic acetylcholine receptors. Ingestion of muscarine-containing mushrooms can cause a cholinergic syndrome with salivation, sweating, tearing, abdominal cramps, vomiting, diarrhea, bronchospasm, and bradycardia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscarinic antagonist} A muscarinic antagonist blocks acetylcholine at muscarinic receptors in smooth muscle, glands, the heart, and the central nervous system. Examples include atropine, ipratropium, and oxybutynin; effects can include dry mouth, blurred vision, urinary retention, tachycardia, and confusion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Muscidae} Muscidae is a family of flies that includes the housefly. Some species can mechanically carry pathogens to food or wounds, and their larvae may occasionally cause myiasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscimol} Muscimol is a psychoactive compound found in some Amanita mushrooms and an agonist at GABA-A receptors. Ingestion can cause altered perception, sedation, agitation, incoordination, or seizures and may require toxicologic evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle cell} muscle cell is an anatomical or histologic term describing a body structure, tissue, or region; its clinical meaning depends on its location, function, and associated findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle cramp} A muscle cramp is a sudden, involuntary, painful contraction of a muscle. It may follow exertion, dehydration, electrolyte disturbance, pregnancy, medication use, nerve disease, or vascular disease; recurrent cramps need assessment of the cause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle development} Muscle development is the formation, growth, differentiation, and maturation of muscle tissue. It depends on genetic programs, neural input, hormones, nutrition, mechanical loading, and repair processes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle fiber} A muscle fiber is an individual muscle cell containing contractile myofibrils. Skeletal muscle fibers vary in size and metabolic properties, while cardiac and smooth muscle cells have distinct structures and functions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle hypertrophy} Muscle hypertrophy is enlargement of individual muscle fibers, usually in response to resistance, hormonal stimulation, or compensatory workload. It can be physiologic or associated with disease, depending on the muscle and cause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle neoplasm} muscle neoplasm describes a neoplastic process involving the tissue or organ named by the term. Diagnosis establishes the tumor type, extent, biologic behavior, and appropriate management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle protein} muscle protein is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle relaxant} A muscle relaxant is a medicine that reduces skeletal-muscle spasm or contraction. Centrally acting agents reduce muscle spasm through the nervous system, whereas neuromuscular blockers interrupt transmission at the neuromuscular junction and require respiratory support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle rigidity} Muscle rigidity is abnormal resistance to passive movement caused by sustained muscle contraction or altered tone. It occurs in conditions such as Parkinson disease, dystonia, meningitis, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, and tetanus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle spindle} A muscle spindle is a sensory receptor within skeletal muscle that detects muscle length and the rate of length change. Its afferent signals contribute to the stretch reflex, muscle tone, posture, and coordinated movement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle strength} Muscle strength is the maximal force a muscle or muscle group can generate under specified conditions. It is assessed by functional or resisted testing and may be reduced by muscle, nerve, neuromuscular-junction, central, or systemic disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{muscle tonus} Muscle tonus, or muscle tone, is the continuous, low-level activity and resistance to passive stretch present in resting muscle. It depends on spinal and supraspinal reflexes and may be abnormally increased or decreased in neurologic disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{musculoskeletal deformity} A musculoskeletal deformity is an abnormal shape, alignment, or structure of bone, joint, muscle, or related tissue. It may be congenital, developmental, traumatic, degenerative, neuromuscular, or caused by disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{musculoskeletal disease} musculoskeletal disease describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{musculoskeletal injury} musculoskeletal injury describes a clinical disorder or finding in the body system or process named by the term. Evaluation identifies the underlying mechanism, affected site, severity, and clinically relevant complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{musculoskeletal system} The musculoskeletal system consists of bones, joints, cartilage, ligaments, tendons, muscles, and associated connective tissues. It provides support, protects organs, enables movement, and contributes to mineral storage and blood-cell production.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mustard compounds} Mustard compounds are chemical agents containing reactive sulfur or nitrogen mustard groups that can alkylate DNA and other molecules. Some are chemotherapy drugs; others are highly toxic chemical-warfare agents.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mustard gas} Mustard gas, or sulfur mustard, is a vesicant chemical-warfare agent that damages skin, eyes, and respiratory tissues and can cause blistering, airway injury, and delayed complications. It is not a true gas at ordinary temperatures and has no specific widely available antidote.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mutagenicity test} mutagenicity test is a diagnostic or laboratory method used to evaluate a clinical question; interpretation depends on the specimen or body site, method, reference range, and patient context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mutant} A mutant is a cell, organism, or genetic sequence carrying a mutation relative to a reference or parental form. The change may have no effect, alter a trait, or cause disease, depending on its location and functional consequence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mutant proteins} mutant proteins is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mutate} To mutate means to undergo a change in genetic sequence. Mutations arise spontaneously or from replication errors, DNA damage, or mutagen exposure and may be inherited, acquired in somatic cells, harmful, neutral, or beneficial.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mutation abnormality} A mutation abnormality is an atypical change in DNA sequence or chromosome structure identified relative to a reference. Its clinical significance must be classified using evidence, such as pathogenic, likely pathogenic, uncertain significance, likely benign, or benign.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mutism} Mutism is absence or near-absence of speech despite the physical ability to produce sound. It may occur with neurologic injury, psychosis, catatonia, severe anxiety, selective mutism, developmental conditions, or other illnesses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mycelium} A mycelium is the network of branching hyphae that forms the vegetative body of a fungus. It grows through soil, tissue, or other substrates and produces or supports formation of reproductive structures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mycetoma} Mycetoma is a chronic localized infection of skin and subcutaneous tissue, usually of the foot, caused by true fungi (eumycetoma) or filamentous bacteria (actinomycetoma). It typically produces swelling, draining sinuses, and grains and may invade deeper tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mycobacteriaceae} mycobacteriaceae is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mycobacterium} mycobacterium is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mycobacterium avium} mycobacterium avium is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\medterm{mycobacterium bovis} mycobacterium bovis is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\medterm{mycobacterium chelonae} mycobacterium chelonae is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mycobacterium flavescens} mycobacterium flavescens is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mycobacterium fortuitum} mycobacterium fortuitum is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mycobacterium gordonae} mycobacterium gordonae is a microorganism or microbial group identified by its biologic and taxonomic features. Clinical significance depends on pathogenicity, exposure, the site involved, and antimicrobial susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{mydriatics} Mydriatics are medicines that dilate the pupil, usually by stimulating the iris dilator muscle or blocking muscarinic receptors in the iris sphincter. They are used for eye examination and selected procedures and can precipitate angle-closure glaucoma in susceptible eyes.

\medterm{myelination} Myelination is formation of a myelin sheath around an axon by oligodendrocytes in the central nervous system or Schwann cells in the peripheral nervous system. It increases the speed and efficiency of nerve conduction.

\medterm{myelitis} Myelitis is inflammation of the spinal cord or, less commonly, other neural tissue described by the term. \medterm{myeloablative agonist} A myeloablative agent, likely intended by this term, is a treatment that severely or completely destroys bone-marrow blood-forming cells. It is used before selected stem-cell transplants and requires stem-cell rescue because of profound, prolonged cytopenias.

\medterm{Malaria Facts} Malaria is a mosquito-borne infection caused by Plasmodium parasites and can produce fever, chills, anemia, confusion, kidney injury, or death. Travel history and testing are crucial because prompt species-specific antimalarial treatment is lifesaving.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mania vs Hypomania} Mania is a sustained elevated, expansive, or irritable mood with increased energy and marked impairment, possible psychosis, or need for hospitalization. Hypomania is milder, lasts at least several days, and causes observable change without severe functional impairment or psychosis.

\medterm{Menstrual Cramps and PMS Medication Guide} Menstrual cramps arise from uterine prostaglandins, while premenstrual syndrome causes cyclical physical and emotional symptoms before menstruation. Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, hormonal methods, lifestyle measures, or other treatments are selected according to symptoms and contraindications.

\medterm{Mental Health (Psychology)} Alternate index label for Psychology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mental Illness} Mental illness includes diagnosable disorders affecting mood, thought, behavior, perception, or functioning, such as depression, anxiety, psychosis, and substance-use disorders. Causes are multifactorial, and effective care may combine psychotherapy, medicines, social support, and safety planning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Mental Illness in Children} Mental illness in children may appear as persistent changes in mood, behavior, learning, sleep, appetite, relationships, or development. Evaluation considers age, medical causes, trauma, school context, family history, safety, and the child's strengths and supports.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{MERS (Middle East Respiratory Syndrome)} Alternate index label for Middle East Respiratory Syndrome.
